ITAR-TASS Carries Press Review for 28 Apr

LD2804065995 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 0556
GMT 28 Apr 95

(“Press Review”—ITAR-TASS headline]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow April 28 (ITAR-
TASS)—IZVESTIYA frontpages an article about the
situation in Chechnya. The main topic of the article
deals with the state of affairs in the information space of
the republic. The new republican newspaper Vozrozh-
deniye has not been published for two weeks while the
Dudayev-published Ichkeria is being circulated
throughout the republic. The so-called Dudayev Televi-
sion is on the air as well. Incredible rumours are rife in
the republic that on May 9 Djokhar Dudayev will ride
into Groznyy on a “white horse”’.

ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA runs an article headlined “A
bandit in red- striped trousers” about the activities of a
racketeering boss who operated posing as a Cossack
chieftain. He even managed to register a certain society
named “Cossack Fraternity” in the town of Dmitrov
near Moscow. After he was arrested by police for illegal
actions, advocates in very high places stood up for his
defence.

ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI frontpages an interview with Rus-
sia’s State Duma Speaker Ivan Rybkin, who dwells on
matters connected with the adoption of the first part of a
Civil Code, the work on a Land Code and other documents
which lay the foundations of Russian legislation.

Rybkin also shared his conception of the economic
situation in the country and ways to improve it, the
problems of the formation of the country’s budget and a
future Federative set-up of Russia.

INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS

ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI runs an interview with Grigoriy
Yavlinskiy, leader of the socio-political movement Yab-
loko, who dwells on the possibility of uniting all Demo-
cratic forces at the forthcoming elections in Russia and
his vision of a Democratic platform.

Yavlinskiy stated that the Yabloko movement is pre-
pared to enter into a coalition only with truly democratic
forces which agree with the Movement’s views, are
committed to democratic principles and repudiate the
forms and methods of the present authorities’ actions.

ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA, under the rubric “Social
Protection”, runs an article about a new Civil Code of
the Russian Federation.

KRASNAYA ZVEZDA (Red Star) frontpages an article
about benefits for the veterans. “Over the past several
months, as if making up for the lost time, Federal and
local authorities seek to do something for the war veter-
ans,” the newspaper writes.

TRUD, under the rubric “Moscow and the Muscovites”,
runs a column of materials about the problems of the
capital of Russia. The author of one of the items complains
that Moscow loses its historical architectural image.

PRAVDA runs an interview with Marshal of the Soviet
Union Viktor Kulikov, who will review the Victory
Anniversary Parade on May 9.

The Marshal shares his point of view that a parade
marking the 50th anniversary of the Victory in the Great
Patriotic War should take place in the Red Square, not
near the Victory memorial complex on Poklonnaya Hill.
The Marshal also expresses his opinion about the
present-day state of affairs in the Russian army.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

Kozyrev Interviewed on Washington Meetings

LD2804 103695 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 2205 GMT 27 Apr 95

[By ITAR-TASS correspondents Viktor Khrekov and
Vitaliy Chukseyev]

[FBIS Translated Text] Washington, 28 Apr—The tele-
phone conversation which took place on Thursday
between the Presidents of the United States and Russia
and my meeting with Bill Clinton analyzed Russian-
American relations in depth on the eve of the Moscow
summit, Russian Foreign Minister Andrey Kozyrev told
the ITAR-TASS correspondents. These conversations
were businesslike and constructive, and they demon-
strated a mood for resolving present and future difficult
questions.

The accent was placed on a most important aspect: over
an unprecedentedly short time, relations between the
two countries have gone from a state of confrontation to
partnership in the sphere of disarmament and in the
political and economic spheres, all in the space of three
years. There are difficulties, he stressed; however, the
partnership has been justified and has entered the stage
of maturity.

The question of Russia supplying nuclear reactors to
Iran is ore of the spheres of disagreement. The minister
stated in this connection that we can expect that a good,
frank talk will take place at the Moscow summit, a talk
aimed at seeking constructive solutions which take
account of the interests of both sides.

The IT. AR-TASS correspondents asked him to comment
upon the statements by a number of Western political
figures that the creation in the North Caucasus region of
yet another army runs counter to the spirit and letter of
the treaty on conventional arms in Europe. I would like
our partners not to come forward hastily with statements
without having studied our position beforehand, the
minister replied. We discussed that problem in detail at
the talks with Secretary of State Christopher Warren on
Wednesday [26 April]. We have introduced a number of
proposals to the treaty, without destroying the substance
of this agreement. This document on the whole meets
with our support, but it has to be combined with specific
actions.

Kozyrev concentrated on the forthcoming meeting of
leaders of the major countries of the world in Halifax
(Canada), noting that the “eight” had already been
formulated as a political institution. There are no polit-
ical issues in the world which can be discussed without
our participation. As far as participation in the economic
round of the meeting is concerned, we are already
discussing a number of such problems, such as debts, the
investment climate and so on. But there are also ques-
tions which we are not required to tackle since they
entail financial obligations. The present format of par-
ticipation in such meetings is very advantageous for
Russia.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

At this stage I am satisfied with the talks which have
taken place, the minister said. They have shown that the
prophecies of those who predicted the inevitability of a
“cold peace” and that we will start to stumble and turn
off the road of partnership have been dashed. Nothing is
perfect, the skies aren't always clear, but neither is their
any turning away from cooperation. On the contrary,
partnership is being strengthened and becoming more
mature.

Regarding the forthcoming celebrations in Moscow on
the occasion of the 50th anniversary of the victory over
German fascism, Kozyrev stressed that we should use it
to pay tribute to the contribution which the Russian
people made to this victory, together with the allies. This
is a good time, he said, to reflect on how to build
cooperation and partnership, now and in the future. The
leaders of the overwhelming majority of countries have
accepted the invitation to visit Moscow. However, the
minister noted, there are certain individuals, for
example, in the Baltic countries, who can’t see the wood
for the trees.

Kozyrev Expects Early U.S. Response on Treaty

LD2804114995 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1025
GMT 28 Apr 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] WASHINGTON, D.C., April
28 (Interfax) -Russian Foreign Minister Andrey Kozyrev
believes that even before the Russian-American summit
of May 9 and 10 Russia will know the U.S. response to its
concerns over the conventional arms in Europe limita-
tion treaty. He said so in a talk with Russian journalists
who accompanied him to Washington.

We made specific proposals on the way Moscow’s needs
could be recognized without destroying the treaty which
Russia essentially backs, Kozyrev said in commenting on
his meeting with U.S. secretary of State Warren Chris-
topher.

Russia wants the restrictions revised on forces stationed
on its flanks, notably in the North Caucasian Military
District. In Kozyrev’s words, western rejection of the
Russian proposals “will not cure the disease but will only
put off the treatment.”

Asked by Interfax, Kozyrev said that Presidents Boris
Yeltsin and Bill Clinton discussed upgrading the bilat-
eral cooperation in their telephone talk Thursday.

This issue, Kozyrev said, was also discussed in his talk
with Clinton in the White House Thursday. “‘The press
emphasizes complexities in our relations so as to add
drama to their reports but we focus on more funda-
mental problems,” he explained. In his words, the Rus-
sian-American partnership has brought about tangible
results, has been a success and matures.

“A good frank talk can be expected in the Moscow
summit on issues where the Russian and U.S. positions
do not completely fit.” Kozyrev said. “Russia and the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

U.S. will look for solutions rather than be engaged in
exchange of reproaches and accusations,” he continued.
Kozyrev did not rule out the possibility that a solution
will be worked out to the differences over supply of
Russian light water nuclear reactors to Iran.

He emphasized that by accepting the invitation to come
to Moscow Clinton made a wise decision which is highly
appreciated by the Russian leadership. Kozyrev went on
to say that the U.S. and French presidents and leaders of
other countries who agreed to come to Moscow under-
stood the basic fact that we had been allies in the war and
are partners now while the presidents of Latvia, Estonia
and Lithuania overlooked the historic perspective and
made “not the shrewdest possible move.”

Kozyrev ‘Scaremongering’ on NATO Expansion
Deplored
MM2704150095 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 28
Apr 95 p 3

[Report by Melor Sturua: “Kozyrev Entreats Americans
To Save Him From the GULAG”]

[FBiS Translated Text] Minneapolis, United States—
Andrey Kozyrev has addressed a U.S.-Japanese-
European forum. The written text which he delivered
was absolutely precise and clear, although debatable.
Kozyrev said that the expansion of NATO via the
inclusion of some East European countries creates a
threat to Russia’s security and national interests. He had
weighty grounds, although once again rather debatable
ones, for declaring that this expansion of NATO may
lead to the nightmare of a new confrontation.

As we can see, Andrey Kozyrev said nothing new. For a
while now the aim of Russian foreign policy has been to
prevent the former Warsaw Pact countries from entering
NATO by all diplomatic means. But, my God, the means
our foreign minister used! Departing from his prepared
text, he entreated NATO not to throw open the door to
East Europe in order to avoid adding grist to the mill of
Russian nationalism, which, you see, may devour even
him, so he would have to write his memoirs “from the
GULAG.” To paraphrase Napoleon, it could be said that
this is worse than a mistake, it is not funny. But Kozyrev
did not feel this. The nationalists, he said, are using the
expansion of NATO as evidence of the failures of the
Russian Government, including its foreign minister.
Indicating his head, Kozyrev added: “The stones are
already landing here.”

Does Kozyrev really think ‘or hope that with such lam-
entations he can move his NATO colleagues and soften
their hardened hearts? Clearly diplomacy is too serious a
business to be left to diplomats. This is not the first time
our minister's wit has made his audience suspect his
arguments are lightweight.

But there is another fundamentally important aspect to
this topic. Literally the other day, explaining in the

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 3

United Nations Russia’s position regarding Iraqi dic-
tator Saddam Husayn and Serbian President Slobodan
Milosevic, Kozyrev remarked: “‘Never demonize people
in politics. There are no demons in politics.” An
extremely fair comment, provided, of course, you do not
take it to absurd lengths. But unfortunately Kozyrev
himself is not following his own advice and is demon-
izing Russian nationalism.

In short, he is scaremongering, but people in the West are
not afraid. And they are not laughing either. THE NEW
YORK TIMES even described Kozyrev’s departures
from his prepared text as “pitiful.”

Further on Gore-Chernomyrdin Commission
Preparations

LD2704190095 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1624
GMT 27 Apr 95

[From the “Diplomatic Panorama” feature: by diplo-
matic correspondents Aleksandr Korzun, Igor Porshnev,
Yevgeniy Terekhov, and others}

([FBIS Transcribed Text) WASHINGTON, April 27
(INTERFAX)—At the fifth meeting of the Russian-
American Commission for Economic and Technological
Cooperation (Gore-Chernomyrdin commission) Russia
intends to point io continuing discrimination against
Russian goods on the American market, Mikhail Tara-
sov, international affairs adviser to Russia’s prime Min-
ister, told Interfax in Washington on Thursday. He is in
the American capital in connection with preparations for
the session.

“The Americans always protect the interests of their
firms and companies, and when a delay occurs, they raise
that issue at the Gore-Chernomyrdin commission
without immediately,” Tarasov said. “But if Russia
behaves in the same way, the issue gets resolved very
slowly as a rule.”

As an example he referred to the situation connected
with the export of Russian hunting weapons produced in
Tula and Izhevsk. “The five-year program for delivering
these weapons is to yield about one billion dollars, but
the U.S., minding the interests of its own producers,
introduced additional restrictions and established tough
barriers for arms imports,” Tarasov said.

“Russia was lumped with Mongolia and China, whose
arms exports are banned because of their low quality,”
Tarasov noted.

In the meantime, he added, “this does not prevent the
Americans from displaying heightened interest in certain
anti-aircraft installations of Russian-make, particularly
in the S-300 complex Belarus already sold to the U.S.
“The Americans intend to build similar complexes with
a view to selling them to Saudi Arabia, the United Arab
Emirates and other countries; this makes one wonder,
however, as to how to correlate the striving to purchase

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

anti- aircraft installations with the ban on the purchases
of ordinary weapons of Russian make,“ Tarasov pointed
out.

According to «im, Russia has raised this issue at the
Gore-Chernomyrdin commission more than once, and
pointed to the need to conduct trade on parity condi-
tions. “Our principle is the following: we shall open our
markets, and you will open yours. We do not want your
aid: just give us access to your market,” Tarasov said.

Chubays Speaks at U.S. National Press Club

LD2704194195 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1857
GMT 27 Apr 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] WASHINGTON, April 27
(INTERFAX)}—Russian First Deputy Prime Minister
Anatoliy Chubays said he believed that in April 1995
“Russia is as close to irreversible financial-economic
stabilization as never before.”

He told a news conference in the U.S. National Press-
Club in Washington today that the inflation rate in
Russia had decreased by 2.5 times over the last four
months and a half. Chubays said he was sure that the
monthly inflation rate would be 2 percent or even less by
the middle of the year.

Chubays said that in particular the financial-economic
stabilization manifested itself in the ruble’s fall against
the dollar slowing down. He said Russian residents
having U.S. dollars had begun exchanging them for
rubles “in mass.”” Chubays said this was a very signifi-
cant sign.

Chubays said the reserves of the Central Bank of Russia
were “constantly growing.” He added that “all these
processes are completely controlled by the government
and personally Russian President Boris Yeltsin.”

When asked about the impact of events in Chechnya on
the economic situation in Russia, Chubays said, “These
events will by no means stimulate the inflation.” He said
all expenses would be carried out within the scope of the
already approved budget expenditure. Chubays said that
expenses on any of the budget items had not yet outgone
the fixed budget expenditure.

Chubays regretted that the West failed to understand the
dynamics of developments in Chechnya. He said about
250,000 people had already returned to Groznyy. Chu-
bays said that water, gas and electric power supply would
soon be fully restored in Groznyy. He said physicians
and teachers had begun receiving salaries. Chubays con-
cluded that “all this is certainly very promising”.

START II Hearings: ‘Paradoxical Situation’ Seen

MM2704155595 Moscow KRASNAYA ZVEZDA in
Russian 27 Apr 95 p 3

[Report by Gennadiy Obolenskiy: “Pentagon May Stake
Its All’’]

[FBIS Translated Text] The discussion of questions
connected with the ratification of the Treaty on Strategic
Offensive Arms II [START II] is continuing in U.S.
Congressional committees. In this connection, it would
not be out of place to recall that the limitations and
reductions of strategic offensive weapons envisaged in it,
partially already implemented, have only became pos-
sible under conditions of the preservation of the 1972
ABM Treaty of unlimited duration.

This reminder is appropriate in connection with the
paradoxical nature of the situation that has taken shape
during the hearings. On the one hand, representatives of
the Pentagon and the administration as a whole are
expressing a Clear desire for a real limitation of strategic

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

offensive weapons (of course, primarily Russian ones).
And on the other hand they want to evade observing the
basic provisions of the ABM Treaty through agreeing
with Russia the kind of parameters of so-called non-
strategic anti-missile defense (or theater ABM) which
would make this system entirely capable of settling
strategic tasks too.

The idea of conducting talks on demarcating strategic
and non- strategic ABM defense and agreeing on the
specifications of the latter in the form of a separate
accord was proposed to us by the Americans. Even the
specific time schedules for conducting them were out-
lined. Reports have appeared to the effect that withing
the Pentagon’s apparatus the acceleated preparation of a
draft of such an agreement has begun. But the Americans
themselves unexpectedly refused to continue the talks.
Why?

Undoubtedly the emergence of a republican majority in
the U.S. Congress plays a fairly major role here. The
Congressmen have obviously decided not to be hasty as
regards expanding cooperation with Russia and will try
to wring new concessions from it. And in this connec-
tion, [they have decided] not to be in any hurry with
getting up the ABM accord proposed by Washington
shortly beforehand.

E:ut there is also another side to this matter. The Amer-
icans’ proposals on ABM defense have proved to be in
direct concontradiction to the limitations on strategic
offensive arms envisaged by the START-II Treaty, and
may hinder its ratification. And after all, it is extremely
advantageous for the United States, and Washington is
very interested in its implementation. That it why it
should be expected that following the conclusion of the
ratification process, the Americans proceed to additional
steps to “push through” ideas in the sphere of anti-
missile defense that will in fact lead to the collapse of the
ABM Treaty.

Discussions can also be heard among independent
American experts to the effect that once it has achieved
significant reductions of Russian strategic offensive
weapons, the Pentagon will stake its all, and, using its
own homespun interpretations of the provisions of the
ABM Treaty, will de facto stop taking it into account.
Particularly since in the Pentagon’s understanding, the
ABM Treaty will not restrict the theater ABM. Admit-
tedly, at the same time, the fact that this is a question of
mobile ground-, sea-, and air- based ABM systems,
which are banned by this treaty, is being deliberately
kept quiet.

And I would like to stress the following here. Until the
sides agree where the dictinction between authorized
and banned activity lies in respect of such ABM systems,
there are no grounds for stating unilaterally that the
creation of a particular ABM theater of military opera-
tions system corresponds to the treaty and does not
undermine it. Otherwise, the entire process of arms
control might as well be scrapped.

Ministry ‘Alarmed’ by U.S. Testing of ABM
Systems

LD2704161595 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1421
GMT 27 Apr 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, April 27 (Inter-
fax)}—The Russian Foreign Ministry is alarmed by the
fact that this year the United States has started testing
new anti-missile defense systems which it has unilater-
ally described as non-strategic or theater anti-missile
systems. The feeling was expressed at a Thursday
briefing in Moscow by a ministry spokesman Nikita
Matkovskiy.

He drew attention to the fac: that the American side
launched the tests (the latest wa: conducted on April 21)
before the approval of an agreement distinguishing
between strategic and non-strategic anti-ballistic missile
[ABM] systems.

The diplomat said that since autumn 1993 the United
States, Russia and CIS countries-successors of the USSR
as far as the treaty is concerned have been conducting
talks to prevent the possible violation or circumvention
of the ABM treaty in the process of developing anti-
missile systems.

According to Matkovskiy, as long as the talks have not
been completed the Russian side cannot recognize the
systems tested in the United States as conforming with
the ABM treaty.

“The tests can be considered as the attempt of the
American side to make its partners in the talks face a fait
accompli. In our opinion the continuation of the policy
of accomplished facts instead of an intensive search for a
mutually acceptable solution can only complicate mat-
ters, if not drive them into a blind alley,” the spokesman
said.

U.S. Astronauts Prepare for Mir Mission

MM 2804091095 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 26
Apr 95 p 8

[Report by Viktor Gritsenko and Veronika Romanenko
of ITAR-TASS: “U.S. Astronaut Prepares for Flight on
Mir, When She Might Break Yelena Kondakova’s
Record”]

[FBIS Translated Text] Shannon Lucid, the first U.S.
woman astronaut, who is to engage in lengthy work on
board the Russian Mir station in 1996, and John Blaha,
her backup, have been training for exactly three months
at the Cosmonaut Training Center of the Russian Air
Force (Zvezdnyy Gorodok).

The 145-day flight planned within the “Mir-NASA”
program will be carried out by two Russian cosmonauts
and one American. It will be the second joint expedition,
as the first one has been taking place since 14 March
1995.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

For the first time the crew members will be put into orbit
not by one craft but by different ones. The Russians
Yuriy Onuirtyenko and Yuriy Usachev are expected to
be launcned in the Soyuz TM-24 craft next December.
Only in March of next year will Shannon Lucid join
them on board the American Atlantis. On the other
hand, Onufriyenko and Usachev will quit the Mir station
almost one month before the American—in June 1996.
Shannon Lucid will return to earth by Shuttle 31 July. So
she will spend her last days in orbit working with a
different crew, whose members include another
woman—Kic + Andre-Dee [name as transliterated], a
French woman.

Shannon Lucid, 52 years old, and John Blaha (his
surname is stressed on the last syllable), 53, have already
participated in four Shuttle flights. Lucid made her last
two flights on Atlantis (August 1991) and on Columbia
(October 1993) together with John Blaha, who was her
commander. Shannon Lucid still holds the record for the
total duration of flights for U.S. women astronauts—34
days, 22 hours, and 52 minvtes. If the Russian-U.S.
flight is concluded successfully, she will overtake Yelena
Kondakova, who has “logged” 169 days, five hours, and
22 minutes.

Despite the fact that the astronauts already spoke good
Russian at the time they arrived in Russia (they studied
Russian for three months in the United States), in
January thcy attended an intensive course in Zvezdnyy
Gorodok and in March embarked on technical training.
Since the astronauts will be delivered to Mir and
returned to earth by Shuttle, they will study the Soyuz
TM craft only to the extent necessary to operate it as part
of the orbital complex, as well as on landing in the event
of an emergency departure from the station or the
Shuttle’s failure to dock with the complex. On the other
hand, the systems of the complex and, in particular, the
scientific equipment of the new Spektr module, whose
launch is planned for 18 May, will be studied thoroughly.

In April the astronauts underwent training for weight-
lessness in the Il-76MDK flying laboratory, and in May
they will rehearse crew operations by landing the descent
vehicle of the Soyuz craft on the water of the Medvezhi
Lakes not far from Zvezdnyy Gorodok. In September the
astronauts will continue training as members of crews
along with the Russian cosmonauts Yuriy Onufriyenko
and Yuriy Usachev (main crewmen) and Vasiliy Tsib-
liyev and Aleksandr Lazutkin (backups).

In January 1996 the astronauts have to undergo survival
training, which could come in handy in the case of an
emergency landing by Soyuz. Lucid and Blaha will be left
in a corner of the forest near Moscow unknown to them
for several hours or days, depending on the weather. In
this way the astronauts will rehearse their actions until
the arrival of rescuers.

Doubts Cast on NASA Contract

MK2804112095 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian 28
Apr 95 p 2

[Report by Mikhail Chernyshov under the “Commerce”
rubric: “Russian Space Agency Hopes To Make a Fast
Buck. NASA Is Rumored To Be Ready To Demonstrate
Unprecedented Generosity”]

{[FBIS Translated Text] The ITAR-TASS news agency
reported 26 April, citing “‘a source close to the space
industry leadership,” that NASA is going to pay Russia
$334 million for five flights of U.S. astronauts to the
“Mir” orbital station in 1995-1997.

Foreign customers paid much more modest fees to
Russia for all previous commercial launches—up to $10
miilion plus per launch at best. Foreign researchers from
West Europe or, for instance, a crown prince from Saudi
Arabia paid fairly generously to the Americans, but these
sums were still smaller than those NASA now allegedly
intends to put up.

Chiefs of Russian space agencies have become used to
complaining that currency proceeds from commercial
flights do not recoup their actual costs. If the ITAR-
TASS report is true, this means that the Americans are
finally ready to pay a decent price for “a prolonged
zero-gravity state,” something they are interested in
above all. It cannot be ruled out either that the “flight”
section of the project also includes a provision for work
involving “Alfa.”

The sensational report elicits distrust, however, at least
because there is no single space industry in Russia: There
are the Russian Space Agency (RSA), the Military Space
Forces (MSF), the Strategic Missile Forces (SMF), and
some other structures anxious to achieve space orbits.
Who has been promised the money and for what services
in particular? Notably, the leadership of the Russian
Space Agency, which traditionally maintains ties with
the Americans on a “priority” basis, could not confirm
or deny the ITAR-TASS information.

Commentary on Outcome of U.S.-DPRK Tolks

SK2804035495 Moscow Voice of Russia World Service
in Korean 1200 GMT 27 Apr 95

[Commentary by Aleksey Kondratyev from the “Today’s
World” program]

[FBIS Translated Text] According to a report from
[words indistinct], the United States has proposed to the
DPRK to hold higher-level U.S.-DPRK negotiations on
the issue of supplying light-water reactors. However, the
DPRK has yet to respond to this proposal.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

In this connection, station commentator Aleksey Kon-
dratyev reports as follows:

Quoting the letter of Kang Sok-chu, first vice minister of
the DPRK Ministry of Foreign Affairs, to the U.S.
Department of State, a Tokyo source reported that
Pyongyang has not yet sent a definite reply to the U.S.
proposal. Meanwhile, an ROK source reported that
Pyongyang had agreed in principle to the U.S. proposal
to continue the talks.

The surfacing of these conflicting news reports are an
indication of the difficulty with which the DPRK-U.S.
dialogue have proceeded. The third round of negotia-
tions began in Berlin on 25 March and ended on 20 April
after numerous periods of recess. According to the
DPRK-U:S. agreed framework signed last year, a final
settlement of the issue of supplying light-water reactors
had to be made by 21 April. However, the Berlin
negotiations failed to bring about any results. The nego-
tiations ended without reaching any agreement as the
United States imposed [kangyo] ROK-model light-water
reactors and Pyongyang resolutely opposed them.

Now, what is the way out of such a stalemate?

The United States has proposed holding higher-level
talks. The United States has also said that it is ready even
io advance a compromise proposal regarding the issue of
supplying light-water reactors. But, the United States has
not revealed the details of the said compromise proposal.

According to the remarks of the DPRK ambassador to
Moscow, Pyongyang believes that it should receive at
least one Russian-model light-water reactor. The DPRK
ambassador noted that an agreement was made between
the former Soviet Union and the DPRK as early as in
1985, regarding the construction of a light-water reactor-
equipped atomic power plant. Moscow has declared
many times that it is ready to supply light-water reactors
to the DPRK.

Washington should have accepted Pyongyang’s opinion
during the negotiations. Only by so doing would it have
found a way out of this stalemate.

This problem, however, has yet to find a means for a
solution.

In the meantime, the DPRK repeatedly declares that it
will not tolerate the pressure by foreign forces against it.

Yeltsin Aide Disputes ‘Factions’ Forecast

MM2804082195 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 28
Apr 95 p 4

{Article by Yuriy Baturin, national security adviser to
the Russian Federation president, and Oleg Dobro-
cheyev, staffer at the Kurchatov Institute Russian Scien-
tific Center: “Russia Is Not Quite So Fatally Doomed to
Stability. Among Other Things, the American Crisis
Could Hamper It”}

[FBIS Translated Text] The forecast of our country’s
political development according to the CIA’s “Factions”
methodology that was published in IZVESTIYA recently
(5 April 1995, No. 62) is, for a number of reasons,
worthy of more attention than the public has paid it,
even despite a headline that was unusually optimistic for
nowadays—‘Russia Doomed to Stability.”

The forecast is interesting, first, as a document that
could be a “substantial factor influencing the elaboration
of the foreign policy course” of the United States toward
our country (as the author of the above-mentioned
article assures us).

Second, as information aimed at convincing us that these
are indeed the conclusions that will form the basis of the
formation of U.S. policy toward Russia (a sort of
reflexive manipulation: “Let them think that we
think...””). IZVESTIYA’s interim conclusion is character-
istic: “* There are substantial grounds to suggest that the
assessments made by us with our U.S. partners will be
borne in mind during preparations for B. Clinton’s May
visit to Moscow.”

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

Third, it is, to some extent, an outsider’s view of Russia’s
development prospects.

But how valid, from the scientific viewpoint, is the
proposed method of modeling political processes? How
far can the forecast that is presented be relied on?

Bright Forecasts

1 <t us begin with the scientific aspect of the problem.
Even from indirect signs contained in the newspaper
item, it is possible to assess the methodological merits of
“Factions.”

The first important aspect of this modei lies in its
subtext, whereby it is deemed possible in principie to
give an adequate formal expression to the dynamics of
political processes. Our own research confirms this view
on the basis of a series of models of sociopolitical
phenomena (see, for instance, STOLITSA, 1994, No. 3)
which for a long time had been examined both in our
country and abroad in a purely qualitative plane.

Something else is more important.

Society can be compared figuratively to white light,
which is actually a “mixture” of all the colors of the
rainbow. It can be broken up into a spectrum—a broad
range of shades of different properties or conditions of
the country’s political, economic, and social system.

The “Factions” methodology is notable for just such a
method of presentation, in which the parameters of the
condition of society that reflect the political structure
and the sociopolitical system are broken up into a
spectrum by a kind of “prism”—by the experts’ intellect.

This approach is certainly strictly scientific. The main
practical problem with this way of describing social
processes is the breadth of the spectrum necessary and
sufficient to build reliable forecasts. The fact that in the
American model the spectrum of the main political
forces is constructed on the basis of 100 points testifies
to the date of its formulation—the early eighties. Since
then, many new ideas have emerged, making it possible
substantially to improve models of sociopolitical pro-
cesses.

It is quite clear that the Americans are hardly likely to
show off the latest developments, still less to publish
them in the foreign press (in fact, we ourselves act in the
same way). That being so, the above hypothesis on a
gentle reflexive manipulation becomes by no means
unfounded. We must not lose sight of the possibility of
our being pushed toward a certain choice, orientation, or
suggestion that is made not “point-blank,” so to speak,
but fairly transparently.

But there is no danger in that provided the nature of the
game is understood.

But let us give due credit to the merits of “Factions.”
First and foremost, for the measuring of processes in
three perspectives: those of the leading economic sectors,

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

en i ee a ee Pe

10 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

regions, and politicians. Each of these active forces in
society’s development has its own orientation and its
own “weight.”

At the same time, in the article published in IZVESTIYA
we did not find any information (except for the fact of
Russian experts having been invited) casting light on the
way in which the national and cultural peculiarities of
the country that is the subject of the forecast are taken
into account, especially in the case of a country like
Russia. Obviously, for relatively short periods of devel-
opment this requirement is not so important, since the
necessary information is present in the initial data. But
in a more general case this factor becomes highly signif-
icant and needs to be included in the model.

Are such models applicable to the analysis of the transi-
tional processes taking place, for instance, in our
country? Not such a trivial question.

On the one hand, for brief periods of evolutionary
development, even the simpler models can be used. On
the other, in order to describe certain political changes of
a qualitative nature it is necessary to use a significantly
larger number of parameters or, in “Factions” termi-
nology, to take account of an exceptionally wide spec-
trum of political forces. This makes doing the job by the
“Factions” methodology an extremely labor-intensive
and therefore unrealistic process.

Therefore, despite all the merits noted, certain conclu-
sions obtained through the “Factions” program must be
treated with caution.

After the “Point of No Return”

Let us now look at the content of the results of the
analysis. Some of these results are fully confirmed by our
own calculations.

The finding on the irreversibility of the sociopolitical
changes that have taken place in our country seems
important to us. However, depending on context, this
finding needs clarification. The point of fundamental
transition, the possibility of return to a highly central-
ized, closed society, was passed in the fall of 1994
(according to our calculations). And indeed, a number of
critical events in the fall did not lead to a change of
direction. At the same time, the inherently unstable,
vacillatory nature of social development always allows
for the possibility of retreat, although usually on a
smaller scale. The fact that Russian society was informed
of the forecast of the Russian situation carried out
according to “Factions” methodology only after the
“point of no return” is an eloquent illustration of the
model's limited potential. Such means of quantitative
analysis of political processes are applicable mainty to
the evolutionary stages of a society’s development, and
certainly not to transitional stages, still less crisis stages.

The conclusion that the “Russian president has done his
job” also seems an overly categorical interpretation of

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

the results of a study which essentially deals in probabil-
ities. According to our forecasts, in the fall of 1995 the
country’s sociopolitical system will approach a new
quality, which implies the possibility of structural
changes, course corrections, and so forth. As a rule such
special conditions are resolved by recertly emerged
internal forces. Long-term forecasts regarding their
development can relate only to general trends, not to
specific details, still less individuals. As an illustzation
you reed only recall how events and forecasts went in the
1993 elections. It is well known that more [or] less
accurate forecasts appeared only two weeks [before the
elections}. We think that in these sort of political mael-
stroms software such as “Factions” is extremely inaccu-
rate.

Will It Prove Possible To Reduce the ““Temperature”?

How do we see the future development of the situation in
the country in the light of our attempts to model socio-
political processes?

Developments in 1995 will be marked by the most
significant spell of “‘bad weather” in the entire period of
reform. But the fact that the burdens (“precipitation”)
will be greatly spread out in time (there will probably be
a bit more in the final quarter of the year) will alleviate
our perception of adversity. There are quite a few
circumstances conducive to this. They include the tre-
mendous experience accumulated by state structures in
recent years in tracking and correcting developing crisis
processes. As an illustration we can cite the compara-
tively stable development of the currency market during
the hardest period shown by statistics over many years—
winter of this year—achieved for the first time in all the
years of reform thanks to the efforts of the Central Bank
and the government. So there are objective possibilities
of keeping the average “temperature” dvring this
“adverse weather” at a relatively low level (although
quantitative analysis of our data indicates that there will
still be a few peaks on the country’s political spectrum in
the immediate future). The momentum of instability will
be conserved. It will take time to overcome it.

We have obtained our latest evaluations from study of
the dynamics of one quite strictly defined indicator of
stability—the state of the political climate in society.
This parameter, in turn, is calculated on the basis of
demographic statistics, indicators of the level of crime,
and so forth.

The qualitative content of the concept of climate is
connected with its prognostic potential. Like the natural
climate, society’s political climate establishes its predis-
position toward social and political actions directed
either toward ensuring society’s stability or toward
heightening its instability. The analogy between the
natural and the political climates has not only a qualita-
tive basis. They are also united by the probabilistic
response of natural and social systems to particular
changes in climate.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

The fewest indicators of stability were registered in 1994.
Of late there has been a tendency toward an increase in
them, which in combination with modeling experiments
has enabled us to draw up forecasts for the future.

Stable progress toward social and political equilibrium,
according to our calculations, is possible from 1997
simultaneously with the start of a long-term industrial
upswing, but the attainment of substantially positive
values in indicators of stability is expected no earlier
than 2002, no matter what contenders for top state posts
promise in the heat of election battle.

According to our assessments, the most dynamic phase
of the country’s industrial upswing may lead in 2006 to
the attainment at lorg last of a living standard compa-
rable with the industrially developed countries of the
world.

Russia’s progress toward stable equilibrium and eco-
nomic affluence may be hampered by the crisis in the
United States expected at the turn of the millennium. We
should make the proviso that the shortage of statistics at
the authors’ disposal and the secondary nature of this
direction in our studies do not make it possible to draw
as categorical conclusions regarding the United States as
the “Factions” experts drew regarding our country.

The results of our studies of certain aspects of American
civilization indicate that in the first half of the eighties
the United States entered a new phase in its technolog-
ical development qualitatively different from the pre-
ceding 50 years or so in the country’s history. The
tendency toward instability inherent in this phase may in
time develop into a serious socioeconomic crisis. We are
not talking about a crisis like the “Great Depression,” of
course. We do not even make any assumptions about its
depth, especially as it is more appropriate to deal with
our own “domestic” crises. (“All happy families
resemble each other, but each unhappy family is
unhappy in its own way.”)

The Russian economy’s close linkage to the world
economy may in this instance turn into the other side of
the coin (although a tendency toward isolation in the
modern world is even more futile). An emerging priority
in Russia’s foreign policy is the need to formulate a new
blueprint for relations with the centers shaping the world
economy, particularly the United States.

The mutual interest in prospects of stable development
is a fundamentally new element in relations between our
countries. Within the framework of solving this global
problem of world order our country will be able to offer
a very particular type of “humanitarian aid” to the
United States. Russia’s unique experience regarding
crisis development in the 20th century may prove handy
for the United States on the threshold of the new
millennium.

Rybkin, Kono View K~rils Dispute

[FBIS Transcribed Text] TOKYO, April 27 (ITAR-
TASS)}—Expansion of trade and economic relations
between Russia and Japan can help settle the territorial
dispute about the South Kuril islands, State Duma
chairman Ivan Rybkin told Japan Foreign Minister
Yohei Kono during their conversation here today.

Ivan Rybkin, who is leading a Russian parliamentarian
delegation on its visit to Tokyo, assured Japanese offi-
cials that he would make all possible efforts to resolve
the outstanding problems between Moscow and Tokyo
in order to facilitate their rapprochement and bring the
two sides closer to the signing of the peace treaty. He said
the groundwork for such efforts should be provided by
the development of bilateral trade and economic rela-
tions.

Rybkin also spoke in favour of setting up as soon as
possible an intergovernmental Russian-Japanese eco-
nomic mission in line with the accord reached during
First Vice Premier Oleg Soskovets’ visit to Tokyo last
November. ~

Yohei Kono agreed that disputes should be settled on the
basis of the Tokyo declaration but said that Tokyo
remained committed to the principle of “expanding
balance”’ in its relations with Moscow, implying that the
development of economic relations should proceed in
line with concomitant progress in the political sphere,
i.e. the progressive settlement of the dispute over the
Kuril islands.

Foreign Ministry Official on Support for NPT

LD2704 163295 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1509
GMT 27 Apr 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, April 27 (Inter-
fax)—Russia supports “limitless and unconditional
extension of the [Nuclear] Non-Proliferation Treaty
[NPT]” at a conference on the issue in New York, the
first deputy director of the Russian Foreign Ministry’s
security and disarmament department, Mikhail Kok-
eyev, told a news conference in Moscow Thursday.

According to Kokeyev, “the overwhelming majority” of
178 states represented at the conference supported
extending the document as they understand its signifi-
cance “for the non-proliferation of nuclear arms, preven-
tion of arms race and cooperation in using nuclear
energy in peaceful purposes.”

Kokeyev said “if the treaty is not extended, this will
affect both regional and global-scale security as well as
possible prospects for the universal disarmament.”

Kokeyev said the conference was already working at a
treaty adoption mechanism, whether they will vote
openly or in private.

Kokeyev remarked that Russian Foreign Minister
Andrey Kozyrev stood by the open ballot, because “‘each
state has to assume historical responsibility.”

According to U.S. experts, 95 out of 178 states had
supported the treaty’s extension on April 25. Another 32
states are inclined to follow suit, while 24 states are
unambiguously against.

Ministry Favors Gradual Withdrawal From
Moldova

LD2804092795 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 0911
GMT 28 Apr 95

[By Yuriy Kozlov]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, April 28 (ITAR-
TASS)—There can be no hasty pull-out of Russian
troops from Moldovan territory at present, Russian
Foreign Ministry official Nikita Matkovskiy told Itar-
Tass responding to a request to comment on the State
Duma resolution of April 28 [date as received] con-
cerning the situation around the 14th Army currently
stationed in Moldova’s Transdniester region.

Matkovskiy said that in principle it was not within the
competence of the Foreign Ministry to comment on acts
and resolutions of the legislative branch of power. He
said the task was made all the more difficult by the fact
that the Foreign Ministry had not yet received the
official text of the Duma Resolution.

“*As concerns the core of the matter, I would like to stress
first of all, that we proceed from the recognition of
Moldova’s independence and sovereignty, and the inad-
missibility of intervention in its domestic affairs. The
development of genuine friendly relations with Moldova
meets Russia’s vital interests,” Matkovskiy said.

The intergovernmental agreement signed in Moscow on
October 1, 1994, concerning the legal status, procedure
and schedule for the withdrawal of the Russian armed
forces temporarily stationed on the territory of Moldova,

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 13

as well as the subsequently signed additional documents
to the same effect, created a legal framework for the
stationing of the 14th Army in Moldova and made
groundless any speculations about the meaning and
character of its presence there, the Foreign Ministry
official said.

In keeping with mutual understanding, the Russian
troops will be withdrawn from Moldovan territory
without haste, on a step-by-step basis during the period
of three years from the moment the above * greement
comes into force, he said.

The period was determined taking into account the
organisational, technical and financial problems associ-
ated with the withdrawal of the 14th Army.

The withdrawal will begin with the pull-out of some
units which are of no vital importance to the Army’s
functioning and cor.:hat ability, Matkovskiy said.

The Foreign Ministry official further stressed the impor-
tance of the accord laid down in the agreement con-
cerning practical steps for the withdrawal of the troops.
They will be taken concomitantly with the political
settlement of the conflict over Transdniester region.

Russia will continue to mediate, jointly with the OSCE
mission, political dialogue between Chisinau and
Tiraspol in which much depends on the good will,
activity and readiness to look for compromise solutions
on the part of the two conflicting parties, the official
said.

He stressed the need for carrying out this complex and
painstaking work “in a businesslike, constructive
manner and in a quiet situation without emotional
outbursts”, proceeding from long- term interests of both
Russia and Moldova, including its Transdniester region.

As concerns the possible restructuring of the command
of the 14th Army, the Russian official said that making
decisions on this and on the Army commander, General
Aleksandr Lebed falls within the competence of the
Russian Defence Minister. He expressed the hope that
the decisions will be made upon due consideration of all
interests involved.

with support in regions,” Chernomyrdin said.

great country and raise its international prestige. Our
people deserve a better life. With this in view, myself and
the like-minded people in the government, the Federal
Council, in the regions, in the industry and in public
organisations decided to form an election mov. ment
which will give the voters a chance to make their choice
in favour of security, stability and development,” the
Prime Minister said.

Yeltsin Aide: Two Blocs Form Stabilizing Factor

MK2804093095 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian 28
Apr 95 p 2

[Report by Yelena Tregubova under the “Clarifications”
rubric: ““Georgiy Satarov: ‘Two Blocs Will Stabilize the
Country.” Messrs. Chernomyrdin, Rybkin ‘Possibly’
Seen as Successors by President”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] If “the idea of two blocs” is
realized, presidential adviser Georgiy Satarov said at
yesterday’s news conference, “this will resolve a dra-
matic antinomy that has currently seized human minds:
On one hand, one would like to hold elections; on the
other, one is fearful that this might inflame the entire
political system.” Once two formidable centrist blocs are
actually established, he believes, “even” presidential

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

elections “will cease to be a choice between stability and
a universal collapse.” Those advocating “an adjust-
ment” of reform will “have to seek that within an
established political system.”

Mr. Satarov described the difference between the
emerging left- of-center and right-of-center blocs as fol-
lows: “Clearly, Chernomyrdin is the continuation of the
current line, economic stabilization, and betting on
successful economic partners. Rybkin, for his part,
means increasing assistance to unsuccessful economic
partners.”

The presidential aide assured journalists that presently
Boris Yeltsin is “greatly interested” in holding parlia-
mentary elections—‘“to an even greater extent than in
implementing his formula for Duma elections,” that is,
125 deputies on party tickets and 175 in majority dis-
tricts. For this reason, Georgiy Satarov promised, the
president will, “too all appearances, sign any”” compro-
mise version of the law on Duma elections on which the
upper and lower chambers may agree, and is even ready
to mediate in their talks.

Mr. Satarov expects “much fiercer battles” over the law
on forming the Federation Council. The State Duma has
passed a bill calling for direct elections for the Federa-
tion Council. Georgiy Satarov is certain that this is
clearly at odds with the Constitution, which stipulates
that the Senate must be formed of representatives of
each region’s executive and legislative authorities. The
incumbent Federation Council, which consists for the
most part precisely of representatives of local authori-
ties, will insist on the right to enter the Senate ex officio.
Judging from Mr. Satarov’s words, the president, too,
will back this arrangement: “This will stabilize the
situation.”

With regard to presidential elections, Georgiy Satarov
assured journalists that Boris Yeltsin “has not yet told
him either whether he is going to run for reelection.”
After the news conference, in the lobby, the presidential
aide hinted to correspondents that Viktor Chernomyrdin
and Ivan Rybkin have become heads of new blocs
because they are “possibly” seen as presidential succes-

Agriculture Minister on Chechnya, Electoral Blocs

MK2804 130095 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian 28
Apr 95 p 2

[Report by Mikhail Lantsman under the “Field” rubric:
“Aleksandr Nazarchuk Rebuts Viktor Chernomyrdin’s
Statement. If Farmers So Demand, Agrarian Faction
Will Raise the Question of No-Confidence in Govern-
ment”’}

[FBIS Translated Text] Agriculture Minister Aleksandr
Nazarchuk announced at yesterday’s conference:
“Nature cannot wait until hostilities cease in Chechnya.”
He has already visited Groznyy twice so as to get down
to restoring the republic’s agro- industrial sector on the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

spot in liberated areas. Unfortunately, Mr. Nazarchuk
noted, during the three years of Dudayev’s rule, com-
modity production in the state agro-industrial sector has
been fully disrupted: Equipment has been misappropri-
ated, all public cattle have been moved to “private
quarters.” The government of Salambek Khadzhiyev has
tried to select new collective farm chiefs, but later
decided to put organizational and legal matters off ana
focused on solving the probiems of food self-sufficiency.

According to Mr. Nazarchuk, the Russian Agriculture
Ministry has done its utmost for field work in Chechnya
to be carried out within a period set by nature. A total of
150 tractors, 100 combines, and 50 trucks worth 27
billion rubles [R] have been delivered to the republic
under leasing contracts along with R19 billion rubles
worth of fuel and lubricants. As of today, 77,000 hectares
of arable land out of the 100,000 hectares planned have
been sown with crops. The Agriculture Ministry has sent
R73 billion to Chechnya in investment to restore the
agro-industrial infrastructure. The minister also expects
the Russian Federation Government to allocate R400
billion from the state budget to construct housing in the
countryside. By Mr. Nazarchuk’s estimate, the Chechen
agro-industrial sector is better equipped with materials,
machinery, and seeds than average Russian farms.

But journalists’ attention centered on matters relating to
the Agrarian Party’s attitude to the Chernomyrdin cab-
inet and the ele. tion campaign. According to Mr. Naz-
archuk, the Russian agro-industrial sector will be unable
to survive in conditions of an austere financial policy
and an absence of centralized Central Bank loans. Pres-
ently, the agriculture minister acknowledged, he is nego-
tiating with a number of commercial banks a R1-trillion
loan at the Central Bank’s interest rate. At the All-Russia
Farmers’ Meeting, which was held in April, the agrarians
voiced a number of demands to the government and
tasked the agrarian faction at the State Duma with
raising the question of no-confidence in the government
should it fail to comply with them. Mr. Nazarchuk said
in that regard that although “this procedure itself is not
conducive to consolidation,” unlike the Liberal Demo-
cratic Party of Russia, the Agrarian Party will not back
away—and if the farmers insist, the question will be
included in the agenda.

The minister also refuted a statement by the prime
minister that all cabinet members are ready to join the
electoral bloc he is presently setting up. The Agrarian
Party, in his view, should contest the elections with its
own list of candidates. Nonetheless, the party's electoral
platform will be discussed 5 May at a joint meeting of the
Agrarian Party board and the Presidium of the Agricul-
tural Trade Unions Central Committee. The elaboration
of a definitive stance, Mr. Nazarchuk said, will depend
on the government’s agricultural policy, otherwise
“farmers will not understand us.”

Effort To Start Two-Party System ‘Timely and
Noble’

MM2704134195 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI in
Russian 27 Apr 95 p 1

[Article by Feliks Babitskiy under the “Opinion” rubric:
“Centrist Blocs Will Help To Squeeze Extremists out of
Big Politics”}

{[FBIS Translated Text] Simultaneous statements by the
president and the premier to the effect that two centrist
blocs will be formed in the very near future have caused
a sensation. Viktor Chernomyrdin himself intends to
head one of these blocs while, according to the president,
State Duma Speaker ivan Rybkin intends to head the
other. True, the latter has so far only welcomed the blocs’
creation in principle, without publicizing his own role in
this. But we have learned from well-informed sources in
the Federation of ladependent Trade Unions of Russia
that the future members of the left-centrist association
have already reached a preliminary accord with him.

Incidentally, the list of these very members is also of
interest. The “Rybkin” bloc could be joined by the
Democratic Party of Russia (Glazyev), the Social Dem-
ocratic Party of Russia (Golov), and the Socialist Party
of the Working People (Vartazarova), but also, according
to other data, by the Party of Social Democracy (Yakov-
lev) and the Russian Social Democratic People’s Party
(Lipitskiy). Analysts also slate the ““Women of Russia”
movement (Fedulova), the “‘Realists’’ movement
(Petrov), moderate communists and agrarians, and the
Federation of Commodity Producers (Skokov) for it. As
for the “Chernomyrdin” bloc, it could embrace the
“Stability” and “New Regional Policy” parliamentary
factions with the corresponding “Stable Russia” and
“Regions of Russia” movements, the Russian Union of
Industrialists and Entrepreneurs with the twin parties
created on its basis—the All-Russia Renewal Union
(Vladislaviev) and the OPP [expansion unknown]
(Shcherbakov]—the Party of the Majority (Grechnev),
which is allied with Volskiy and Luzhkov, the Repub-
lican Party of the Russian Federation (Lysenko), the
moderate section of the “Business and Politics” round-
table (those entrepreneurs who do not support Yavlin-
skiy), and, finally, on certain conditions, Russia’s Dem-
ocratic Choice (Gaydar).

The purpose of creating the two blocs is to win from the
radically inclined parties the votes of the majority of
voters, who are tired, so they believe, of upheavals. The
fact that the head of state himself was the first to report
the news of the blocs’ creation to the press points to the
level at which this political decision was adopted.

Well, the idea is good in itself and had matured within
society long ago. But it would also be dishonest not to
warn what difficulties lie in wait for it on the way to
being realized. Let us start with the fact that the mem-
bers of the two blocs should not be united on the basis of
their formally declared values. Meanwhile, we should

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

18 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

expect not a union between just such political players
but, rather, a war for survival. In other words, it is hardly
possible to “unite in friendship” Vartazarova, Lipitskiy,
and Yakovlev, because they are fighting over the same
electorate. True, it is possible to retort that one by one
they will only hinder each other, whereas by squashing
up they can not only surmount the 5- percent barrier but
also take a sizable proportion of the Duma seats. Prac-
tice shows, however, that the primary and regional
organizations of Russian parties, when such blocs are
unsympathetic to them, simply ignore the decisions of
the leaders in the capital and knock together their own
blocs in the provinces. But even if, being aware of the
need to pool efforts, the leaders do unite, their union will
resemble Siamese twins—many personalities but one sei
of insides. When these unfortunates eventually die, it is
certainly not of brotherly love.

Nevertheless, the very idea of building a two-party
system and squeezing “extremists” out of big politics is
very timely and noble. But, as the old song goes, when
moving toward your goe" in any matter, you must see all
the obstacles at once.

Ryabov: Elections Will Be Held on Time

MK2704102095 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian 27
Apr 95 p 2

[Report by Petr Zhuravlev under the “Assurance”
rubric: “Nikolay Ryabov: ‘Elections Will Be Held on
Time.’ Central Electoral Commission Head Does Not
Think President Would Jeopardize Democracy in
Russia”’]

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

[FBIS Translated Excerpt] Central Electoral Commis-
sion head Nikolay Ryabov said yesterday that parlia-
mentary elections will “be held on schedule without
fail.” “Currently one must be a reckless adventurist or
have a deep hatred for one’s people and state to talk
about delaying elections,” he said, emphasizing that the
Constitution, “the general line of society’s development,
and an evident stabilization in the life of the country”
provide guarantees that the elections will be held on
time. Viktor Chernomyrdin’s statement on setting up an
electoral bloc, Mr. Ryabov believes, is yet more proof of
this assertion.

Thanks to Vladimir Shumeyko, the CEC head said, the
Federation Council appears to have already been
deprived of an opportunity to consider ihe draft law on
the election of lower chamber deputies, which was
passed by the State Duma yet again 21 April: the next
Senate meeting is scheduled for 4 May, but it is clear that
for understandable reasons (holidays) it will be impos-
sible to reach a quorum, the constitutional deadline by
which the Federation Council must consider laws (two
weeks since they are passed by the State Duma) will be
over, and the election law will be automatically sent to
the president.

At the time of a Chechen crisis and facing many other
problems, Nikolay Ryabov noted, if Boris Yeltsin resorts
to his right of veto, he “would take over responsibility
for the fate of democracy in Russia—in the negative
sense.” The CEC chief is certain, however, that the
president will not do that. It should be recalled that
according to earlier reports Mr. Yeltsin is opposed to the
225:225 ratio of “single-seat” deputies and deputies
elected on party tickets, which is provided for in the
Duma draft law. But Mr. Ryabov made it clear that an
absence of election legislation will not provide a pretext
for postponing the election date. [passage omitted]

Move for Six-Month Election Delay Gaining
Ground

MK2504104095 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian 25
Apr 95 p 2

[Report by Sergey Parkhomenko: “Delayniks Gaining
Upper Hand”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] Confidential but trustworthy
reports coming from the Kremlin indicate that things in
Russia are indeed heading toward a postponement of
federal parliamentary elections. The other day your
SEGODNYA correspondent had a chance to see for
himself that a similar assurance has gradually come to
grip Moscow’s diplomatic community, which tradition-
ally has its own network of informants, a rather peculiar
but fairly reliable one at that.

Evidently, the issue is still not a cancellation of State
Duma elections for an unspecified period, but their
deferral to a more or less clearly specified later date. To
all appearances, for six months. Therefore, the idea is to

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

hold parallel election campaigns in spring 1996 and—in
mid- or late-July—concurrent elections for both the
Federal Assembly lower chamber and for president.

All this is beginning to look like a democratic end of the
world, particularly given that the terms of the Moscow
and St. Petersburg mayors will expire by the same time;
what is more, it also appears qu'te realistic that elections
for administration heads (.nayors, governors, you name
it) and parliaments (aumas, majlises, khurals, iegisla-
tures, whatever they’re called) may be held within the
same dates in a whole slew of Russian territories.

The likelihood of deferring or canceling presidential
elections is a subject for a separate analysis. But at this
stage, judging from talk in Kremlin lobbies (particularly
in near-Kremlin parks, where bureaucrats from the pres-
idential adminisiration have recently taken up talking to
journalists), one may firmly conclude that they still see a
forecast with presidential elections held within constitu-
tional dates as the main working scenario. It should be
noted parenthetically that fully defeatist sentiments and
forebodings in that regard are visibly prevalent in the
presidential administration. Something for which the
author of these lines cannot give a sensible account,
being sincerely confident of the fact that Boris Yeltsin,
despite everything, still has—objectively speaking—
fairly good chances of successfully renewing his presi-
dential term for another five years by legal, that is,
electoral, means. Otherwise, he would be devoured by
his own cronies.

Returning to the subject of parliamentary elections, let
us bear in mind two diametrically opposed concepts that
have up to now been bitterly divided administration
officials whose views can at least in some measure affect
a definitive decision on the dates of the voting.

According to the first concept, the upcoming parliamen-
tary elections should be seen as a public opinion poll of
sorts—an extremely costly poll, but one that is as repre-
sentative as possible and, hence. reliable—preceding a
presidential race. Proponents of this viewpoint have
been arguing that a six-month pause between the two
votes would be fully sufficient for the ruling team to
prepare for the “‘main” (and singularly important)
expression of the popular will. Namely, it could coolly
assess Boris Yeltsin’s chances for reelection; select the
most advantageous “‘load-bearing’’ candidacy for
upcoming elections in case the assessment turns out to be
negative; draw up a long-term election campaign strategy
for its candidate in any event; and hold a series of local
political and economic actions designed seriously to
discredit a left majority in the parliament’s new lineup.

There are no differences of assessment with regard to the
Duma’s would-be majority. Everyone is absolutely cer-
tain here that a voting majority in the new deputy corps
will be represented by the communists and the agrarians
(which appears to be correct) together with Liberal
Democratic Party of Russia [LDPR] members, of whom
a whole horde is expected to enter the Sixth Duma (this

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 19

is nonsense, inevitable because “‘generals always prepare
for past wars’). Opponents of an “experimental
approach” are undeterred by this prospect, however: No
serious harm will come from the Duma. they think,
however obnoxious its lineup might be, so limited its
parliamentary prerogatives currently are. And, after all,
when it becomes really unbearable, it would not be very
difficult to dissolve the Duma rigiit after the presidentia?
elections: You nominate a Lev Ponomarev for prime
minister three times in a row, receive three rejections—
and voila.

The opponents—let us call them a party of “‘delayniks’-
’—draw on the same forecast as their main argument in
a dispute with the “experimenters.” In their view, six
months of political coexistence with a communist-
Zhirinovskiite Duma would be simply unendurable. It is
not yet clear who would succeed more in discrediting
each other during these six months. A long series of
parliamentary investigations on all conceivable matters
would kick off, accompanied by demands that deputies
be given access to extremely sensitive (particularly,
financial) documents. Amendments to electoral legisla-
tion would come next. Wages of budget-financed indus-
tries and the minimum pension would be driven upward.
The president would have to fire back with endless
“vetoes” or once again to incur too great expenditures to
court the Federation Council.

Any reasonable electoral strategy in these circum-
stances—the “delayniks” continue, choking with emo-
tion—would be out of the question. What is more,
sooner or later Boris Yeltsin could lose his temper,
cancel presidential elections as such outright at one
stroke, and then go to Sochi for an unprecedentedly long
period. And although the Chechen story has shown that
even a very big scandal can be weathered and that no
Council of Europe resolutions break any bones—this
time one may well trigger a really serious international
outcry.

So, to all appearances, the “‘delayniks” are gradually
gaining the upper hand over their opponents. They even
seem to have completely overpowered them.

Let us wait for yet another presidential trip—somewhere
to the Urals, for example. Such tours traditionally tend
to produce a rich harvest of sensational statements.
Nothing helps the president see the situation so clearly
and express himself so definitely as grateful audiences of
ordinary working people. By the time the president
departs to meet the people, his advisers are usually
rushing to present their helpful considerations on urgent
matters of the day to their boss. While polishing yet
another analytical memo to their august patron, they
argue hotly, grow angry, and sometimes even fall apart.

It is not that they are completely disinterested in what
views you and I may hold. Clearly, they are of interest to
them in themselves, but when so crucial a matter comes
under consideration, they are disregarded as amateurish.
It is as if they were professionals in this political business
and we were amateurs.

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

20 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

Meanwhile, it’s our turn. It is we who are going to elect
them, isn’t it?

Ryabov Interviewed on Election Preparations

MM2504135595 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI in
Russian 25 Apr 95 p 1

[Report by Lyubov Tsukanova on interview with Central
Electoral Commission Chairman Nikolay Ryabov; date,
place of interview not given: “If Central Electoral Com-
mission Gets Started, Everybody Wins”]}

[FBIS Translated Text] Everything now being done by
political parties and public organizations pending the
parliamentary elections can be described as an election
campaign only in the most general way. Central Electoral
Commission Chairman Nikolay Ryabov stressed in a
conversation with ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI’s correspon-
dent that the official start of the election marathon will
be signaled by the appropriate Russian Federation pres-
ident’s edict. Everything done before that is unofficial.
Apart from the Central Electoral Commission’s
actions—right now it is making strictly official prepara-
tions for the elections, and it is a case of the earlier the
preparations begin the greater the chances that the
election campaign will pass off without complications.

What is the most important thing to ensure an uninter-
rupted technological process of preparing for the elec-
tions? The Central Electoral Commission chairman cites
the legislative base above all. It is a most alarming aspect
because as of today only one law is ready—on guarantees
of citizens’ electoral rights. The rest are still going around
parliamentary corridors. Without laws it is difficult for
us to begin large-scale work on a legal knowledge pro-
gram, Ryabov says, above all with the organizers of the
election process.

The Central Electoral Commission must urgently carry
out the state registration of voters. The Central Electoral
Commission chairman said that this is the first time that
this is being done in the country and that there is a
massive amount of work, but as a result both the
Federation as a whole and every component is obtaining
a reliable database. This is absolutely necessary for the
functioning of state structures, for the activity of public
associations, and, of course, for electoral commissions.
Unfortunately, Ryabov said, the executive branch has no
documents regulating this work, therefore the Central
Electoral Commission, relying on the law, has had to
issue a temporary statute according to which the work
will be carried out. The deadline is very tight, all work
must be completed no later than 10 June. The technical
wherewithal to do this does exist: Fairly good computer
facilities have already been set up in Federation compo-
nents and resources for this work have also already been
allocated by the Central Electoral Commission. Unlike
in previous years, information from voters will be sys-
tematic rather than fragmented, and it will be rechecked
and revised twice a year. The system will also make it
possible to mark out the borders of districts with a great

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

degree of reliability—this is also a very important stage
in the preparation of the election campaign.

Installing the “Elections” automated system is a very
important task. We are now already entering, Ryabov
said, the practical stage, in which it is necessary to
distribute equipment, instruct cadres, and introduce the
relevant programs in thousands of the country’s cities
and rayons. To do this it is necessary to allocate special
premises and train cadres, who could in the near future
start getting to grips with the equipment and thoroughly
preparing for the elections.

Open Letter to Yeltsin on Privatization

954E0883A Moscow DELOVOY MIR in Russian 12
Apr 95 pp I, 2

[Open letter to B.N. Yeltsin, president of the Russian
Federation}

[FBIS Translated Text] Esteemed Boris Nikolayevich!

Concerning the meeting of the Government of the Rus-
sian Federation of 30 March 1995 devoted to the tasks
for implementation of stage two of privatization in 1995,
we consider it necessary to call your attention to certain
problems of this stage of privatization that are causing
serious concern.

The draft documents examined at the meeting of the
Government of the Russian Federation contemplate
prior to the end of 1995 the early sale of a considerable
quantity of the blocks of shares consolidated in state
ownership of privatized enterprises, including structure-
forming enterprises of the base branches of industry. It is
planned to secure the receipt for the federal treasury here
of monetary resources of the order of 9.5 trillion rubles.
It is contemplated selling the shares in large blocks and
in the shortest possible time. Your message to the
Federal Assembly cites as a principal task of privatiza-
tion policy in 1995 the “maximum furtherance of the
upturn in investment activity with the simultaneous
palpable replenishment of budgets of all levels... by
means of a transition from the distributive to the invest-
ment phase of privatization.”

But the strategy of privatization proposed by the State
Property Committee of Russia is not geared to the
adequate accomplishment of this task. The absence of
substantiation and calculations in the documents that
have been rzesented compels doubt as to the feasibility
of the receipt of the planned federal budget revenue from
the sale of state property. Nor are the said documents
oriented toward the creation of the conditions for an
influx of investments into the privatized enterprises.

The accelerated sale of the shares of the enterprises
affords no guarantee that the federal budget will receive
the planned revenue. The proposed privatization
strategy will lead to a reduction in the sale price of the
blocks of shares of enterprises of the priority branches of
the Russian economy. State property will sold here at

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

prices considerably lower than those that would be
established were a strategy of a metered sale of shares
oriented toward long-term investors and favorable sale
terms adopted.

A sizable nonstate sector of the economy has been
created in Russia at the present time. Under these
conditions the second—monetary—stage of privatiza-
tion should be of a more balanced nature.

The possible consequences of the sale of blocks of shares
reserved «wo the state of enterprises in key branches of the
national economy such as the oil and gas and rnilitary-
industrial complexes, power engineering, and ferrous
and nonferrous metallurgy shouia be studied in detail to
prevent damage to the strategic interests of Russia and
its national security.

It is essential to determine in legislation in which
branches of the economy, in what volume, and within
what timeframe public ownership should be preserved.

Under the conditions of the formation of a market
system of management the consolidation of a proportion
of state property in the authorized capital of the priva-
tized enterprises would make it possible to ensure state
regulation in the base branches of industry. The latter
may be achieved only if there is the possibility of state
influence on the structure-forming enterprises of the
base branches of the economic complex via control of
the blocks of shares reserved to public ownership. The
state should be an equal subject of market relations.

The success of the reform process in Russia and its future
as a great power will largely depend on the policy of
privatization that is adopted. This applies first and
foremost to the legislation governing privatization. An
official privatization program should be confirmed in
legislation as soon as possible. The State Duma is pre-
pared to reexamine the draft federal law “On the Officied
Program of Privatization of State and Municipal Enter-
prises in the Russian Federation After 1 July 1994.”

We consider it necessary also to call your attention to the
need for the formalization in legislation of the procedure
of the sale of the shares previously consolidated in
federal ownership and of the harmonization of the
decisions on the release and sale of blocks of shares.

In connection with the foregoing we request that you
consider in conjunction with the Government and the
Federal Assembly of the Russian Federation the strategy
of the second stage of privatization and, specifically, the
expediency of the early release from federal ownership
and the sale before the end of 1995 of large blocks of
shares of base enterprises of the Russian economy.

[Signed] V.S. Medvedev, New Regional Policy group of
deputies

M.L. Lapshin, Agrarian Party of Russia faction

S.Yu. Glazyev, Democratic Party of Russia faction
Ye.F. Lakhova, Women of Russia political movement
faction
