Savitskiy on Leadership, Moral Issues

954F2151A Moscow SOBESEDNIK in Russian No. 15,
Apr 95 p 3

{Interview with Vitaliy Savitskiy by Larisa Usova; place
and date not given: “The Devil Does Not Sleep!”]

[FBIS Translated Text] Vitaliy Savitskiy, leader of the
Christian Democratic Union—Christians of Russia
Party and deputy of the State Duma, is convinced that
the struggle for our souls between God and the devil does
not cease for one second. And it is not always God who
is victorious.... To the atheist, this material will appear at
least strange. But is it not strange that in our primordi-
ally Christian country we have ceased to evaluate people
from the viewpoint of Christian morals? What criteria
have we not thought up, and this primordial Russian
criterion we have forgotten? Although history has shown
us, it would seem—very vividly, alas—that nothing good
comes from a disregard for Christian roots.

[Usova] Vitaliy Viktorovich, we reiterate the Christian
commandments quite often, everybody knows them,
but, obviously, to live in sacred observance of them is
well-nigh impossible. Do you, for example, manage to
“love the deputies as yourself’?

[Savitskiy] I pray for them. All of them. Because there
are no lost souls for God, and even the most inveterate
wretch may return to God. The most important thing
from which the deputies’ souls need to be saved are the
numerous temptations. And prayers alone do not get the
job done here—specific actions are needed. I am con-
stantly raising, say, the question of deputies being
stripped of their immunity. After all, according to the
Constitution, all are equal before the law, but in reality,
all except the deputies. In no other country is this the
case. It was because we have this infamous deputy
immunity that Deputy Skorochkin died. I am not in
sympathy with the kind of activity in which he engaged,
and it is incomprehensible to me, as both a Christian and
a citizen, how he could support a family in Britain and
have his own personal security. But I grieve for him and
I realize that had the Duma permitted investigatory
actions in respect to Skorochkin, he would now be alive.
Whether he would have fled to England or would be
doing time in Butyrka is immaterial; he would be alive!

(Usova] Your prayers, obviously, do not always reach
God, because, alas, not all deputies can resist tempta-
tion.

[Savitskiy}] Alas.... In this sense the most striking
example is what happened with Bauer, the democrat cast
by the will of fate into the high office of chairman of the
Committee for Organization of the Work of the Duma.
This person found himself in the office of distributor of

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

22 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

benefits and changed before our eyes. He demonstra-
tively allocated himself an apartment in the area of
Smolenskaya Square and furnished it with antiques and
changed his suits just about every day. Result: He was
removed from office. Whether a deputy has changed in
his heart or not may, generally, be judged by the clothes
he wears. If a person shows up all the while in stylish
coats and smokes expensive cigars, he is, consequently,
most likely unscrupulous. Our deputy’s wages, despite
the fact that the majority of us live in hotel. and dine in
expensive restaurants, cannot extend to all this.

[Usova] For what do deputies take bribes, I wonder?

[Savitskiy] For lobbying, primarily. From foolish busi-
nessmen, who very often do not understand what a
deputy can actually do, and what, he cannot. Besides,
deputy immunity extends to a deputy’s property also,
and for this reason any firm may be registered to him
and money laundered through it, and if it goes bust,
there are no consequences for the deputy for this.
Approximately one-third of the deputies have such firms
today.

(Usova] Has there been a change, in your view, in a
person who literally shot up “from rags to riches”? I refer
to Ivan Petrovich Rybkin, who from being a little-known
politician has become a leader of the country.

[Savitskiy] He has, generally, been well known for a long
time. He was long known for his astonishing flexibility as
a beneficiary of well-placed telephone calls and as an
invertebrate with quite magnificent bodily shapes. He
was, after all, chairman of the Communists of Russia
faction in the former Supreme Soviet, and when the
Communist Party was banned, he deserted to the agrar-
ians, and he subsequently attempted to go back. His
election to the office of chairman of the Duma was none
too attractive either. He was two votes short, and one
vote was, in addition, questionable. It was suggested that
Rybkin hold a runoff, and he would in this case, | am
sure, have won. But he was afraid to take the risk, and he
succumbed to temptation, which, I am sure, will now
cost him dear. His first drive through the Spasskiye Gate
into the Kremlin in a black limousine made a strong
impression on him, I believe. He ceased to uphold the
interests of both the communists and the agrarians, the
main thing for him being to dig himself in, in his
advantageous position, understanding full well that in
two years’ time he would have the support neither of the
communists nor of the agrarians. He has become a
spineless individual with an enormous staff of aides and
advisers from the former CPSU. I am afraid that in
today’s situation, Rybkin will insist on a postponement
of the elections.

[Usova] Vladimir Volfovich Zhirinovskiy talks a great
deal in his speeches about the Russian idea, about
Russia. Could you evaluate his activity from the view-
point of Christian morals?

[Savitskiy] I have known Zhirinovskiy since 1988. He
was at that time a normal, modest person, and we would

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

drink tea together in the kitchen from mayonnaise jars.
He made friends with many civil rights activists at that
time, dressed modestly, and even his outward appear-
ance was different. Now everything has changed, but I
pray for him all the same, because I imagine that on
Judgment Day this person will catch it more than anyone
else. Zhirinovskiy is a person whom Satan is leading.
This is shown, for example, in the fact that he abjured his
father. But the main thing is that, as a politician, he is
perfectly consciously leading people nowhere, and he
needs people merely as a means to achieve his future
goals. But this from the Christian viewpoint is the most
fearful punishment, for it is said in Holy Scripture that
“the punishment is 77 times greater for one who tempts
others into evil.” Zhirinovskiy is inciting hatred and
affording an opportunity for all that is most ugly in the
human soul to come to the surface. When journalists
print details shaming Zhirinovskiy, they forget that there
will always be people—three out of every 10, approxi-
mately—who will, following such publications, like
Zhirinovskiy all the more. Because they think: Were I
overseas, I also would as my first order of business head
for a bordello; I also hate blacks because they have more
money than me, and I also hate Jews, since I can find no
other reasons for my calamitous situation. Zhirinovskiy
is one of numerous politicians in Russia who has under-
stood that it is useless to work for all the voters all at
once. And his electorate is composed of people who are
beyond the standards of Christian morals and ethics. He
has gambled on villains and rogues.

[Usova] But he was the winner at the 12 December
elections. Are the majority of people in our country
scoundrels and villains?

[Savitskiy] I shall now tell you a story. My childhood was
spent in Estonia, but my family is Russian, I read
Russian books, and I once read “Timur and His Team.”
I had an excellent memory, and outside I would tell the
boys the story of the book of the writer Gaydar, of whom
few of them had heard. Everyone was so entranced by
my story-telling that they would skip dinner, even. I
would end by suggesting that we organize Timur’s team
here in the yard. I was expecting with horror that
everyone would now want to be Timur and that I, quite
a slender fellow, would not get this splendid role. But
here our main yard kingpin stepped forward and
declared: “I will be Mishka Kvakin.” “‘No, I’m Kvakin,
no, I am!” the other boys began to shout. A fight started.
Then our elder brothers joined in, then, our fathers, then
it went wall to wall, house to house. We were pulled apart
by the police.... And it was then that I understood for the
first time that the temptation of the devil is very strong.
It should not be forgotten that a struggle of the forces of
darkness and light for each human soul is under way.
And if this is forgotten, it is very easy to become villains.
I believe that at the time of the elections, people stupe-
fied by the devil became scoundrels, and I am convinced
that many subsequently saw the ' ght and have bitterly
regretted that they succumbed to the devil’s temptation
and seduction. And the blessed Augustine, who said:

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

“Government without ethics is a gang of scoundrels,”
was right. The same may be said about parliament also.
Unless we work on the souls of the members of parlia-
ment, other, dark forces will begin to work on them.

[Usova] It was for this reason, probably, that you for the
first time in the history of all the Dumas of Russia
organized the Hour of Prayer?

{Savitskiy] Yes, of course. We pray not only for a
cleansing of our souls. We pray first and foremost for our
parliamentary colleagues. It is not only we, I am sure,
who pray for the salvation of the souls of the members of
parliament, for the salvation of Russia. It is for this
reason, perhaps, that Red Square has not yet become
Minutka Square in Groznyy and that the tremendous
changes that are taking place in the country are occurring
with very little bloodshed. God hears us.

[Usova] When Gaydar began to pursue his reforms, were
the forces of light victorious over the forces of darkness
in his soul at that time?

[Savitskiy] For me Gaydar is undoubtedly an emissary of
the forces of light, primarily because he is an honest
man. He did not promise blessings in a month, whereas
many deputies were saying that paradise in Russia would
come tomorrow—we merely need to release prices. But
the forces of darkness crowded around Gaydar.

[Usova] Who specifically?

[Savitskiy] First, all of us. We wanted miracles, but did
we deserve them? We had, after all, not even repented
for having tolerated the communist regime for 70 years.
And we can, in addition, name specific persons. Gen-
nadiy Burbulis, for example. How can a politician be
taken seriously if he proposes the concept of a liberal-
conservative party; throughout the world the dividing
line runs between liberals and conservatives. And
Andrey Makarov? Even his sympathizers were incensed
when the underlying motives of his false charges against
Rutskoy were revealed. When it was demanded of
Makarov, however, that he relinquish his mandate, he
simply switched to another faction. It seems to me that
the duality of Andrey Kozyrev has become apparent as
of late. After all, Kozyrev was in contact daily with the
Kovalev group in Chechnya and had all the detailed
information and, nonetheless, continued to campaign for
the war, although he saw full well that human rights were
being violated in Chechnya. For this also, when some
people use their outward appearance, mild manners, and
the image of a cultured individual not to augment the
dignity of Russia in the world arena, but to defend
crimes against humanity and thereby crimes against
God, is Satanism.

[Usova] You have, it seems to me, adduced in conver-
sation quite a clear-cut criterion: the struggle for man’s
soul of the forces of darkness and light. Which forces are
winning the victory in the soul of our president?

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 23

[Savitskiy] It is written in Scripture that any authority
comes from God and that you should pray for your
rulers. Pray, because even the most appalling criminals
have a God-given soul and there is in them a divine
spark, which we at times extinguish ourselves.

[Usova] These words may be applied to Yeltsin directly?

[Savitskiy] Yes, directly. From my viewpoint, Yeltsin
did not consciously repent, and I discerned in his ““Con-
fessions on a Set Theme” neither sincerity nor repen-
tance. This was nothing more than an attempt to excuse
himself for actions that I, as a Christian, cannot excuse.
And one of them was the blowing up of the Ipatyev
Home, where the royal family was executed. Had Yeltsin
so wished, he could have ordered the KGB to leave the
house alone, and then the Ipatyey Home would have
been spared, as formeriy the Hermitage, the Rostral
Columns, or the Exchange building in St. Petersburg,
which also had been ordered destroyed “from above,”
were spared. I remember well Yeltsin’s first months in
power in Moscow and do not consider his methods of
bringing order to bear in the CPSU Christian. I was
always opposed to the CPSU; but I cannot accept
methods where to escape the anger of their superiors—
Yeltsin—employees were jumping out of the window.
Today Yeltsin has an irresponsible attitude toward his
political statements and is issuing promises right and left
and is not responsible for them. Yeltsin is not struggling
actively against the fledgling fascism in Russia. But we
should not leave off praying for Boris Nikolayevich: and
we should try to ensure that he can exercise his functions
in the high office for the good of Russia.

[Usova] What do you think, when Yeltsin goes to church,
is he motivated by a sincere belief in God?

[Savitskiy] I doubt that Yeltsin, like others of our
leaders, for that matter, sincerely believes in God. The
Savior said in the Sermon on the Mount: When you pray,
do so not like the Pharisees, in public, lock yourself in a
dark room so that no one can see you. Prayer demands
concentration. What kind of concentration can there be
when you are blinded by floodlights?

[Usova] In the former Supreme Soviet, Sakharov was the
Christian ideal. Are there such people in the Duma?

[Savitskiy] I wou!d like to remind you that Sakharov did
not always appear such an undoubted authority. He was
abused by many people—the Afghan vets, for example,
or Yuriy Boldyrev, who now lays claim to the role of
“mind, honor, and conscience” of our era. I would
mention Sergey Kovalev, of course, although, as you
know, there is no unequivocal opinion of his activity
either in the Duma or in society. I am greatly impressed
by the position of Ella Pamfilova, who, in whatever high
office she has been, has always remembered that she is a
human being. General Nikolay Stolyarov seems to me to
be a true Christian. I recall how, having become chief of
the KGB following the August putsch, he declared that
there would be no change in Russia until there was
repentance.

[Usova] You evaluate the deputies and many people
very severely. What about the principle “Judge not, lest
ye yourselves be judged”?

{Savitskiy] It is said in the Revelation of John the
Divine: Whom I love, I condemn! If you see a failing and
point to it, does this mean you are judging? I do not
condemn the people about whom we have spoken today,
it is simply that I am attempting to struggle for their
immortal souls. If we do not speak about sins openly, we
are hereby committing a spiritual crime.

Vitaliy Viktorovich Savitskiy is deputy chairman of the
Committee for Public Associations and Religious Orga-
nizations and leader of the Christian Democratic
Union—Christians of Russia Party. He was born in
Tallinn on 18 July 1955. He does not know what his sign
of the Zodiac is, but he wears a cross. He was a member
neither of the Pioneers nor the Communist Youth
League nor the CPSU. He was sent from high school to a
correctional vocational-technical school for burning the
Soviet flag. He worked at a plant, where he organized a
clandestine group for the study of Christian literature.
He served in the Army, in a construction battalion. He is
a graduate of the A.A. Zhdanov Leningrad University.
He was a research associate in Chernobyl. In 1988 he
organized the Man group, which subsequently became
the first Mercy society. In 1992 he was cochairman of the
Democratic Russia movement in St. Petersburg. He did
not recognize the legality of the Soviet state and for this
reason did not take part in the elections either in 1989 or
in 1990. In 1992 he ran as a member of the Christian
Democratic Union in a head-to-head constituency and
won. He is married with a 14-year-old daughter and a
seven-year-old son.

Provisions of Law on Protecting Judges, Lawmen

MM 2804095895 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 26 Apr 95 First Edition p 1

[Article by Yevgeniy Skukin: “Judges Request Protec-
tion. Federal Law ‘On State Protection for Judges and
Officials of Law Enforcement and Control Organs’
Passed” ]}

[FBIS Translated Text] Neither deputies nor the head of
state doubted the need to protect judges and employees
of the law enforcement and control organs from strong
pressure and even revenge from the criminal world. The
delay in passing the law was caused by the scarcity of the
budget revenues. In the government’s view, the law was
too expensive.

The long-awaited “breakthrough” came after the March
meeting between the president and the State Duma
chairman at which the leaders agreed on the immediate
need to institute state protection for the people who
dispense justice. Everything was decided in the Security

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

Council. The president supported the speaker’s view-
point that the law could be phased in: Some articles 10
days after publication in ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA, the
main bulk on | January 1996, and the part of Article 10
concerning the rehousing of a protected person on |
January 1997. The government was obliged to make
provision in the federal budget for the considerable
expenditure associated with the law’s implementation.

The range of security measures, which will be applied
within the law’s framework when there is a threat of
offenses against the lives, health, or property of law
enforcement officers, includes personal and residential
protection, the issuing of firearms and special individual
security equipment, and the notification of danger. But
the series of measures is so specific that it involuntarily
forces us to think about how dangerous and unpredict-
able is the fate of the people who administer justice. The
law makes provision for, in particular, the temporary
accommodation of protected judges in a safe place, the
changing of their place of work and their rehousing
elsewhere, and the changing of documents and even
appearance.

In short, this law is fully up to world standards. It also
envisages decent social protection measures for the vic-
tims of criminal offenses. Henceforth judges’ lives and
health are subject to compulsory state insurance in a sum
equal to 180 times the average monthly wage (average
monthly pay). In the event of a victim’s death their
relatives will receive this sum.

An insurance payout equal to 36 times the average
monthly wage is due to maimed judges whose bodily
injuries preclude any further possibility of their engaging
in professional activity, the multiplier being 12 in the
case of bodily injuries not entailing permanent loss of
fitness to work. Those who have lost the opportunity to
engage in professional activity will also receive monthly
compensation in the form of the difference between their
average monthly wage (pay) and the pension awarded to
them because of this, not counting the sum of state
insurance payments received. In exactly the same way
family members of those killed who are dependent on
them will receive monthly compensation in the form of
the difference between the proportion of the deceased’s
wage (pay) they are due to receive and the pension
awarded to them in the event of the loss of the bread-
winner, not counting the sum of state insurance pay-
ments received. In addition, loss incurred by the destruc-
tion of or damage to property belonging to employees of
courts and law enforcement institutions is subject to full
reimbursement.

Decree on Support for Science, Technology

MM72704144095 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 26 Apr 95 First Edition pp 3,5

[Decree No. 3360 of the Russian Federation Govern-
ment “On State Support for the Development of Science
and Scientific and Technical Developments,” signed by

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

V. Chernomyrdin, chairman of the Russian Federation
Government, and dated !7 April 1995, carried under the
general heading “Urgent Assistance for Science”)

[FBIS Translated Text] With a view to overcoming the
state of crisis in the scientific and technical sphere and
intensifying the state’s influence on shaping the innova-
tive potential of the economic transformations, and with
a view to ensuring state support for the development of
Russian science and technology, the Russian Federation
Government decrees that:

1. Urgent measures shall be implemented to improve the
financial state of scientific organizations:

a) The Russian Federation Ministry of Finance shall:

allocate by 1 May 1995 targeted funds, including trea-
sury bonds, for the payment of the federal budget arrears
for 1994 to Russian Federation state scientific centers
and scientific organizations funded by the federal budget
in respect of state investment, payment for energy
sources, leasing of buildings and premises, and munic-
ipal services. The amount of the aforesaid targeted funds
shall be determined in conjunction with the Russian
Federation Ministry of Science and Technical Policy and
interested ministries and departments following an
examination of reports on the implementation of
spending estimates in 1994;

examine with the participation of the Russian Federa-
tion Ministry of Science and Technical Policy, the Rus-
sian Academy of Sciences, the Russian Academy of
Medical Sciences, the Russian Academy of Agricultural
Sciences, and other sectoral academies of sciences and,
during the second quarter of 1995, resolve in accordance
with prescribed procedure questions pertaining to:

the allocation of hard currency for the patenting and
promotion of Russian technological developments
abroad;

the allocation of hard currency for the payment of
arrears and the payment of the 1995 dues to interna-
tional scientific organizations and for the procurement
of scientific and printing equipment, office equipment,
materials and reagents, and foreign scientific journals,
backup for maritime expeditions, and repairs to research
vessels;

b) The Russian Federation Ministry of the Economy, in
conjunction with the Russian Federation Ministry of
Science and Technical Policy, the Russian Academy of
Sciences, sectoral academies of sciences, and interested
federal executive organs, shall review the list of installa-
tions being built with centralized capital investment for
the Russian Federation’s state scientific centers and for
establishments of the Russian Academy of Sciences and
its regional branches, and, when the 1996 federal budget
is being drawn up, shall submit to the Russian Federa-
tion Government in accordance with prescribed proce-
dure proposals that investment resources be concen-
trated on the completion of installations which are still

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 25

needed and which are scheduled for commissioning in
1996- 1997 and on the construction of social installa-
tions on a shared basis with funds provided by local
budgets and citizens and enlisting federal budget funds;

c) The Russian Federation Ministry of Science and
Technical Policy shall, when allocating budget appropri-
ations in 1995 to ministries and departments—the state
customers for the federal targeted programs adopted by
the Russian Federation Government for funding—take
into consideration the expenditure on research and
development work included in the aforesaid programs
within the bounds of the 1995 federal budget funds
allocated to these ministries and departments for the
implementation of scientific studies, which should be
channeled by ministries and departments primarily into
the implementation of the aforesaid work.

2. The appended plan of measures for 1995 for the
provision of state support for the development of science
and scientific and technical developments (not pub-
lished) shall be ratified.

3. With a view to preventing the further destruction of
the country’s scientific and technical potential and cre-
ating the conditions for the full-fledged development of
fundamental studies and top-priority avenues of science
and technology, the Russian Federation Ministry of
Finance, the Russian Federation Ministry of the
Economy, and the Russian Federation Ministry of Sci-
ence and Technical Policy shall review in the 1996 draft
federal budget appropriations for the funding of civilian
research and development work amounting to at leasi 3
percent of annual budget expenditure with this propor-
tion being increased in subsequent years.

4. It shall be prescribed that the top-priority avenues for
the development of science and technology, as well as the
list of critical federal-level technologies, are ratified by
the Government Commission for Scientific and Tech-
nical Policy.

5. With a view to ensuring the reorganization of the
scientific and technical sphere during the first half of
1995, federal executive organs, the Russian Academy of
Sciences, and sectoral academies of sciences shall:

analyze the network of scientific organizations working
in the interests of the corresponding sectors, determine
in accordance with prescribed procedure the optimum
organizational-legal forms for scientific organizations
under their jurisdiction and the main avenues of their
activity, and submit the results of this work to the
Russian Federation Ministry of Science and Technical
Policy;

complete the work of drawing up the list of scientific and
technical installations subject to reorganization in accor-
dance with the procedure envisaged by Russian Federa-
tion Government decree No. 870 of 26 July 1994 “On
the Privatization of Scientific and Technical Installa-
tions’’ (SOBRANIYE ZAKONODATELSTVA
ROSSIYSKOY FEDERATSII, 1994, No. 15, p 1783).

The Russian Federation Ministry of Science and Tech-
nical Policy shall inform federal executive organs of the
methods used to conduct the analysis of the network of
scientific organizations, sum up the results during the
third quarter of 1995, and take steps by the end of 1995
to draw up a state register of scientific organizations.

6. The Russian Federation Ministry of Science and

Grounds for ‘Optimism’ Seen in Decree on

MM2804085495 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in

{[Unattributed account of remarks by Vsevolod
Medvedev, corresponding member and deputy chief
academic secretary of the Russian Academy of Sciences,
carried under the ““Commentary” rubric and the general
heading: “Urgent Assistance for Science”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] Recent efforts have yielded pos-
itive results, he said. It cannot now be claimed that
Russian science is in a state of collapse. It has achieve-
ments to its name. These were cited at the Russian
Academy of Sciences annual meeting recently. Our sci-
entists have produced interesting results in a whole
number of areas, although the alarming situation
regarding the delay in transforming scientific ideas into
actual deeds is undeniable.

Vsevolod Medvedev also reported the following inter-
esting facts: The exodus of Russian scientists abroad is

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

slowing down and, what’s more, the number of those
wishing to do postgraduate study has increased this year.
Young people have begun to realize that brain rather
than brawn is the chief route to achieving anything in
life.

However, a lot has to be done to ensure more effective
use is made of the country’s scientific potential, in the
chief academic secretary’s opinion. One problem is that
often the results achieved in the laboratories of institutes
are not further exploited. Sometimes this happens for the
mundane reason of lack of information.

According to Vsevolod Medvedev, the steps taken by the
country’s leadership give grounds for the scientific com-
munity to look to the future with optimism. The latest
Russian Federation Government decree is further con-
firmation of this. The envisaged appropriation—as of
next year—of 3 percent of federal budget expenditure
will make the existence of scientific collectives more
assured. Certain concessions are also being granted:
Customs duties on instruments and equipment tempo-
rarily imported or exported for the purpose of scientific
research are being removed, and charitable assistance
given to Russian science by foreign foundations and
organizations is exempted from tax. Measures are being
taken to ensure that scientists’ wages are no lower than
the average wage in the national economy.

However, fears are being expressed in the scientific
world: After all, various promises were made in the past,
but they have not been met. For example, the normal
sums of money were apparently allocated last year, but
only 55 percent of the statutory amount was eventually
obtained. We would not want any repetition of that.

Media Issues

New Agency Seen as Censorship Committee

MM2804092295 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 27 Apr 95 First Edition pp 1-2

[Article by Vladimir Verin: “Censorship Committee Is
Being Created....”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] On the evening of 24 April on
Russian television in the program “without retouching”
presenter S. Torchinskiy made public a curious docu-
ment—a Russian Federation presidential draft edict on
the formation of a new department—a kind of ministry
of propaganda. The journalist had an even sharper name
for this administrative invention —“the department of
propaganda under the CPSU Central Committee.” This
draft is indeed so unique that it is worth publishing in
full. [There follows text of draft edict]

On the formation of the Russian Federation United
Federal Agency for state information policy (Gosinform)

With a view to ensuring state support for the mass
media, strengthening the legal protection of the mass
media and the rights of workers in television, radio

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

broadcasting, the press, and other mass media, and
putting in order the registration and licensing of the mass
media, in accordance with article 80 of the Russian
Federation Constitution I decree:

1. That the Russian Federation United Federal Agency
for State Information Policy (Gosinform) be created on
the basis of the Russian Federation Committee for the
Press and the Russian Federal Service for Television and
Radio Broadcasting.

That the relevant changes be made to the structure of
federal organs of executive power approved by the
Russian Federation President’s 10 January 1994 edict
no. 66 bearing in mind that the Gosinform is a federal
organ of executive power subordinate to the Russian
Federation president on questions assigned to it by the
Russian Federation Constitution.

2. That the Gosinform be assigned the implementation
of state policy in the sphere of the mass media, the
monitoring of the observation of legislation on the mass
media, including questions of technical inspection, pre-
ventive work, and the halting of law violations in this
sphere and the registration and licensing of the mass
media.

3. That it be established that:

The service for television and radio broadcasting and the
committee for the press join Gosinform as its main
administrations;

each of the above structural subdivisions of Gosinform is
headed by a director, who is ex officio first deputy
general director of Gosinform.

4. That it be established that the leaders of regional
(interregional) subdivisions of Gosinform are appointed
to their posts by the general director of Gosinform.

5. That the Russian Federation state committee for the
administration of state property be given within a week
for the Gosinform the buildings at the following Moscow
addresses: No. 12 Kachalov Street, no. 5 Strastnoy
Boulevard, and no. 25 Pyatnitskaya Street wit’ all the
equipment, furniture, organizational equipment, and so
forth there as of | January 1995 to accommodate the
central apparatus and services of Gosinform.

6. That Sergey Petrovich Gryzunov be appointed Gos-
inform general director.

7. That the following be appointed Gosinform first
deputy general directors:

Sergey Pavlovich Luknitskiy, director of the service for
radio and television broadcasting;

Aleksey Borisovich Morgun, director of the Committee
for the Press.

8. That the Gosinform general director submit within
two weeks for approval a statute on the Russian Feder-
ation united federal agency for state information policy
(Gosinform).

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 27

9. That the Russian Federation Government bring its
decisions into line with this edict.

10. This edict enters into force from the moment it is
signed. [text of edict ends]

And so having made public this document, S. Torchin-
skiy asked a program guest, Vsevolod Bogdanov,
chairman of the Russian Journalists Union, to comment
on it. And here there was instantly exposed the author of
what V. Bogdanov called the latest “attempt to lay hands
on the press’”—S. Gryzunov, chairman of the Russian
Committee for the Press.

It must be said that the draft for the new agitation and
propaganda department delighted virtually none of the
journalists present. Only a journalist from
ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI, revealing some knowledge of
the content of the document which had just been
described, tried to argue that since Mr. Gryzunov is
involved in the draft and is planning to be minister of
propaganda, then everything will be fine. He said he
could not imagine S. Gryzunov telephoning some chief
editor and asking or demanding that he publish some-
thing or not print something. There followed a highly
characteristic reply from V. Bogdanov:

“Of course, the sacred duty of the presidential news-
paper is to support this new presidential department...
But Gryzunov will not only telephone, he is already
telephoning today to almost all the chief editors and

trying to guide them.”’ (The chief editor of
ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA is not among their number).

And so we have the latest attempt to create a large
propaganda apparatus designed to ensure tough control
over the mass media. It has come to our knowledge that
S. Filatov, leader of the Russian Federation president’s
staff, has rendered great assistance to the project for the
agitation and propaganda department named for S.
Gryzunov. In an analytical note accompanying the text
and signed by him he wrote: Analysis shows that the
balance of information sources aimed at the mass audi-
ence is taking shape by no means in favor of the Russian
president. Actually, apart from the Information Admin-
istration (the formula for whose activity on its first
encounter with reality has fully shown its unsoundness)
the president has nothing. As for ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI
then under pressure from the State Duma committee for
information policy and communications (M. N. Polto-
ranin) like many other publications it has been deprived
of its state subsidy and naturally will be obliged to define
a new formula for its survival.“

It emerged that Messrs. Filatov and Gryzunov have little
concern for the grave situation which has taken place in
important sectors like television and radio broadcasting
or Russia’s publishing and printing complex. They are
not alarmed by the fact that state television is faced with
a choice—either reducing broadcasting for the summer
months or stopping it completely in the fall of this year.
They are not perturbed by the fact that the catastrophic

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

28 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

situation with regard to the Russian printing industry
could leave schoolchildren without textbooks by | Sep-
tember. As the analytical memorandum states, it is far
more important that “there is no coordination of the
activity of the state mass media and Russia’s propaganda
activity has declined drastically.”

Yet again bureaucrats are trying to convince the coun-
try’s leadership that they are the ones who can ensure the
political prosperity of the country and people. And what
they want is very trivial—unrestricted power over the
mass media, a separate ministry, and a colossal staff.

But the most curious thing is that all this is being
demanded by Mr. Gryzunov, who has already proved
throughout the period of the imposition of constitutional
order in Chechnya, his total inability not only to explain
the position of the president and government and not
only to defend it but even to organize even halfway
reasonably information backup for the operations the
state carries out.

Incidentally, S. Gryzunov left Chechnya at the most
troubled time because he had “throat trouble.” In gen-
eral this is called desertion. In particular they wanted to
dismiss S. Gryzunov for it. But later they obviously
decided to promote him.

So Gryzunov’s team, which calls itself democratic, is
proposing to create nothing other than a censorship
committe. After all, everything has already been created
for imp! ‘menting the Russian leadership's policy—state
television and siate newspapers, the information backup
administration and the press service of the Russian
president and government. Let everyone do their job,
and after all some people are doing it!

S Gryzunov should also do his job as chairman of the
Russian Committee for the Press: engage in organizing
the publication of textbooks, books, and school work-
books and exercise books. But that is probably beyond
him: The press is full of alarming articles on blunders in
this important area....

So draft edicts are compiled in which the functions of
chief ideological controller and chief ideological “hard-
hitter” are assigned to Gryzunov. Well, some people
never want to do humdrum work.... You can put the
banner of democracy in one of their hands and the
censor’s stamp they long for in the other.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

The pressing need for this decision is obvious—it is
sometimes hard even for ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA,
founded by the government itself, to clarify any details,
never mind other journalists. That is why the new
structure has been set up on the basis of the govern-
ment’s press service, whose activity, as a recent inspec-
tion showed, was not only ineffective but also did not
entirely match its status as a government organ.

It is incidentally worthy of note that the leader of the new
directorate has not as yet been appointed. This con-
vinces us that it is not being tailored to a specific person
as was previously the case but is being set up to perform
the task of providing objective, full inass media coverage
of the Cabinet of Ministers’ activity.

Military, Nuclear & Space Issues

Lebed Suggested as Army Inspector-General

MM72804133295 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI in
Russian 28 Apr 95 p 2

[Article by Vladimir Guliyev: “So Far the Swan Song
Has Not Become a Hawkish Squawk’’]

{[FBIS Translated Text] The fates have decreed that
Lieutenant General Aleksandr Lebed, commander of the
14th Army stationed in the Dniester Region, is increas-
ingly being embroiled in the political maelstrom. He said
that he intends to quit his post as commander of the 1 4th
Russian Army owing to the upcoming reorganization of
the command. Gen. Lebed stressed that the planned cuis
in the 14th Army command cannot be carried out until
all conditions have been created for the implementation
of the Moldovan-Russian agreement on the Russian
troop withdrawal.

“I regrettably learned of the decision to reform the
army’s staff from the press,” the general said. “Hitherto
none of my leadership has told me about this nor has
anyone offered me any post.”

Colonel Nikolay Malyshev, chief of the Russian Ground
Forces Press Center, said during a conversation with
your ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI correspondent that the
Russian defense minister decided to reform the staff of
the 14th Russian Army. He explained: “This decision is
due to the fact that the overall numerical strength of the
14th Army is verging on that of a reduced-strength
division owing to the consistent cuts in the army,
whereas the numerical strength of the army's staff—
these changes notwithstanding—has remained virtually
unchanged at over 200 men.”

There would be nothing terrible in this had Aleksandr
Lebed decided from the very outset to make a career of
politics, abandon military service, and get involved in
the ups-and-downs of party politics and parliamentary
elections.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

Nowadays people are increasingly frequently trying to
disprove the well-known maxim of any civilized demo-
cratic state that the army is outside politics. But it has
long been known that the armed forces as a special
instrument of the state are created precisely in order to
perform political functions, admittedly using special
means—armed struggle. Needless to say the army and
the navy were and remain special instruments for the
implementation of policy, nothing more than that. But
the policy of the state as a whole. The army must not be
embroiled in formulating, much less implementing,
party policy within the state or become the means of
securing a particular party’s or political movement’s
objectives. It is particularly dangerous when the armed
forces themselves become a kind of quasi- party and
fight for power, which in this case risks becoming
nothing but a military dictatorship. That scenario is
completely precluded by the Constitution and legislation
of any rule-of-law state.

But let us return to the hero of our piece. Aleksandr
Lebed, who has found himself the focus of public atten-
tion and is developing a taste for it, has increasingly
boldly overstepped the dividing line between army life
and politics. According to the latest press reports,
Aleksandr Ivanovich has been invited to head an influ-
ential patriotic and pro-state [derzhavnyy] political
movement. But an opposition general ard politician who
has not foresworn the military life is a “candidate
Napoleon,” a constitutional usurper of power. And any
dictatorship is contrary to the constitutional system, be
it the rule of the most enlightened, personally honest,
and intelligent officer.

In the public imagination he is an honest, intelligent, and
unsullied professional army man, a bold defender of a
foreign state’s Russian-speaking citizens. In actual fact
Gen. Lebed has long since deserved more credit than
being the commander of a troop formation of several
thousand men, known as an army out of politeness. But
is there a vacancy for Lebed? | am afraid that there is
not. District commander is no big deal. Even now the
14th Army is in the position of a military district, as it
were (only abroad). What about deputy minister? The
Defense Ministry has loads of them without him coming
along. Furthermore, A. Lebed personally and profession-
ally is less of a colleague and subordinate than a rival for
the incumbent defense minister.

Aleksandr Ivanovich is a born military inspector. That is
a post which is particularly lacking at the Presidential
Staff and the Government apparatus. Inspector general
of the Armed Forces, in other words someone who has
the right and the duty of inspecting everything, but
commanding nothing—in our view that post is equally
beneficial to both the supreme state leadership and the
Armed Forces themselves and appropriate for the kind
of “high-caliber individual” that Aleksandr Ivanovich
Lebed undoubtedly is.

Working outside the Defense Ministry and the General
Staff, an inspector general would be an independent,

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 29

extradepartmental “master’s eye,” which is what is
lacking today in the supreme state leadership and the
Russian Armed Forces themselves.

In short we suggest that the post of Inspector General of
the Russian Armed Forces be instituted, subordinate to
the chairman of the Federation Council, the President of
the Russian Federation, and directly to an official within
the apparatus of the Service of the head of state’s aides or
in the apparatus of the Russian president’s Security
Council. The most suitable person for this job is Lt. Gen.
Aleksandr Ivanovich Lebed. Give him a lofty post before
his swan song becomes a hawkish scream.

As you know, Russian Defense Minister Pavel Grachev
offered the commander of the 14th Russian Army the
post of deputy commander of the Transbaykal Military
District for combat training. Commenting on Aleksandr
Lebed’s stance, Pavel Grachev said the following:
“*... Judging by his political statements and involvement
in various congresses, Aleksandr Ivanovich has of late
had a penchant for politics.... The ministry leadership
has noticed this and the army commander has been
asked to abandon this activity but he has not as yet
heeded our suggestion. I do not think that a strong
politician can be made of A. Lebed.”

General Lebed on Chechnya, NATO, Media

[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow-Novosibirsk—This
lieutenant general of the Russian Army does not need an
introduction: He is probably the most popular man in
uniform today. Popular among both the military and

were statements about a goat and carrots, unflattering
opinions of top officials in the Russian leadership. People
got to know Lebed, and, having gotten to know him, many
wanted to see in him as the future president of Russia.

Lebed gave interviews and talked about his position with
journalists, but did not declare it publicly. He did it at the

may be considered a program statement (keeping in mind
that none of the mass media in the country published its
text, we decided to publish it in VECHERNIY

the general is interesting to journalists not only by his
official statements. Therefore, most of the 200 journalists
accredited at the congress plus interested delegates liter-

after saying “I have no time for newspapers,” at the same

{[Kuzmenkin] Aleksandr Ivanovich, how do you know

[Lebed] I will try to publish a book within the next two

everything in it. It is hard to answer this question in two

{Kuzmenkin] You came up with a very unexpected idea

II and Lenin simultaneously. The reaction was, I think,

[Lebed] Any concept and idea goes through three stages
in its development. The first stage: This is baloney. At
first it is perceived with irritation, and then with an
afterthought: There is something in it... There are the
whites and there are the reds—no matter how they call
themselves, they are still the whites and the reds. This
division has never resulted in any good, and never will.

{[Kuzmenkin] Elections are coming soon. There is a lot of

[Lebed] I have not heard a more idiotic question for a

[Kuzmenkin] Many people hold a grudge against Gen-

[Lebed] People on the same team were quarreling over
power. None of them got hurt. The leaders spent some
time in a cooler, then they forgave each other and were
released. While the small fry were buried in political
games.

{Kuzmenkin] You took part in the work of the Congress
of Russian Communities. What are your impressions?

[Lebed] Generally, more positive than negative. Positive
because it took place; negative because | have certain
criticisms—probably because of my disagreeable char-
acter. But the important part is that now we have a
guiding star: where to go.

[Kuzmenkin] Why did you come to this movement of all
places?

[Lebed] Where else?

{[Kuzmenkin] You had many offers.

[Lebed] Yeah, I did. I have no liking for or interest in
their platforms, programs, and views. Russians have
gathered here: If I stand up—with my face—and start
saying that I am a Jew or Georgian, nobody will not
believe me...

{[Kuzmenkin] Does the congress have a future?

[Lebed] It does. How can it not have one? It is a
movement behind which there are 124 million Russians
on the territory of Russia alone and 25 million outside it.
One hundred fifty million people!

{[Kuzmenkin] But Aleksandr Rutskoy’s Derzhava

[Lebed] Good luck to this calf in saving our wolf. We will

and ask: “Well, pundits! Explain what it is that I have
done!”

{[Kuzmenkin] You were asked here whether your service

[Lebed] A hare married an elephant and became

{[Kuzmenkin]} They complain about you.

[Lebed] No consequences. It was clear from the very

{[Kuzmenkin] But the Dniester Republic has not been

will soon be five years since this republic was formed. As
I recall it, Soviet Russia was not recognized until 1932.
They did not recognize it for a long time, and then gave
up and recognized it. This is a fact, reality.

have to begin with simple things, the simplest—the

they serve in the army. They got there as a result of the

and divisions were being dissolved, and the personnel

This is the way the 14th Army was manned: More than

[Kuzmenkin] Is NATO a threat to Russia today?
[Lebed] Of course, how else?...

am surprised: Why did they not put Bush on the foun-
dation of the Dzerzhinskiy monument and write on it:
“To the Winner in the Cold War.” He would look good
there. He did win it—everybody agreed with this. So why
now? Confrontation with who? Against who?!

[Kuzmenkin] Why is all this needed?

[Lebed] Territory is needed, to fulfill certain functions. It
is large—one-eighth of the world. There is a lot of
everything in it—just pump it out! There are 150 million
cattle on it. This is what they want, but they will not get
it. You know, the Russians have this gesture...

[Kuzmenkin] Your position on Chechnya.

[Lebed] I have already said: There is a slaughter taking
place there. We have not used either political or eco-
nomic measures. They nurtured this regime, and now
they all jumped to beat up on it. My position is that you
only need to hit twice: Once on the head and the second
time on the lid of the coffin. This is the correct way to
organize things.

{[Kuzmenkin] There is a rumor that General Lebed’s
position has changed.

[Lebed] Of course it has changed. Spring is coming: The
shrubs and trees are greening. Shali was surrendered
calmly. A sluggish, endless war will begin. There will be
10-12 dead every day—this is not too many, mothers will
bear more. But this will be every day: every 10 days—
120; every 100 days—1,200. Corpses of people and
animals under the rubble will start talking. They will be
talking the language of hepatitis, the plague, and cholera.
We have glorious prospects: Tomorrow waves of it will
spread all over Russia, carried by refugees, relatives,
rotating military servicemen. So, of course the position
changed: Spring dictates its conditions.

[Kuzmenkin] And from the viewpoint of a military man?

[Lebed] What I said was said from the standpoint of a
military man. We have to prepare; we need to learn the
military art properly. The army’s structure should be: six
fighting men and four service personnel. This is the
proportion! You get him up in the middle of the night—
before he opens his eyes—and he ought to jump into the
turret, shoot, and hit the target. To do this, you have to
train 22 days a month. This will be a military, thank
God! They will crush anybody.

[Kuzmenkin] How do you feel about the idea of
increasing the service term?

[Lebed] It is easy to slacken the reins and hard to pull
them tight again. In 1990 everybody was applauding:
Oh! We exempted students. The intellectual levei in the
military has dropped sharply, and the number of soldiers
also has declined sharply. We have dumped it all on
semi-literates and illiterates. And now, when it finally
began to get through that the military and the country are
directly dependent on it... Can you imagine, for instance,
some Afghanistan in 1979-1980 to dare to attack the
Soviet Union? You cannot imagine it, right?! But now
they are trying!

[Kuzmenkin] How about a professional military then?

[Lebed] Russia will never have a professional military.
We do not and will not have the money for it. There are
only two professional militaries in the world: the British
and the American. The Americans spent 13 years
building it after Vietnam, and put 17 social programs
under it. We cannot pull this through, and therefore,
whether we want it or not, we will have to man it with
draftees. How to use them is another matter! This is what

Stepashin: Foreign Intelligence Targets Defense

LD2504215495 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1955
GMT 25 Apr 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] NIZHNY NOVGOROD, April
25 (Interfax-Eurasia)}—Chief of the Federal Counterin-
telligence (FSB) Sergey Stepashin says the country’s
defense sector remains in the focus of foreign intelli-
gence. He was speaking at a meeting by FSB senior
officials in Nizhny Novgorod on Tuesday.

The meeting discussed how to tighten security at the
country’s nuclear facilities and military-industrial com-
plex.

Stepashin said that the area of interests by foreign
intelligence covers Russia’s shipbuilding (submarines
and warships), aerospace technologies, mass destruction
weapons and prototypes of conventional weapons, tanks
and tank protection and weapons based on new princi-
ples of physics.

Growing threats of nuclear sabotage was also on the
agenda, members of the FSB’s local branch told Interfax.
Senior FSB officials approved a range of measures aimed
to tighten security at the defense-related nuclear facilities
and prevent the divulgence of state secrets and sabotage
or terrorist attacks.

— Fleet Feature Shows ‘Super-Modern’

MM2504090995 Russian Public Television First
Channel Network in Russian 0730 GMT 16 Apr 95

[From the “Peleng Alpha” program: Video report by Igor
Prokopenko, identified by caption; figures in brackets
denote broadcast time in GMT in hours, minutes, and
seconds]

[FBIS Translated Text] [Prokopenko over video of
recent Northern Fleet exercises} Russia’s Navy has been
guarding tue state’s maritime borders for almost 300
years. Its history began in the Baltic and the Black Seas
and continued 75 years ago also in the North. Since that
time Northern Fleet surface ships and submarines have
constituted a most important part of our country’s
defense potential at sea.

In recent years, following the disintegration of the USSR
and the loss of positions in the Black and Baltic Seas, the
Northern Fleet has acquired special strategic impor-
tance. It is no accident that NATO country submarines
are increasingly patrolling this area in particular.

What you see here is rare footage. A U.S. nuclear
submarine in Arctic waters. [video shows conning tower
emerging through Arctic ice and what appears to be the
interior of a U.S. submarine] Despite expanding cooper-
ation and confidence-building measures, series produc-
tion of the latest multi-role nuclear submarines, espe-
cially adapted for operation in Arctic conditions, will
begin in the United States next year. Can we, in turn,
create the necessary preconditions to ensure that the
Northern Fleet serves as a sufficient nuclear deterrent?

This year the ships of the Northern Fleet put to sea on 60
occasions. Despite pessimistic forecasts, the fleet is
coping with the tasks it has been set. But at what cost to
the sailors? The pride and beauty of the Navy, the
nuclear missile cruiser Admiral Ushakov, has been laid
up for the past four years, awaiting an overhaul. Dozens
of other warships share the same fate. The reason is
banal—lack of money. Nonetheless, the main problem
lies elsewhere.

[Andrey Kokoshin, Russian Federation first deputy
defense minister, identified by caption] The main
problem is connected with the nuclear safety of decom-
missioned submarines. This is our main priority.

[Prokopenko over video of moored submarines] Under
the terms of the START I Treaty 126 nuclear submarines
have been decommissioned. They all have to be disman-
tled and recycled.

[Prokopenko to camera] At the moment the problem of
dismantling warships which have reached the end of
their service life is just as acute for the Navy as the
problem of obtaining new ships.

Meanwhile the process of dismantling ships is no less
expensive than building new ones. To date only 11

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

submarines have been broken up; 13 are in the process of
being dismantled. The rest are awaiting their turn. The
disused submarines moored at the quayside demand no
less attention and expenditure than operational ones.
And what is more, they are a heightened radiation
hazard. This is what a submarine which is being disman-
tled looks like. Where you see the wire mesh, nuclear

{Rear Admiral Shcherbak] Having done their duty, just

This is Rear Admiral Shcherbak. He commanded this

[Prokopenko] One day history will acurately assess the
price of the demise of these submarines. Today we are
forced to buila new ones. What you see is unique footage
of a super-modern nuclear submarine which will become
the flagship, probably not just of the Northern Fleet, in
the 21st century. [video shows brief footage of submarine
with tube covers open]

The outpost on our northern maritime border is still a
force which cannot be ignored. The heightened interest
sh -vn by foreign special services in the latest large-scale
exercises of the Northern Fleet was not fortuitous. The
exercise area was hemmed in by submarines and spy
planes. Thus the Northern Fleet’s standard of training
was being assessed not just by the command. The other
side was also able to see for itself that the Russian Navy
is staffed with high-class professionals.

However, we must not forget that the future might of the
Russian Navy depends directly on the solution of its
current problems. [075143] [video shows naval ships
during recent exercise; conning tower of U.S. submarine
emerging through ice; submarines and warships in port;
Kokoshin clip; submarines being dismantled; Shcherbak
interview; brief “unique footage” of super-modern sub-
marine under construction; more exercise footage]

Well, neither the deputy minister nor the minister him-
self are miracle workers, and they cannot turn figures on
paper into housing for servicemen.

Decree on Russian Securities Market Assessed

MM2504135195 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 21 Apr 95 First Edition p 3

{Commentary by Aleksandr Velichenkov under the
rubric “Foreword to Document”’: “Housing Certificate”’}

[FBIS Translated Text] The Russian Federation Govern-
ment Decree “On Measures To Develop the Securities
Market in the Russian Federation” outlines approaches
to certain pressing questions which have become espe-
cially topical in recent times.

Numerous conflicts at the annual shareholder meetings
of joint- stock companies of privatized enterprises (even
including hunger strikes by shareholders) connected with
the keeping of registers of shareholders, the counting of
votes, and the procedures for adopting decisions—

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

including on a secondary issue of shares—have
impressed upon the government the need to regulate
these questions. This applies equally to the development
and regulation of the securities market. The projected
measures, which include the creation of a Russian stock-
market telecommunications system, will undoubtedly
make our stock market more civilized.

The clause on the participation of nonresidents in the
acquisition of shares issued by Russian enterprises gives
rise to certain questions. There are doubts that the
Federal Commission for Securities and the Stock Market
is up to resolving this issue. After all, someone becomes
a nonresident precisely so as not to carry out economic
activity on Russian territory. To allow nonresidents only
to buy shares is economically senseless; a situation could
arise at any time whereby a nonresident winds down
operations in Russia—what is he then to do with the
shares? They are not quoted in other countries. Conse-
quently, nonresidents must be permitted to sell shares as
well. And this is already economic activity and the
derivation of profit. Therefore, a nonresident must be
registered as a Russian juristic person. But then the
problem needs examining at a higher level.

All the signs suggest that regional, oblast, municipal, and
so forth, loans, bonds, and housing certificates will soon
be given a legal basis. This will affect above all the
taxation of securities.

Concerning the problems of post-voucher privatization,
the decree chimes in with previously adopted docu-
ments. At the same time the thesis on the implementa-
tion of “‘measures for replacing share deposition certifi-
cates with registered shares” could invite different
interpretations. For example, does this mean an end to
the circulation of securities in the name of the bearer?
Bearing in mind that the introduction in our country of
a mechanism based on the circulation not of shares, but
share deposition certificates (so-called ADR’s [American
depository receipts] in U.S. stock- market practice) is
being actively discussed, can one consider that our stock
market will be deprived of this promising mechanism?

individuals sentenced for crimes of negligence for terms

criminal cases which are in the investigation stage.

Duma Geopolitics Committee’s Gusman on
s

MK2704105095 Moscow KURANTY in Russian
27 Apr 95 p 3

[Interview with Yuliy Gusman by Yan Ulanskiy: “Geo-
politics Is a Science, Not a Military Doctrine”’]

[FBIS Translated Excerpt] The State Duma Committee
on Geopolitical Questions was created on Vladimir
Zhirinovskiy’s initiative and became composed mainly
of LDPR [Liberal Democratic Party of Russia] faction
members. But despite the numerous forecasts, the com-
mittee has not become a generator of nonsensical ideas.
What is more, its chairman Viktor Ustinov recently
abandoned the Liberal Democrats’ camp to join the new
“Stability” faction. Our correspondent talks with Yuliy
Gusman, deputy chairman of the committee, about on
what and how the Duma geopoliticians worked.

[Ulanskiy] What did the committee manage to do during
its one year of work?

{Gusman] Four draft laws developed by us were passed.
In their number are the laws on the border and the days
of military glory. Ready for first reading is a draft law on
the continental shelf. [passages omitted]

[Ulanskiy] What is geopolitics after all?

{Gusman] It can be defined briefly as political interac-
tion of states and groups of states depending on their
position on the map and on historical peculiarities. For
example, our geopolitical position obviously changed in
the wake of the USSR’s disintegration. [passage omitted]

[Ulanskiy] Whose objective is it to develop a geopolitical
concept of the state?

{[Gusman] Unfortunately, no one here is developing the
state’s geopolitical concept. Geopolitics is only seen as a
science and it is virtually not taken into account in real
politics. Such a concept is being partially developed in
our committee and in the Foreign Ministry. But, pos-
sibly, it should be set forth point by point and promul-
gated like, say, a military doctrine would. [passage
omitted]

(Ulanskiy] There must be some crimps in the workings
of the LDPR now. People are leaving the faction. Take
your committee chairman, who also quit.

[Gusman] I do not know the details of relations between
them, but there are many deputies in the LDPR who do
not share their chief's “liberal-democratic” beliefs. More
people could have left the faction, but some stay on

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 35

having an ax to grind; others think it unethical to leave a
sinking ship, still others are simply afraid. But LDPR
members still are in the majority in our committee. I
repeat, however, that there exists among us mutual
respect for views and stands. My amendments, in par-
ticular to the draft law on military service, were taken
into account. But I did not support it on the whole.
Although I fully realize that reforms in the army are of
vital necessity, a moral audit, say, should be conducted.
For example, you can read in the papers about hungry
conscripts in Salekhard or in Kursk and also right there
and then about generals’ enormous dachas worth
$300,000 to $500,000. Why not sell those very dachas
and feed the conscripts? You mustn’t think I am against
generals’ having dachas, but one would like to know with
what money were they built? And, naturally, everyone
would like the Russian Army to look as good as did the
English on the Falklands, or the Americans in Iraq. If
one is to allocate money to the army at all, it should go
for a radical reequipment, for electronic equipment, for
expanding contract-based service. It is stupid to sink
trillions into maintaining a huge, unwieldy and obsolete
military machine which it currently is.

(Ulanskiy] Let us go back to your committee.

[Gusman] The committee mostly has a stand completely
different from the stand of our “‘Russia’s Choice” faction
or from my own. But work on laws, at the same time, is
proceeding normally. There is an enormous gulf between
the extreme views, slogans, and real policy. It is one
thing to utter “the last dash to the South” [Zhirinovskiy’s
autobiography] and quite another to attack India. It’s
like the saying: It [an elephant] can eat [a hundred kilos
of bananas], but who'd let it?

Ministry on Search for U.S. Citizen

LD2704140495 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1340

[By Olga Semenova]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow, April 27 (Itar-
Tass)—Russian Interior Minister Viktor Yerin gave a

the criminal investigation department and interior units
stationed in Chechnya in connection with the disappear-
ance in the zone of combat actions on the territory of the
Chechen Republic of American citizen Fred Cuny and
Russian citizens who accompanied him, head of the
public relations center of the Russian Interior ministry,
Vladimir Vorozhtsov, told Itar-Tass here today.

Unfortunately, he said, the search has been made more
difficult because American officials informed the Inte-
rior ministry bodies too late.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

36 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

American representatives should have reported on Fred
Cuny’s disappearance to interior bodies immediately but
not to new media as under Russian legislation just as in
any other country, militia will not start searching for a
disappeared person without an official inquiry, Vladimir
Vorozhtsov stressed. [sentence as received]

Units of interior troops, special detachments of police
and quick reaction forces on the struggle against orga-
nized crime have been taking all necessary measures to
establish the location of Fred Cuny and his Russian
companions.

The Interior ministry representative also noted that Fred
Cuny had arrived in Chechnya actually illegally without
informing the command of the allied federal forces of his
intentions.

According to Vladimir Vorozhtsov, this is often the case
with members of different humanitarian missions and
journalists who thus subject themselves to additional
risks.

When journalists receive accreditation at the temporary
information center, and representatives of governmental
or public organizations notify the command of the
federal forces about their arrival in Chechnya, they are
provided with proper guard, the head of the public
relations center stressed. So far, none of them has
disappeared, he noted.

Grachev on Cease-Fire Implementation

MM2804092095 Moscow KRASNAYA ZVEZDA in
Russian 28 Apr 95 p 1

[Report by Oleg Viadykin: ‘Victory Parade-95:
Rehearsals Under Way”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] A second rehearsal for the mili-
tary parade to mark the 50th anniversary of victory in
the Great Patriotic War took place on Poklonnaya Gora
late Wednesday evening. This time it was held according
to the fullest possible program for this event, including
an inspection of the troops by the Russian Federation
minister of defense himself and precise timing of the
whole parade operation.

Assessing the results of the rehearsal, Army General
Pavel Grachev told the invited journalists that he would
be acting against his conscience if he voiced satisfaction
with the parade team’s preparation. But there is still time
to eliminate the shortcomings, the minister emphasized.

In the presence of the mass media’s representatives, as
usual, Army Gen. Grachev explained the positions of the
Russian Federation Ministry of Defense on the most
topical issues at this time.

In particular, the Russian Federation president's edict
imposing a moratorium on combat operations in
Chechnya did not pass without comment. According to
the minister of defense, its implementation is realistic.
The edict has already been conveyed to the field com-
manders of the illegal armed formations. But this cer-
tainly does not mean that, if they open fire on federal
troops, there will not be a response to this.

In principle, it is possible to stop the conflict in
Chechnya by means of active intervention in its course
at the concluding stage, Army Gen. Grachev said. Prac-
tically all the field commanders wish to meet with the
Russian Federation minister of defense and discuss the
question of the final cessation of combat operations. He
has, in general, been set the task of examining the
possibility of such a meeting. Colonel General Anatoliy
Kvashnin, the minister’s plenipotentiary, is already
working in this direction.

Opposition Leader on Moratorium
LD2704233795

[FBIS Editorial Report] Moscow Radiostantsiya Ekho
Moskvy in Russian at 1540 GMT 27 Apr 95 broadcasts
a 20-minute studio interview with Doku Zavgayev,
former chairman of the Supreme Soviet of the Chechen-
Ingush Republic disbanded by Dzhokhar Dudayev and
now chairman of the Assembly of the Chechen People.
Reception is good.

Commenting on Yeltsin’s edict on the moratorium on
the use of force in Chechnya, he says:

“In my view, it was a wise decision by the president and
the government. Boris Nikolayevich Yeltsin himself
already spoke about it in his TV interview. This edict
was expected; people were waiting for a moratorium
decision. The most important thing now, and this
answers the aspirations not only of the Chechen popula-
tion itself but of all our country, is to ensure that this
moratorium does not end in a week or two as specified
but essentially becomes the beginning of an end to all
armed actions—not only in northern Caucasus. Let’s
hope to God that this moratorium should become a ban
on any use of force in our country. This is not only my
personal view, this is the view of many people I have
talked to.

“As for the situation in the Chechen Republic, the
moratorium will be a pre-condition for ending military
actions there.”

He goes on to reaffirm his stance opposing the use of
force in Chechnya:

“I still maintain the same position. If you had asked me
today, I would have given you the same answer. What
has happened and is happening on territory of the former
Chechen-Ingush Republic was not initiated by the
people who are, unfortunately, suffering and who have
fallen the first victim to the criminal regime created and
led by Dudayev and his accomplices.”

He goes on to say:

“I know very well that the president was constantly
looking for a peaceful solution to this problem. One
should be objective and honest here, what is true is true.
No matter what might happen, a peaceful solution to the
problem was sought. Whether all the methods, whether
all the possibilities were exhausted is another matter.”

He continues:

“It seems to me that the current resistance of the field
commanders is mainly caused by the fact that those who
allegedly offer them a cease-fire and arms surrender do

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

38 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

not inspire their trust. They do not trust them. Naturally,
if the warring sides in the republic at present do not have
mutual trust, they will never agree on anything except to
continue what they have been doing lately. Therefore it
is necessary to count on new people, on people who enjoy
support of the local population, inter alia those whom
the field commanders trust. If these commanders are
offered a truce by a person who will keep his word and
whom they trust, I believe the moratorium announced by
President Boris Yeltsin will be observed.”

Zavgayev, however, does not name any names. He says
the Chechen people themselves should decide since they
know who is worth what.

Speaking of the objectives of the Chechen Assembly
congress held in Moscow on 26 April, he says its main
task was to disperse the negative image of the Chechen
people created by Dudayev’s regime in the last three
years. Another important task was “to assist in restoring
peace and civil accord in the Chechen Republic and
around it, to restore good-neighborly relations not only
on a human level but to restore economic ties; in other
words, to join normal political, economic, and cultural
life of the rest of Russia.”
