Dudayev Explains Chechen Cause

AU2804115195 Kiev HOLOS UKRAYINY in Ukrainian
22 Apr 95 p 10

{Interview with Chechen leader Dzhokhar Dudayev by
Lyudmyla Kokhanets in Chechnya; date not given: “I
Did Not Send Anybody Call-Up Papers”—first para-
graph published in a box]

[FBIS Translated Text] Recently, Sergey Stepashin, chief
of the [Russian] Federal Counterintelligence Service
[FSK] (presently Security Service), claimed through the
mass media: The whereabouts of Dzhokhar Dudayev,
the “bandit of the century,” are no secret for them and
they even know what the disfavored general has for

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 39

breakfast. I cannot of course compete with Mr. Stepa-
shin’s omniscient department: I have not been initiated
in the particulars of Dudayev’s morning menu. Still,
Dzhokhar Dudayev himself confessed to me that he does
not normally eat breakfast: He goes to bed before dawn
and, after a short sleep, can only have lunch. I met with
the president of the Chechen Republic of Ichkeria in his
homeland, where rivers of blood have been flowing for
the fifth month running. Somewhere high above our
heads, aircraft droned in the black abyss of the sky.
“Bombers,” explained the former fighter pilot, “they will
again ’work upon’ the mountain passes.” Disquieting
flashes of lightning flared up above the mountains.
General Dudayev himself started the interview amidst a
war environment. “How is Ukraine, what’s new there,
my dear friends? How is life in my beloved Poltava
Oblast?” he asked in Ukrainian with genuine interest.
Only after discussing Poltava news did we proceed to the
problems of Chechnya proper. To many people, the
general’s words may seem too sharp. However, I could
not temper them. It was precisely to present his point of
view, which one cannot hear through “Ostankino,” that
I made my way to Dudayev.

{[Kokhanets] Mr. President, the “establishment of con-
stitutional order” in your land has brought about great
bloodshed. The small Chechen nation is becoming still
smaller as the days pass. Do you believe that the war was
inevitable? Should both Russia and Chechnya perhaps
have been more compliant in their mutual relations?

[Dudayev] I do not think that the war could have been
avoided. It is not a matter of our diplomatic straightfor-
wardness. You must understand that Chechnya is only
an excuse. Russia is ill with a horrific disease: racism,
whose symptoms are extreme cruelty, insidiousness, and
lack of spiritual values. Everything that satanism has
accumulated over the entire history of this land, which is
full of sin, is presently manifested in Russia’s actions
against small Chechnya. We have felt the pernicious
breath of this disease for three centuries. Now, its
chronic stage has given way to its death pangs.... Believe
me, Chechnya’s troubles are nothing extraordinary; they
are no greater than in the case of other nations.

We have simply been subjected to the strongest pressure
and the most vexing blow. Here, the individual is being
destroyed in the cruellest way and his right to life is being
trampled upon.

This ideology is more dangerous that fascism, racism,
Nazism, or all the misanthropic ideologies put together.
If there ever existed a terrorist state, it is Russia. The
highest-level officials, starting from the president or
prime minister, are taking part in the aggression,
destruction, and genocide against Chechnya. Every-
where one can see lawlessness and the power is in the
hands of real adventurers. That is why they do every-
thing that they want.

Russia is the only country in the world that has no
spiritual values and no faith. Over the centuries, these

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

40 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

moral values have been beaten out of its unfortunate
citizens as unnecessary for the dominating nation. It was
only when they embarked upon campaigns against
adherents of other faiths that they yelled about faith.
When there is no faith, there are no moral values either.
These are military hordes, armed to the teeth with
supermodern weapons, that carry forth their satanism.
This is racism in action.

{[Kokhanets}] Do you believe that it is Russia that pro-
vokes all the interethnic conflicts?

[Dudayev] I am convinced of it; it is not my emotions,
but the facts. Unless we probe everything that goes on in
Russia and unless mankind takes measures to defend
itself, the planet will be scourged with bloody wounds.
The Chechens will survive, I have no doubt of this: Over
300 years, we have become used to living on such terms
with Russia and have developed immunity to racism.
But other nations will not survive.

Throughout its history, Russia has never come through
against a stronger or even an equal force. It always
selected the weakest and most vulnerable victims and
destroyed them to intimidate others. It fancies Nagorno-
Karabakh—there you go, Russia grabs and swallows it
up. It fancies Abkhazia, North Ossetia, Ingushetia, or the
Dniester Region—swallowed up they are. And again
Russia goes in circles in search of more game. Aha, here
is the Crimea. Harbor no illusions, the troops that are
engaged in “disarming bandit formations” in Chechnya
will turn to the Crimea. The cards of eastern and
southern Ukraine will then be actively played and
attempts will be made to have you clash head on and to
draw you into a fratricidal war.

Furthermore. Just see how Russia behaves in the inter-
national arena. As soon as Israel's relations with its Arab
neighbors improved and a constructive dialogue was
about to start, here they come—the Russian diplomats.
Where else is there a conflict? In former Yugoslavia? Let
us go there. Between Turks and Kurds? Lovely—Russian
politicians have something to do, all the more so since
they already have an trick up their sleeves, and a
specially created Kurdish Center is added to the sce-
nario. Who else needs to be “reconciled”? Who needs
peacekeeping forces?

Once the “Chechen mafia” is crushed, they will go about
creating a Slavic union with renewed vigor. This utopia
will inflict an ocean of misery upon you. Of course, the
wrapping is quite attractive, but how about the essence?
The essence is in enhancing the role of satanism after
Ukraine and Belarus are drawn into it. Do not let
yourself fall into that trap.

[Kokhanets] Even the most severe disease needs to be
treated and not simply diagnosed. Can you offer a
remedy for ridding Russia of its “racism”?

[Dudayev] The world must become a physician and not
a witness to the disease, a witness who bashfully looks

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

down: “This is Russia’s internal affair.” No, sirs, not
internal. Today, it is Chechnya, and tomorrow—one of
you. The whole world must see: Russia is an evil empire
and the question of its permanent membership of the
Security Council must be put on the agenda. Because, by
remaining within such an authoritative body, it only
discredits the validity of international legal cooperation.
Instead, the aggressor must be isolated from the rest of
the world with the help of economic sanctions. There are
methods to block the epidemic of racism. Unfortunately,
nobody is in a hurry to apply them.

{Kokhanets}] Despite Russia’s military might, it was
unable to turn this war into a blitzkrieg. It is already the
fifth month that combat actions have not abated. The
Russian troops are having to storm again the same towns
that have already been “‘liberated.”” Where does the
endurance of your military formations lie? As a matter of
fact, over the three years of its proclaimed independence,
the Chechen Republic has not been able to create its own
regular army.

[Dudayev] The phenomenon of endurance lies in the
Chechens’ spirit. Over all these 300 years, we have
always been ready to resist. The Chechens are intrinsi-
cally intolerant to slavery. We have not been created for
bondage. The Caucasian mountains may be leveled like
this table, but nobody will succeed in breaking the spirit
of the nation. Not by the most advanced weapons, or by
deportations, or through insidious political games.

With regard to our own armed forces, our people are the
army. I did not send anybody call-up papers. However,
after the aggressor deprived 300,000 people of their
homes and deprived them of means for subsistence, he
pushed us to the only possible action—fighting.

The Chechen factor has shown that the Russian military
operational style and combat tactics may be crossed out
and a new page opened. We have the key. War teaches
many things. This particularly applies to the Chechens.
Is there any other state in the world capable of with-
standing for almost five months the enormous military
force applied in the same direction and at the same time?
(5,600 armored vehicles were simultaneously brought
into an area of 11,700 square kilometers). We are getting
used to war. Our defensive tactics have so far been like
this: While Deynekin [Russian general] in the mountains
engages in launching missile and bomb strikes, we learn
the skills of fighting in the plains. Experience is gained
very fast. We now forbid our combatants in voluntary
suicide battalions to throw themselves at tanks, as was
the case at the beginning of the combat actions. If only
100 tanks with their crews are the target, only in that case
have you the right to risk your life. To rush under every
Russian tank—we do not have enough people for that.

Therefore, thanks to Russia, we are forming our own
army “on the march.” Of course, Ichkeria is too small to
maintain such a large military potential here, in the
mountains, under the bombs of the Russian “falcons.”
That is why the “liberated” plain part of our country

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

burns under the feet of the “liberators.”” While sending
bombs and missiles at us and covering us with fire,
Russia also calls us its citizens. Well, I have nothing
against that. Let these 300,000 “subjects of the Russian
Federation” go beyond the borders of the “autonomy”—
Russia is a large country. Let the people there fight for
their right to live. To destroy is not the same as to build.
We can also do that. Russian towns can also catch fire
easily. Until now, it was hunting for wolves. It is now
time for the wolves to start their hunt.

[Kokhanets] Do you mean that famous Chechen ter-
rorism with which Russia has been intimidating nations?

[Dudayev] Nobody will be able to lower us to the level of
terrorists, no matter how hard they might try to create
for us the image of a Chechen mafia, bandits. If the
Chechens had allowed themselves to perpetrate things
similar to those of the Russian vandals, we would not
have regarded ourselves as a worthy nation. I am partic-
ularly proud that there have been no such actions.
However, we are forced to defend ourselves. Until the
enemy feels misery in his own home, he will not com-
prehend the grief of others. It was Harry Truman who
said: There is no policy, no ideology, no diplomacy, no
laws, and no jurisdiction in the world in terms of which
one can speak with Russia. It only understands the law of
brute physical force.

It has even taken the form of cynical sacrilege. Children
in neighboring Stavropol Kray collect presents for “the
gallant Russian soldiers”: embroidery, drawings, and
poems about those who kill children of their age. This is
truly frightening! Russian soldiers, with their high-
precision w-apons, destroy boarding schools, schools,
and maternity homes: At present, there is not a single
school and not a single hospital left—everything has
been razed—and at the same time, Russian pedagogues
teach schoolchildren the “humanitarian attitude”: to
give presents to murderers. They also scare them with
“‘Dudayev’s bandits.” What about these ‘bandits’?
They took a few soldiers prisoner. One had fired 1,732
rounds with his machine gun and another 4,321 rounds.
We asked whether they had known where they had been
aiming. “Yes, we did,” they said, “at the population—
the Chechens.” “You felt they you could kill civilians?”
“Those were our orders....” What did the Chechen
bandits do to them? Have them torn to pieces? It is a sin,
in accordance with Chechen law, to raise one’s hand
against prisoners of war. Defenseless people are spared.
They are given food and cigarettes.

[Kokhanets] While meeting many Chechens here,
mothers who have lost their children, and refugees who
barely escaped from their burning homes with nothing
but light clothing on, I noticed that they really harbor no
hate for the Russian people. Tell me, did I just simply
come across such soft- hearted interlocutors or is bitter-
ness generally not characteristic of your people?

[Dudayev] Bitterness is not among the genetic features of
the Chechens. They may hate concrete murderers, but

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 41

not the entire nation. The Chechens would feel sorry for
the “poor Russian soldiers” who are sent to war against
their will and would sympathize with their mothers, who
cry as they look for their sons among the POWs. Our
mothers do not even cry when they bury their children.
Hearts turn to stone with grief and mountain women do
not feel it dignified to shed tears.

[Kokhanets] However, every nation has its share of
scoundrels. Chechnya is no exception. What is worse for
you, Russia’s military might or your own scoundrels?

[Dudayev] You know that not a single nation is without
its scoundrels (in the Soviet Union, the training of such
“cadres” was done on a conveyor-belt basis). Take your
nation or ours. | have given much thought to this breed
of people. In Kyrgyz literature, there appears the notion
“mankurt.” Bulgakov [writer] created the image of
Sharikov [in “The Dog’s Heart’’]. I would call this breed
“bedbugs.” What is their principle? It is the absence of
principles. For them, fatherland, nation, mother, and
freedom are empty or trite notions. We do not have too
many “bedbugs,” but they do exist. One can follow their
pro-Moscow leanings.

[Kokhanets] What types of weapons is Russia using,
according to your evidence?

[Dudayev] There is not a single type of weapon or
military hardware, from sea-based troops to military
space forces, that would not be deployed against Chech-
nya. Moreover, nobody divides people into Chechens
and non-Chechens. Members of over 100 nations who
have lived in Chechnya are targets for the “gallant”
Russian pilots. Missiles are unleashed at churches, cathe-
drals, synagogues, and mosques alike. It makes no dif-
ference. Carpet bombing. Weapons that are banned
everywhere in the world. “Scorched earth” tactics. Any-
thing goes when Russia needs to disarm Dudayev's
“bandit formations.” Well, according to our informa-
tion, they have put all the citizens of Ichkeria into the
category of bandit formations. We got a “trophy” (he
shows it—author): documents, signed by Chernomyrdin,
on a new deportation of those Chechens who remain
alive (if there are any) to five Russian oblasts.

[Kokhanets] Tell me, can Russian tanks make their way
into the mountains?

[Dudayev] We will be happy to receive them here, for we
need tanks. Incidentally, our 12-year-old boys have been
cracking the famous T-80 and T-81 like nuts, although
they were considered a miracle of Russian military
technology: They found a vulnerable spot in them (so far,
this is a military secret, I will tell about it when the war
is over}—and no wonder.

[Kokhanets] What are your estimates of the losses sus-
tained by the Russian Army?

[Dudayev] They are so huge that I am afraid to give a
concrete figure. It is hardly known in Russia itself. I
know for sure that it is more than 30,000. I state this with

full responsibility: Bodies of Russian soldiers in con-
tainers are being dropped by aircraft into the Caspian
Sea, acid lakes, and mountain canyons, and then those
places are “ironed” with bombs. The present Russian
leadership does not value the lives of their soldiers. It
seems to me that they do not know what to do with their
army and are deliberately destroying the soldiers physi-
cally. At a meeting between our elders and Chernomyr-
din’s representatives, we proposed: Take your army
away and let us start peace talks. The answer was this:
“Where are we to take it?” Well, they have found

Hundreds of bodies were lying abandoned in the streets
of Groznyy. Our Chief of Staff General Maskhadov
offered [Russian] General Babichev: Let us stop this
massacre and take away the bodies: We guarantee that

answer was in typically Russian style: “If they bother
you, you take them away!” Lots of inexperienced boys
from the autumn call-up were brought here to be slaugh-
tered—minelaying units covered them... in the rear.

Their top brass, tasked with “establishing constitutional
order,” seem to be concerned with one thing only: to
acquire the rank of marauding general: Aircraft are en
route to Russia loaded with goods looted in Chechnya;
they do not even balk at taking used furniture. As for the

There will never be a shortage of cannon fodder.

{Kokhanets] Do you believe in victory?

[Dudayev] If I had the slightest doubt, I would not be at
the head of my people’s struggle for freedom.

[Kokhanets] However, war is a cruel affair. You yourself
are saying that the Russian Army uses “‘scorched-earth”
tactics. Even after a victory, it is difficult to rise to one’s

great human losses....

[Dudayev] The aggressor has left not a single blade of
grass from our economic potential. As | traveled through
the territory and studied the situation, I noticed that
even high-voltage transmission pvlons had been mowed
down, like sheaves, with high- precision weapons. On
just a single stretch, which is barely accessible, 27 pylons
were destroyed. Precision weapons are extremely expen-
sive, much more expensive than the “target.” They do
not spare their own huge costs to uproot the economy
here. Vandals... However, we are tenacious people. It
will be difficult for Russia to rebuild its economy after
the war, because slave labor from colonized nations is
unproductive. We will be able to replenish our economic
losses within just a few months, because we will work on
our free land. We will work with faith in Allah’s mercy.
He did not abandon us on the days of battles and will not
abandon us in the time of peace.

[Kokhanets] Some people characterize this war as a
struggle between Christianity and Islam.

[Dudayev] Nonsense. The aggressor does not have the
army of Christ: It is an obscure military mass, ready to
kill, burn, and rape without any Faith [upper case as
published]. Anyway, you yourself can see here how
“Orthodox” Russia adheres to the commandments of
the Christian faith.

I thank your [Ukrainian] people for the humanitarian
assistance rendered to our refugees and for your compas-
sion for our grief. We do not need anything else, although
I know that “Ostankino” has spread myths about
“hordes of Ukrainian militants in Chechnya.” Myths
remain myths. We do not need assistance in the form of
weapons or volunteers. We will be able to deal with the
aggressor on our own.

I wish Ukraine to become a strong power with a clear
position. Do everything to achieve this goal; thereby you
will help all those nations that believe in your and their
own future.

[Kokhanets] P.S. When I was saying goodbye to

Groznyy Said Returning to Normal

LD2804114095 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1025
GMT 28 Apr 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, April 28 (Inter-
fax)}—The situation in Groznyy is going back to normal
and the population of Groznyy stands at 250,000 now
that more refugees have returned to the Chechen capital,
the head of the information team of the federal commis-
sion on the Chechen reconstruction and an adviser to
First Deputy Prime-Minister Oleg Soskovets Yuriy
Mikhaylov told Interfax.

As of April 28, the Groznyy bakeries produce 46 tonnes
of bread a day. Eighty five shops are open to customers.
They have been supplied with 16.3 billion rubles’ worth
of foodstuffs. Thirty nine of Groznyy’s fifty eight
schools, three hospitals and six out-patient clinics are
functioning.

According to Mikhaylov, 111 kilometers of electricity
lines and a 207-kilometer gas line have been restored.
City buses run on 15 routes.

More than 15,000 hectares of land have been sown in
Chechnya. A total of 10.4 billion rubles of retirement
and other benefits and 2.7 billion rubles of the delayed
salaries and wages have been paid to the citizens of the
Chechen republic, Mikhaylov said.

Use of Air Force, Artillery in Samashki Denied

LD2704 165395 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1509
GMT 27 Apr 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, April 27 (Inter-
fax}—The reports on the federal troops’ use of aviation
and heavy artillery systems against the village Samashki
in Chechnya are untrue, according to a press release
published by the interim commission for overseeing
citizens’ constitutional rights and freedoms.

The commission chaired by Russian Justice Minister
Valentin Kovalev made this conclusion “‘on the basis of
a thorough examination of documents, landscape and
polls among locals” as well as reports by commission
members from the village.

At the same time, the press release stated that “the
commission had locals’ evidence of arsons, pillage and
killings that did take place.” “It proceeds from eye-
witnesses’ evidence that such actions were committed by
armed people with black strips on their heads and
dressed in a customized military uniform,” according to
the document.

The commission decided to submit all available mate-
rials to the Prosecutor General’s Office to institute
criminal proceedings on the case.

[By ITAR-TASS parliamentary correspondent Pavel

[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 27 Apr (ITAR-
TASS)—Acting Prosecutor-General Aleksey Ilyushenko
instituted criminal proceedings which he will keep under
his personal control, on the fact of civilians being killed
during the taking of the Chechen village of Samashki by
federal troops. Yuriy Voyevoda, a member of the

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 43

Duma’s “Stabilnost” deputies group, told an ITAR-
TASS correspondent this, referring to a talk between
Aleksey Ilyushenko and representatives of the Duma
commission for investigating the causes and circum-
stances of the development of the crisis in Chechnya.

Yuriy Voyevoda stressed that the commission members,
after listening to the acting prosecutor-general, hope that
now Defense Minister Pavel Grachev, Internal Affairs
Minister Viktor Yerin, and Federal Intelligence Service
Director Sergey Stepashin will come to a meeting of the
commission.

According to the deputy, Aleksey Ilyushenko informed
commission members that the prosecutor-general’s
office instituted a mass of criminal proceedings on
events in Chechnya, from the appearance of false
“Chechen letters of advice” to crimes committed in the
course of military actions both by Dudayev’s supporters
and by federal troops.

The acting prosecutor-general also noted that he sanc-
tioned the search for Dudayev and his arrest, because
previously there was no such document in the prosecu-
tor-general’s office. In this connection Yuriy Voyevoda,
in his turn, stressed that at one of the earlier meetings of
the Duma commission, former prosecutor-general Val-
entin Stepankov asserted that he signed such a document
on 7 November 1991.

Besides that, the deputy reported that during the meeting
of the commission Aleksey Ilyushenko expressed serious
concern that the amnesty for individuals who carried out
illegal actions during the armed conflict in the North
Caucasus, which might be approved by the Duma in
May, will not attain its goal—the surrender of arms by
Dudayev’s upporters and the cessation of military
actions in Chechnya.

The acting prosecutor-general believes that there are
individuals among the militants, including criminals
who have gathered in Chechnya, who have nothing to
lose and who will not surrender their arms whatever
amnesty is offered.

Zhirinovskiy Hopes for Border With Kaliningrad

LD2804073795 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Valeriy Biryukov]

[FBIS Translated Text] Kaliningrad, 28 Apr—Liberal
Democratic Party of Russia [LDPR] leader Vladimir
Zhirinovskiy promised to establish a common frontier
between the westernmost region of Russia and the center
should he be elected president of the country. Addressing

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

44 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

Vladimir Zhirinovskiy visited Kaliningrad Region on
Thursday [27 April] along with representatives of prac-
tically every State Duma faction. The legislators spent
the day at frontier outposts and in the patrol escort
brigade in Baltiysk and met the administration head and
the chairman of the Region’s duma.

In his address, Gennadiy Zyuganov, leader of Russia’s
communists, called for the restoration of the USSR and,
mirroring Zhirinovskiy’s tone, he proposed that the
interests of the Russian-speaking population in the
Baltic countries should be defended by tough economic
measures.

Security Specialist Defends N. Caucasus
Deployments

LD2704141495 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1321
GMT 27 Apr 95

[By Anatoly Yurkin]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW April 27 (Itar-
Tass)—The group of troops in the North Caucasus does
not upset the balance of forces and does not pose a threat
to European security, Anton Surikov, Expert at the
Centre for Military, Strategic and Technological Studies
of the Institute of the United States and Canada of the
Russian Academy of Sciences, told Itar-Tass in an exclu-
sive interview on Thursday.

The Treaty on Conventional Forces in Europe (CFE),
which was signed in Paris on November 19, 1990, is
being implemented in good faith, and Russia observes all
points of accords about a stage-by-stage reduction in
conventional arms and hardware, Surikov said.

However, his opinion is that individual provisions of the
CFE Treaty have not only become obsolete but also
entered into conflict with geopolitical and military real-
ities.

The Treaty was worked out and signed under conditions
of the opposition of major military-political blocs (the
Warsaw Pact an¢ NATO) and when the territory of the
Soviet Union, where independent sovereign states have
sprung up now, was viewed as flank areas, Surikov said.

The new geopolitical situation has resulted in a situation
that units of Leningrad and North Caucasian military
districts, the territories of which account for more than a
half of the entire European part of Russia, may have not
more than 700 tanks, 580 combat vehicles (infantry
fighting vehicles, armoured personnel carriers), and
1,280 artillery systems.

Such a state of affairs runs counter to the basic provision
of the CFE Treaty which envisages the principle of
equality and consideration for the security interests of
any signatory country.

As of now, the Treaty provisions about flank limitations
are discriminatory to Russia, Surikov emphasises. This
is illustrated by the situation in the Caucasian region

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

where several foci of armed conflicts exist in proximity
to Russia’s borders. Besides, all states may deploy con-
ventional arms on their territories as they see fit while
Russia is not allowed to do that, Surikov said.

Such a position of Russia is emphasised by the leader-
ship of NATO and the U.S. which, in the expert’s view,
does not promote the achievement of accords during
consultations in Vienna on a revision of individual
provisions of the CFE Treaty. A siatement made by U.S.
State Department spokesman Nicholas Burns at a
briefing on Wednesday about the danger of Treaty’s
being ripped apart is unfounded.

The expert believes that the temporary concentration of
the combined Group of Federal forces in Chechnya for
the period of the restoration of the constitutional order
in this region of Russia poses no threat to European
security or to the members of the North Atlantic Alli-
ance.

The question of raising a combined-arms force in the
North Caucasus is of porely theoretical nature and is
being used by certain forces to fire the passions, the
expert said.

Ossetian leadership’s concept of a dosed return of refu-
gees,” he said in his statement available at Interfax
[sentence as received].

According to the Ingush administration, over 60,000
Ingush refugees have been staying in Ingushetia since the
autumn of 1992 when they fled from their homes as a
result of armed clashes.

Explosion Called ‘Commonplace Event’

MM2704153995 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 28
Apr 95 pl

[Report by Valeriy Yakov: “Accident on Gas Pipeline
Spotted From the Air”}

[FBIS Translated Excerpt] The Ukhta-Torzhok main gas
pipeline ruptured with subsequent combustion of the gas
near the settlement of Vodnoye at 0240 hours, 27 April.
Most likely, only the members of the accident and rescue
services and the operators of the gas pipeline would have
known about this event had the blaze not been spotted
by the crew of a Tokyo-bound passenger plane. [passage
omitted]

According to information supplied by the Ministry for
Emergency Situations Press Service, the accident near
Ukhta was a commonplace event in no way different
from a long string of similar occurrences. Gas and fuel
pipelines rupture, catch fire, and pollute the environ-
ment virtually every day. And no one would have paid so
much attention to the present fire had the world’s press
agencies not reported it. Around 20 meters of the gas
pipeline were ruptured by the explosion and one-tenth of
the surrounding forest has burned down—with esti-
mated damage of 150 million rubles. There are no
human casualties, because the accident occurred in a
pretty sparsely populated area. However, more serious
accidents with more significant consequences occur in
Russia virtually every day. But nothing is said about
them because both the press and society are sick and
tired of hearing about endless tragedies and accidents.
Attention is paid only to the most significant ones.

However, in the opinion of Emergency Situations Min-
ister Sergey Shoygu, even the most minor accidents on
gas and oil pipelines should be regarded as harbingers of
doom. The state of the majority of pipelines in the
country has reached a critical stage. Patching up endless

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

Ae, RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

ruptures is no longer a solution to the problem, if only
because you cannot have pipelines made up of nothing
but patches. The increase in the number of such acci-
dents is advancing at a frightening rate and is becoming
catastrophic. That is why attention should be paid to the
problem of the condition of the pipelines right now, with
no more talk about “routine” accidents, before a real
tragedy occurs.

Official Describes Gas Fire

[Report by Stephane Orjollet]

{[FBIS Transcribed Excerpt] Moscow, April 27 (AFP)—
An explosion at a compressor station triggered a huge
blaze on a gas pipeline in the far north of Russia
Thursday [27 Apri!], officials said, contradicting earlier
reports that the fire was started deliberately after the
discovery of a leak. [passage omitted]

Earlier a spokesman at the ministry of emergencies said
Russian technicians deliberately set fire to the section of
pipeline overnight after discovering a leak at the facility.
[passage omitted]

“It is nothing extraordinary—these are just technical
problems,” Anatoly Nesterov, an advisor to the Komi
economy minister, told AFP.

The blaze was spotted by crew members aboard several
Japan Airlines planes that flew over the region. They said
the flames reached as high as 3,000 meters (10,000 feet)
with smoke billowing up to 6,000 meters. A spokesman
for the environmental organisation Greenpeace in
Moscow voiced doubts about the official version of
events.

“It seems strange to us to speak of a ‘controlled fire,”
said Ivan Blokov of Greenpeace. “As usual the Russian
authorities are minimising things and are the last to
provide information.”

Blokov said the situation appeared to have stabilised but
the accident “illustrates the ecological risks posed by
power installations.”

He said 73 percent of Russian oil and gas pipelines were
more than 10 years old and more than 10 percent had
come on stream more than 35 years ago. [passage
omitted]

Tatar President on Reforms, Ties With Russia
MM2804131195 Moscow TRUD in Russian 26 Apr 95
p3

{Interview with President of Tatarstan Mintimer
Shaymiyev by Nadezhda Nadezhdina and Yevgeniy
Ukhov; date and place not given: “Mintimer Shaymiyev:
"We Are Adopting A Soft Approach to Entering the
Market’”—first paragraph is introduction]

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

[FBIS Translated Text] Mintimer Shaymiyev is 58 years
old; he comes from a large peasant family. No one can
now remember why his parents gave him the name
Mintimer, which in translation from Tatar means “I am
made of iron.” But throughout the long period in which,
after graduating from Kazan Agricultural Institute,
Shaymiyev worked as an engineer, then as minister of
land reclamation and water resources, deputy chairman
and later chairman of the republic Council of Ministers,
first secretary of the republic CPSU committee, and then
chairman of the Supreme Soviet, no one in Tatarstan
ever thought about the Russian meaning of his name.
They began talking about “iron” Shaymiyev from the
moment he was elected chairman of the republic
Supreme Soviet. Because he adamantly defended Tatar-
stan’s sovereignty, did not sign the “common” Federa-
tion Treaty, walked out of the Constitutional Confer-
ence, and concluded an unprecedi-nted bilateral treaty
with Moscow. But surely he is not »nly an “iron” man?
No; he insists on a centrist rejection of radical nation-
alism and is capable of flexibility an’ compromise when
the matter concerns the interests of the republic and the
people living in it.... This was the starting point for our
conversation.

{[TRUD] Mintimer Sharipovich, what features of your
character help most in the job of president?

[Shaymiyev] Being true to my word. And, of course, a
love of people. These are not simply words. Pecpie say
about some people: tiresome complainers.... But I am
patient—after all, they are our people, not people from
another planet. That means life is forcing them to appeal
to us, and we have to help them. A man who has power
should bear no ill will toward people; that, I believe, is
the worst trait a leader can have....

{TRUD] And what about your “iron” will?

[Shaymiyev}] An “iron” will is for business, not for
people. When we were conducting the difficult negotia-
tions with Moscow that lasted three years, character was
needed. But also the ability to compromise. We decided
that it was necessary to move on from the simple
questions to the complex ones. Working groups toiled
and Boris Yeltsin and I held talks. And by 15 February
last year we were ready to sign. The word for “treaty” in
Russian is etymologically linked to the word for “to
negotiate.” There can be no treaty without mutual con-
cessions.

Yes, there are various differences between the Russian
and the Tatar Constitutions, but we have recognized
them on a mutual basis and avoided political confronta-
tion, giving ourselves the time, and ensuring a calm
environment, for further work and to reach a greater
measure of accord. After all, the Russian Constitution
also requires certain changes. The Chechen events
showed that there are many processes which can be
influenced neither by the State Duma nor by the Feder-
ation Council, even when it is a question of war and
peace. That is why I say that our joint work must
continue.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

[TRUD] How has the treaty affected the internal situa-
tion in Tatarstan?

[Shaymiyev] Highly favorably. Earlier we adopted a
declaration on the state sovereignty of Tatarstan, then
the Constitution, enshrining in it that both the Tatar and
Russian languages are state languages, and that every
citizen has the right to both Russian and Tatar citizen-
ship. Before the treaty with Russia was signed, some
people, primarily Russians, felt a certain unease.
Everyone could see “hot spots” cropping up all over the
place. Would we reach accord, would there not be some
discrimination on grounds of nationality?—these ques-
tions were also very natural. I myself am a Tatar, but I
understand very well the feelings of people of other
nationalities. We stated very firmly at the time that
sovereignty should not have a national hue. And the
treaty put everything in its proper place.

Let us recall. The nationalist parties advocated the full
independence of Tatarstan and secession from Russia.
There were stormy rallies—extremists even burst into
parliament. They pushed things to absurd lengths—
banning mixed marriages. On the other hand, the “‘fed-
eralists” were opposed to sovereignty—let Tatarstan
remain as it was, an autonomy without rights. I always
said that both approaches were unacceptable to us. I
have always been a centrist, because I am convinced that
only a centrist can unite people in such a situation. In
conditions in which the renewal of society is under way
and national self-consciousness has grown, the centrist
course has been vindicated. This was also confirmed by
the recent elections to our State Council: Not a single
deputy was elected from the extremist parties or move-
ments. Victory was won by forces advocating support for
the president’s centrist course—an attitude of respect
toward every nation and the free development of all
peoples in the republic.

[TRUD] Do you consider that a treaty akin to the
Russian-Tatar treaty is the master key which would help
to organize relations between the regions and the center?
How in general do you see the rational federal structure
of Russia?

[Shaymiyev] No, I do not believe that the Tatarstan
model is the only one possible. I would not liken the
Russian Federation to any other federation—neither to
the American federation, nor to the German federation.
Our multiethnicity and our multifacetedness are a huge
asset. Some people say that Russia needs to be restruc-
tured, divided into guberniyas, with equal rights for all.
They apzarently mean well. But no, such reformations
would lead to no good. I would even put it more strongly:
What, isn’t one Chechnya enough? Surely, if the reforms
are not working, it is not for want of a redrawing of the
map? This is simply politicking, the ambitions of lead-
ers.... Tatarstan will never agree to that. I have always
firmly supported the integrity of the Russian Federation;
we will never retreat from this position. But there should
be no restructuring which ignores the lawful rights of the
republics.

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 47

We concluded a bilateral agreement and are fulfilling our
obligations under 12 intergovernmental agreements; we
pay our taxes punctually and receive no subsidies,
relying on our own budget. Surely that is not bad? But it
is not a fixed standard. Relations with the center can be
various. We have a bilateral agreement, but the majority
of components signed the Federation Treaty. Whether it
is good or bad is another matter. But they signed
it—therefore they must observe it. It is annoying that the
significance of this treaty has been greatly diminished. If
we want the Federation to be really meaningful, the
treaty must work. It was conceived as part of the Con-
stitution, but today it turns out to be who-knows-what
kind of addendum to it. We must realize that if the
Federation Treaty becomes a meaningless sheet of paper,
we could easily go down the old path of a unitary state.

And one more thing. The Federation Treaty was
“launched” from above, being proposed to the Federa-
tion components. The center delegated to the compo-
nents what it considered necessary. But if we want to
build a democratic commonwealth, powers must be
delegated from the bottom to the top. Seeing that history
has opened up for us an opportunity to restructure
society, why not do so democratically? The center must
become attractive for the regions.

[TRUD] What does attractive mean?

[Shaymiyev] Carrying out a policy that draws people
toward it. But at the moment the center is committing
too many mistakes, both political and economic, and
making many ill-considered decisions. You know, when
we signed the treaty, it was as if a weight fell from our
shoulders. Nevertheless, I said: “Boris Nikolayevich,
Tatarstan will not stop at this.” The president looked at
me in surprise: “But we have just signed a treaty...” I
explained: “If we want Russia to be strong, we must set
about decentralizing the economy and give more rights
to the localities.”

The center even now wants to keep everything in its own
hands. Thus in early 1992 a decision was made to
dissolve the kolkhozes and sovkhozes. Moreover, virtu-
ally right before the spring work. They declare: Develop
yourselves on a democratic basis, but they themselves
dictate. Well okay, we will dissolve the kolkhozes. But
what do we put in their place? Private farmers? Yes,
everyone understands by now that personal interest is
intrinsic to the individual, that it must be skillfully used.
But after all, it is not because we are against reforms that
we have few private farmers. It is simply because at the
moment we do not have the right conditions for them—
credits, machinery, servicing to go with it. It is well
known that countrywide last year 40,000 private farms
folded. But we did not rush things, and the 700 people
who have started their own farms are holding on.

We have been scolded for carrying out reforms slowly.
Yes, we are implementing them without haste. Tatarstan
has its own, soft, as we say, approach to entering the
market. We have endeavored to avoid drastic reductions

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

48 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

in the standard of living and have not rushed to release
all prices, to transform the kolkhozes, or to privatize.
Have things become worse because of this? No. Our
republic is one of the most stable and prosperous regions.

{[TRUD] Where did you begin your reforms?

[Shaymiyev] It is not possible to extricate all sectors at
once. We have neither the money nor the capacity. We
decided that ensuring food supplies was the most impor-
tant task. And we support agriculture through the
budget. Moreover, the banks are not sitting on the
money, the agricultural producers have actually received
it—for mineral fertilizers, plant protection agents, road
construction, gasification.... Thanks to this, for the third
year running we are gathering record harvests and
ensuring the growth of livestock production.

{[TRUD] We have been round the stores and into the
market. Vegetables are noticeably cheaper than in the
center. There is plenty of cottage cheese and milk....

[Shaymiyev] And most importantly—it is of our own
production. People buy it in the stores more readily than
in.ported products. At the moment we are still subsi-
dizing bread, milk, and meat. Soon we will stop even
these subsidies. And we grow so many vegetables that
sellers have themselves been forced to lower prices. The
land and property in kolkhozes is shared out among their
members. But the work continues to be mainly coliec-
tive. A man can leave a farm, but does not do so, because
he understands that he would find it difficult on his own.
And those private farmers who have gone their own way
are at the moment still helped by the collective farms.
They conclude contracts with them, buy their products,
and deliver them to the market, making a profit for
themselves by doing so. We want farm leaders to become
brokers between those who work on the land and the
market. But everyone must determine for himself what
form of organization of work suits him best: to set up a
private farm on his own, to unite several farms together,
or to remain in the collective farm. Or should we carry
out decollectivization by force, just as we once carried
out collectivization by force?

{[TRUD] Nevertheless, people are beginning to feel the
masters of their own fate?

[Shaymiyev] Yes, that is the main thing. Unfortunately,
we have made plenty of blunders. But overall people are
finally beginning to feel that they are individuals. I have
been in power for a long time. If would appear that one
or two things depended on me. But to be frank, in terms
of my own self-esteem, what was it really like? A party
member spent his whole life trembling before the party.
He couldn’t do this, that, or the other. And if his party
card was taken away, he was nothing. We were all
blinkered—blinded, as it were. 1 will say honestly that at
one time I did not fully understand why they are so
concerned about human rights in America, why
Sakharov, for example, risked everything to fight for
them. But now I have been through a lot and done a lot

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

of rethinking. I meet with people and ask: Do you want
things to be as they were before? They reply: no. After all,
everyone has but one life. And he wants to live it
properly, to feel himself free.

{[TRUD] Free.... That’s nice, of course. But he has
children, a family, his wages are not paid, and he is
destitute...

[Shaymiyev] That is indeed terrible. I agree. 1 share that
pain. But we will have to so through these difficulties. I
have already said: We have made many mistakes, there
is no consistency in the way in which we carry out
reforms, and the process of faiiing living standards is
dragging on. We in Tatarstan are doing everything
possible to make this period as short as possible. Until
1994 we regulated prices across the board; now we are
releasing them gradually; we are trying to ensure that
income is not lower than the subsistence minimum.

{[TRUD] How?

[Shaymiyev] We have established that no one should
receive pay of less than 110,000 rubles [R]. (The subsis-
tence minimum in Tatarstan at the time was R108,000.)
This means that the law obliges the employer to pay an
employee not less than this sum. If you cannot pay this,
resolve the problem another way: Reduce your work
force or declare yourself bankrupt. If a man becomes
unemployed, we will fix benefit for him—he will not be
destitute. The Cabinet of Ministers adopted a decree on
the payment of wages. It says that 30 percent of all funds
coming into enterprises should be reserved for the
prompt payment of wages.

We have introduced a targeted system of social protec-
tion. By decision of the Russian Government, child
benefit (a beggarly sum!) is issued to all families, irre-
spective of the family’s income. We, however, have set
up a special institute of social problems, which catego-
rizes families precisely according to income, and deter-
mines who should get what benefits. We give consider-
able help to those who are in great difficulty. The same
thing will happen with rent. There is an indicator
showing the ratio of the pay of the richest 10 percent to
the pay of the lowest paid 10 percent. In Russia as a
whole it is somewhere over a factor of 15, but in our
republic it is a factor of nine. That is why there is also
less social tension in the region and the situation is
calmer.

Take also privatization. In addition to the Russia-wide
voucher of R10,000, we have allocated to everyone a
personal privatization deposit of R13,000—we worked
out that there is more property in the republic. These
deposits will participate on preferential terms in auc-
tions for the sale of real estate. Of course, people will not
see any return on this today, or perhaps even tomorrow.
But in time the investments will get moving-—we are
working intensively on this—and income will follow. At
the Kama Truck Plant they have already begun to
receive dividends. And when there was a fire at the plant,

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

shareholders gave up their income for the year in order
to overcome the accident more quickly. So there is
something worth striving for, isn’t there?

[TRUD] Of course, your main interest is your work, the
affairs of the republic. But all the same, to what do you
devote your rare free time?

[Shaymiyev] I love to potter about on the land. Just what
don’t I grow on my three hundredths of a hectare—
vegetables, fruit, berries. All according to the latest
know-how. I think that my family is the first in the
republic to eat new potatoes from its own plot.

I also love horses. I get this from my grandfather. We
built a racecourse in Kazan and bought pedigree horses.
In Tataria Sabantuy [traditional Tatar spring holiday] is
not complete without horse racing. The whole city is at
the racecourse on those days. And I am there too.

Pskov Finds Itself in Wake of Moscow Government

[FBIS Translated Text] The Pskov Oblast administra-
tion head has issued a directive that introduces a nearly
blanket regulation of retail prices on his territory. Thus,

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 49

after Moscow, Pskov Oblast has become the next partic-
ipant in the campaign of emphatic insubordination to
Moscow, which not so long ago prohibited such price
experiments.

The message of the directive signed by Pskov Governor
Viadimir Tumanov is piain: state regulation of prices of
more than 100 brands of goods and services is in effect.
The argument for this step is artless in a provincial sort
of way—“with the view of further strengthening of
economic reforms and streamlining state prices and
tariffs." KOMMERSANT-DAILY has written more
than once that such actions by regional authorities
directly contravene the edict signed by the president this
past March “On Measures Streamlining State Regula-
tion of Prices (Tariffs).” Equally, more than once did
KOMMERSANT-DAILLY predict that regional adminis-
trations would overtly and covertly sabotage it.
Restraining prices artificially by regulating them is the
backbone of many governors’ economic policies.

The extent of gubernatorial insubordination, however,
may put the grimmest forecasts to shame. The leader
here, of course, is Moscow Mayor Yuriy Luzhkov. The
directives he signed on changing the maximum trade
surcharges on soap and milk sent the first signals that
Boris Yeltsin’s edict remained ‘“‘unnoticed.” The
Moscow government initiative was backed and further
developed in the country’s Northwest. The Pskov Oblast
government not only approved a list of concrete goods
and services whose prices are subject to administrative
control (like in Moscow) but it introduced price regula-
tion of all goods “manufactured and consumed on oblast
territory.” But then Pskov Oblast has been reputed
throughout the recent decade for being a land of undis-
turbed raykoms [Communist Party rayon committees],
and such a decision by the oblast administration did not
generally come as a surprise.

By the same token, one should not overestimate the
economic consequences of the revival of socialism in one
isolated oblast. Pskov is far from a federal-scale center of
business activity, and it is up to its administration to
figure out how to subsidize money-losing products. Thus
the only thing it entails for Moscow is that the Finance
Ministry will face demands for bigger subsidies to the
region. The real problem, however, is the fact that it is
becoming increasingly evident that the president’s edict
is ineffective and that the government is unable to
enforce federal laws. The president’s edict on liberalizing
prices in general provides for no sanctions at all against
administrations that violate it, while an attempt to solve
this problem head on—by firing governors—is doomed
to failure for obvious reasons.

Yeltsin Lifts Restrictions on U.S. Banks

LD2704165695 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1509
GMT 27 Apr 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, April 27 (FIA-
Interfax)—Russian President Boris Yeltsin signed a

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

50 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

decree today on lifting the restrictions on the perfor-
mance in Russia of the U.S. banks which had received
the permission to carry out banking operations in the
country before November 15, 1993.

The Russian p*%ident’s press service said the decree had
been issued in addition to the earlier signed presidential
decree of June 10, 1994, on improving the performance
of the Russian banking system. In accordance with this
decree, foreign and joint banks which earlier received
licenses of the Central Bank of Russia are allowed to
work with Russian residents.

A total of 12 foreign and joint banks have received the
licenses of the Central Bank for carrying out banking
operations in Russia. Among these twelve banks are
Credit Lyonnais, Bank of Austria, Societe-
Generale-Vostok, Bank of China, BNP-Dresdner Bank,
Credit Suisse, ABN-AMRO Bank, ING Bank and the
U.S. Citibank and Chase Manhattan Bank.

However, the presidential decree of June 10, 1994, was
applied only to those foreign banks which had begun
operating in Russia before mid-November 1993. Thus,
the decree was not applied to Chase Manhattan which
has been working in Russia for more than 20 years.

LD2704165495 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1509
GMT 27 Apr 95

{FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, April 27 (PIA-
Interfax)—President Boris Yeltsin signed Thursday a
decree on supporting priority investment projects
financed under the Russian-American framework agree-
ment on the oil and gas industry.

The decree relieves Russian oil and gas companies
getting loans from the U.S. export-Import Bank from the
compulsory sale of part of currency returns made by
exporting oil and oil products.

A high-ranking Fuel and Energy Ministry spokesman
told the Petroleum Information Agency that the decree
amounts to launching lending programs of the Export-
Import Bank in keeping with the July 1993 framework
agreement with the Russian Central Bank, the Energy
and Finance Ministries.

Earlier 11 Export-Import Bank projects in Russia total-
ling $1.4 billion were frozen when benefits to companies
receiving credits were cancelled. Programs under the
framework agreement involve such leading banks as
Societe Generale, Citibank, Paribas, Chase Manhattan
that offer loans for Export-Import Bank guarantees to
purchase equipment for the reconstruction of Russian
oil-extracting and processing facilities.

During a visit to Moscow in February 1995 a delegation
of Export- Import Bank was told at leading Russian
ministries and the Central Bank that they would pro-
mote the implementation of the framework agreement.
According to the ministry spokesman, the April 27

Yeltsin Issues Decree on Stock Market
Operations

LD2704165595 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1609
GMT 27 Apr 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, April 27 (FIA-
Interfax)—President Boris Yeltsin issued a decree
Wednesday [26 April] on adjusting the operation of legal
entities in the financial and stock markets to Russian
legislation.

Within three months executive bodies are to take steps to
transform legal entities operating in these markets
without licences from the Central Bank or the Finance
Ministry into investment institutions and offer them
organizational and advisory assistance. After that all
unlicensed operations must be stopped.

The president also invited the cabinet to decide about
government support for the formation of non-
government regional funds to help victims of economic
crimes and guarantee conditions for the compensation of
money losses.

Yeltsin gave orders to submit in a month’s time a draft
law to the Duma providing for the possibility of arresting
the assets and property of legal entities for unlicensed
activities in the financial or stock markets on actions
from appropriate executive federal bodies.

The Central Bank is to charge for interest the means
confiscated or arrested under court rulings.

found a solution to the problem of wages which worries

Deputy Minister on Funding Defense Sector

MM2804093695 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 26 Apr 95 First Edition pp 1-2

{Interview with Russian Deputy Finance Minister Andrey
Astakhov by unidentified ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA cor-
respondent; date, place not given: “Defense Industry:
Living Within One’s Means. Government Intends To
Provide Military-Industrial Complex With Financial Sup-
port”—first paragraph is introduction}

{[FBIS Translated Text] Military reform and defense
enterprises’ problems have been the subject of discussion
for several years now. How are the country’s defense
requirements being funded now? This was discussed
during our correspondent’s conversation with Andrey
Andreyevich Astakhov, Russian deputy finance minister
and leader of the Defense Complex Department.

[Astakhov] The law on the federal budget is the main
official document governing defense industry funding.
The current level of military spending is not just the
result of a change of state approach to foreign policy. It
is rather the logical result of the move away from the
system of planned distribution to a market economy. In
this situation the Finance Ministry’s role has been con-
siderably stepped up. Particularly at the stage where the
federal budget is formulated and ratified. Moreover, the

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 51

main provisions of Russian military doctrine, the need
for real reform of the Russian Army, and other precon-
ditions predetermined the possibility of a considerable
reduction in military spending and the optimization of
the structure of the aforesaid spending, which is fully “in
line” with the state’s tough financial and monetary-
credit policy. Therefore during the work on the draft
budget the Defense Ministry’s requested funds were
subjected to thorough analysis. Ultimately 27.7 percent
of federal budget revenue in 1995 is being channeled into
national defense. Let us point out that this is the first
time that this spending has been fully revealed and the
priorities have been highlighted insofar as procurements
of arms and military hardware and military research and
development work are concerned.

[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] Are there any changes to
the system for funding defense orders in market condi-
tions?

[Astakhov] The current system of funding the state defense
order developed in the past. In simplified terms it looks
like this: Budget funds are paid directly to the state
customer—the Defense Ministry (other management
organs). It distributes the funds among branches of troops,
which in turn pay for the output and work performed. New
forms of payment in our economy such as treasury bonds
and tax exemptions are also being used along with tradi-
tional means of funding military expenditure.

This system is functional and effective on three main
conditions. First, that the contracts concluded by state
customers are rigidly linked with the budget funds allo-
cated for this purpose. Second, that budget funds are
allocted in full, and, third, that the delivery and targeted
use of budget funds are monitored.

What is the actual situation? The 1994 state defense
order approved in December 1993 amounted to more
than double the appropriations scheduled for this pur-
pose. Enterprises were informed of this order's indica-
tors, which geared them to manufacture defense output
that in cost terms did not have real funding to back it up.

Foreseeing the consequences of this situation (total
“consumption” of their own and borrowed funds, worth-
less outlay, wage arrears for workers employed in defense
production) the Finance Ministry was forced in February
1994 to repeatedly raise the question of bringing the
defense order into line with the state’s financial poten-
tial. The defense order was regrettably only adjusted in
August: It was considerably reduced. As a result of the
adjustment some of work and output ended up
unclaimed by the customer during the reporting year.

Mindful of past negative experience, the Finance Min-
istry suggested that the 1995 order be drafted and
submitted to the government on the basis of draft budget
indicators. This would make it possible for enterprises to
begin work in quantities that guaranteed payment. But
by mid-April the materials necessary for this matter to be
discussed and adopted had not emerged. Enterprises are

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

52 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

still afraid of concluding contracts with customers,
although they have been given that right.

Now let me say something about monitoring. In the
Russian market economy its significance and effect on
the system of funding cannot be underestimated. Let me
point out that monitoring of the delivery and targeted
use of budget funds allocated to pay for the defense order
is still feeble on the part of both customers and supervi-
sory bodies. Given the commercialization of banking
activity and the fact that enterprises can have several
payment accounts, there are a whole series of difficulties
involved in monitoring the delivery of funds from the
Defense Ministry to the people carrying out the orders.
Let me first point out the imperfect nature of tax and
banking legislation and the shortage of qualified cadres.
Many professional specialists have gone off to commer-
cial structures. The Finance Ministry’s Audit Adminis-
tration is also suffering as a result. And the supervisory
department set up within the Defense Complex Depart-
ment remains unstaffed.

I think that monitoring will improve with the move to
implementing the federal budget via the Treasury (this
work is due to be completed by the end of the year). We
are also pinning some hopes on the work of the Comp-
troller’s Office.

[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] Andrey Andreyevich, we
should probably not ignore conversion. What is your
opinion of the progress being made with it?

{Astakhov] In 1992-1994 around | trillion rubles [R] in
repayable soft loans and budget funds were used to fund
enterprise conversion programs. This made it possible to
begin the reconfiguration of military production at 884
enterprises. Some 267 programs have been completed in
full. In the past three years enterprises undergoing con-
version via these sources of funding and their own
resources have commissioned capacities producing
civilian output worth around R4 trillion a year.

Analysis shows that every ruble invested in military
conversion produces an average growth of R4 in civilian
production. It is important for this new potential to be
tapped in a market economy.

At the same time experience of conversion over these
past few years has identified unviable areas and short-
comings in the mechanism and in the use of state funds.
Some adjustments must be made. For instance, in 1995
only short-term, cheap programs that produce a rapid
return and guarantee repayment of the funds will be
provided with state support—it is set at R1.4 trillion in
the federal budget. Long-term projects which involve a
large outlay must be implemented within the framework
of the investment process.

[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] In Western countries there
is guaranteed financial support for base defense produc-
tion units. Do we have that? And is it surviving in the
crisis conditions?

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

[Astakhov] This kind of support does exist. Moreover, in
crisis conditions it takes even more active and some-
times nontraditional forms. For instance, last year the
Finance Ministry worked to grant enterprises tax exemp-
tions on payments to the federal budget to settle state
customers’ debts. These exemptions were granted to 820
enterprises.

Here is another example. The government decided to
exempt enterprises where defense output accounts for 15
percent of production or more from paying a fine for late
payments to the federal budget and nonbudgetary funds.
This concession was granted to over 700 enterprises and
organizations.

In 1994 enterprises and scientific organizations under-
going conversion received federal budget subsidies to
swell the consumption fund and for the upkeep of social
installations, testing facilities, and unique equipment
worth a total of R666 billion. Work has been carried out
to write off defense enterprises’ debts under centralized
credits and loans issued in 1992-1994. Several programs
of particular importance to the state are being imple-
mented with guaranteed financial backing.

I would particularly like to point out a new element in
the implementation of the federal budget. The most
important kinds of arms and military hardware and
avenues of research and development work have been
included, under the adopted law, in protected sections of
the budget and are to be fully funded.

{[ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA] Nevertheless, there is
plenty of talk about the state’s debts to military-
industrial complex enterprises.

[Astakhov] Yes, there is talk of federal budget arrears in
respect of state defense order funding for 1994. The
budget now envisages appropriations of R2.0 trillion for
this purpose. A schedule for the repayment of these debts
has been drawn up. It must be hoped that the budget
commitments will be met. It is first intended to pay
wages. The Defense Ministry has now received a targeted
payment of R590 billion for this purpose.

To conclude our conversation, I would like to stress that
the government understands the needs of defense
industry personnel but, given the acute budget deficit, it
is nonetheless necessary to live within our means.

Observer Discusses Wages Arrears Problems
MM2704123095 Moscow TRUD in Russian 27 Apr 95
p2

[Article by political observer Vitaliy Golovachev: “Two
Months Without Wages”]

[FBIS Translated Text] In the space flight control center
near Moscow the average wage by present yardsticks is
simply miserly—less than 200,000 rubles [R]. When U.S.
specialists from the center’s counterpart in Houston
come to the Russian space flight control center they are

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

told everything and shown everything except for one
“military secret’’—wages. It is humiliating and
shameful. If an American receiving over $2,500 were to
learn that the labor of his counterpart, a leading spe-
Cialist, is assessed at $44 he would perhaps feel uncom-
fortable on his colleague’s behalf. But the trouble also is
that even these pittances are being held up and are not
paid for six to eight weeks (in early April flight control
specialists received their money only for February).

A desperate situation has also taken shape at the
Baykonur cosmodrome which, as we know, Russia rents
from Kazakhstan. Teachers, Doctors, and workers in
other professions at Baykonur have not received a kopek
since January. The arrears are already R40 billion. And
the federal budget’s debt to the entire space sector has
reached nearly R300 billion (this year alone). At many
space plants and design bureaus people have not
received their wages for two or three months.

In all as of 1 April 36,500 Russian enterprises and
organizations were in arrears in paying wages to their
workers. That is slightly fewer than in February and
March but nonetheless 5,500 more than in January. If
you look at how the volume of arrears has changed in
rubles then the negative trends are manifested particu-
larly clearly here: The sums of arrears are growing every
month. And although in March the increase was rela-
tively small—R43 billion—it remains a fact that the
arrears are not declining but growing: As of | January
they were R4.2 trillion, in early February they were
R5,534 trillion, in March R5,644 trillion, and in early
April R5,687 trillion...

The situation is worst of all in industry, where wages
arrears are over R3 trillion. In construction, agriculture,
and transport arrears as of | April had nonetheless
declined, albeit not by much. (Although that does not
make it any easier for the workers in every other agri-
cultural enterprise and every third construction organi-
zation, where they have not seen the cashier for two
months now). And in industry total arrears in February
and March increased by R70 billion a month.

Some economists are saying that inflation is increasing
faster than wages arrears so that the real “weight” of the
arrears is decreasing, as it were. But that is not an
entirely honest approach because it is precisely because
of inflation that a person receives (if he does receive)
partially devalued money two months late. Today's
arrears of R5.7 trillion should be measured not in today’s
rubles but in January and February rubles when the
money was actually not paid.

Who is to blame for the fact that over 4 million Russians
do not receive their wages on time? In the recent past the
brunt of criticism was directed at the federal govern-
ment. And there was reason—one third of all arrears
were from the federal budget. But the situation is
changing now. We can only welcome the resolve of the
government to settle its obligations in full. The federal

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 53

budget has already settled accounts with many organiza-
tions of the sociocultural sphere. The Ministry of
Finances is on schedule in issuing last year’s arrears to
the defense industry and the coal industry. That new
arrears are mounting is another matter—that is borne
out by the situation in the space sector, at Baykonur and
a number of other sectors. The treasury is not giving
money even for those expenses which were envisaged in
the recently adopted budget. Perhaps they are going on
the Chechen war or preparation for the victory celebra-
tion? However that may be the government has no
reason to slide into debt again (and as far as I know it is
at present engaged in resolving this problem).

But as a whole the lion’s share of debts is accounted for
not by the government but by clients and local budgets.
In a number of cases some people (including the direc-
tors themselves of “recumbent” enterprises) are “‘mint-
ing” money on the quiet, taking the “cream” and leaving
tens and hundreds of thousands of people without wages.
And here is what is interesting: In such cases the trade
unions are afraid to oppose the director or local admin-
istration (you cannot avoid trouble and in some places
the director is simply “feeding” the trade union com-
mittee chairman) but they are tremendously enthusiastic
in going to picket the government house in Moscow. here
they fear nothing, no one will persecute them, but
outwardly their “action” looks good and formidable:
Well, they are issuing demands and ultimatums to the
government itself.... But why picket if the Ministry of
Finances has handed the money over to the sector or
region? But it turns out that many trade union commit-
tees are not up to demanding a reply from the director.

And it is quite incomprehensible when money is
demanded from the government for the payment of non-
state wages. What is the point? If the plant has failed to
find its niche in the market then it must seek it and display
inventiveness. For the insolvent use must be made of the
mechanism of clearing up (normalizing) and in some cases
the plant should be sold. They cannot endlessly beg for
money from a kindly “uncle’—the state—while con-
cealing their thriftlessness and lack of ability.

Meanwhile chronic wages arrears are now one of the
strongest social irritants in society. Since the start of the
year there have been strikes at 513 enterprises and
organizations. That is more than the entire previous
year! Some 163,000 people took part in the strikes and
669,000 man-days were lost. A serious alarm signal.
Especially as, the Russian Federation State Committee
for Statistics reports, in January through March there
was the lowest correlation between monetary incomes
and the minimum living wage in the past two years. In
the first quarter 45 million Russians were living below
the poverty line. Under these conditions delays in wages
become a “fuse.”

It is impossible to continue to live in debt. The govern-
ment has begun to untangle the nonpayments and there
should be a movement from the nonstate sector to meet

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

54 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

it. In the very near future the finances of 1,600 enter-
prises with a state ownership share of over 25 percent
will be cleared up. In all there are over 7,000 enterprises
on the insolvency register. The hardest and most crucial
stage of the reforms is coming—the stage of real deeds in
the production sphere. While arrears are mounting (the
total deferred arrears as of 1 March were R116 trillion)
the reform will mark time and there will be nothing with
which to issue wages. Normalization is needed not only
for particular enterprises but for the entire economy.

Inflation Rate Slows With Arrival of Spring

MM2604100195 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 26 Apr 95 First Edition p 2

[Report “based on Center for the Economic Situation
figures”: ““The Week’s Prices 17-23 April: Prices of
Sneakers and Bicycles Increase in Spring’’]

{[FBIS Translated Text] Inflation has become sluggish
and subsided now that spring has finally arrived. In the
past week the overall inflation index stood at 101.7
percent,and 105.3 percent since the beginning of April.
Admittedly, if you calculate from the beginning of the
year it turns out that prices have managed to increase by
a factor of 1.5 in this period.

Prices of basic foodstuffs have not increased more than 2
percent a week over the last three weeks. By and large, of
the reported foods, over the last week the prices of fruit
and canned fruit have increased by 3 percent while the
prices of meals at public catering enterprises and certain
kinds of groats, confectionery, and alcoholic beverages
have increased by 2.2-2.8 percent. Prices of butter and
margarine have not increased compared with last week's
level. Prices of dairy produce, eggs, vegetable oil, and
herring have increased by less than | percent.

As of 17 April the average cost of the basket of 19 most
important foods stood at 164,000 rubles[R], an increase
of 1.4 percent over the week, up 4.1 percent since the
beginning of the month, and 50 percent since the begin-
ning of the year.

The cost of the food basket in Moscow and St. Peters-
burg over the week increased by less than | percent
totaling R198,000 and R179,000 respectively.

Over the past week prices of nonfood commodities
increased by 1.8 percent and in general have increased
by 5 percent since the beginning of the month. Prices of
gasoline have increased rapidly, up 6.5 percent over the
week (almost doubling since the beginning of the year).

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

Prices of seasonal goods like bicycles (up 4.6 percent),
denim pants, and sneakers (up 3.2-3.9 percent) saw
above average increases in Russia. Prices of construction
materials increased by 2.5 percent. Prices of refrigera-
tors, furs, carpets, and gold jewelry remained virtually
unchanged.

In Russia’s economic regions prices of industrial com-
modities increased the most in the Far East region—up
2.4 percent. While prices increased least in the Central
region, where the increase did not top 1.5 percent.

Prices of the same goods in various Russian cities still
vary dramatically. Thus, coal prices last week differed by
a factor of 583, while prices of firewood and peat
briquettes differed by factors of 407 and 39 respectively,
and the price discrepancies in respect of certain medi-
cines ranged between 6- and 48-fold.

Last week the rate of increase of prices and tariffs for
paid consumer services continued to outstrip those in
respect of consumer goods. The dramatic increase of
liquid gas tariffs—up 20 percent—was the main factor
behind the increase of prices and tariffs of services in
Russia as a whole. For instance, for residents of Yekat-
erinburg the monthly per capita charge for liquid gas
increased by a factor of 171 and now stands at R2,400
instead of R14, while for residents of Petrozavodsk it
increased by a factor of 2.4 to stand at R2,928 instead of
R1,220. Furthermore, the overall increase in the price of
services was affected by the considerable increase in the
price of mains gas—up 12.7 percent (in Yekaterinburg
again the charge increased by a factor of 60 from R14 to
R840 per month per capita) and the cost of travel on city
transport increased by 4.8-7.4 percent.
