International Affairs

Minister Views Talks With Iran, Turkmenistan

WS2704132295 Kiev VSEUKRAINSKIYE
VEDOMOSTI in Russian 21 Apr 95 p 5

{Interview with Industry Minister Anatoliy Holubchenko
by Heorhiy Chornomorskyy; place and date not given:
“The Minister Does Not Belong to Himself’—first para-
graph is VSEUKRAINSKIYE VEDOMOSTI introduction}

{FBIS Translated Text] A government delegation has
returned to Kiev after holding tripartite negotiations with
Iran and Turkmenistan. Ukrainian Industry Minister Ana-
toliy Holubchenko has answered questions from a VSEU-
KRAINSKIYE VEDOMOSTI correspondent.

[Holubchenko] Given the fact that the industry minister
was selected to participate in the delegation, one can
assume that economic issues were pretty high on the
agenda of these negotiations. Is that correct?

{Chornomorskyy] Yes, that is correct. Economic issues
were predominant. I headed a group in charge of issues
concerning power engineering, metallurgical, and chem-
ical industries. The scope of the problems that were
discussed was quite broad. We are now examining a
project for constructing a gas pipeline connecting Turk-
menistan, Iran, and Europe. I believe that our country
can become one of the cofounders of a consortium that
could implement this project.

{Holubchenko] How? Does Ukraine have extra money?
After all, we do not have hard currency.

(Chornomorskyy] Indeed, our country has no hard cur-
rency so far. But we do have the pipes and pump and
stop fittings [perekachivayushchaya i zapornaya armat-
ura}. Besides, is the scientific potential of Ukrainian
scientists and the experience of Ukrainian construction
workers, who successfully laid pipelines both above the
arctic circle and in the burning deserts, now worthless?
Whatever can be said, we are good partners and I expect
very much from the next tripartite negotiations, which
are scheduled for Ashgabat in two months.

{Holubchenko] Everything has become clear with the pipe-
line now. There were, however, also other issues that were
discussed in Tehran. In what was Ukraine interested?

{[Chornomorskyy] Iran is planning to build two electric
power stations, which presents us with a large field of
potential activities. 1 do not rule out that representatives
of business circles from third countries will also want to
participate in the construction of these facilities. There
may even be a tender announced for this project. |
believe that the projects we have developed will prove
quite competitive. We should not write off the fact that
Ukraine’s explicitly neutral policy is welcomed by both
the leadership of this Islamic state and its residents. In
one word, I am optimistic about this issue.

UKRAINE 55

[Holubchenko] Obviously, tripartite relations have
advantages over bilateral ones. If this is true, what were
the advantages of the Tehran summit?

[Chornomorskyy] This is not exactly so. Both bilateral
and multilateral relations have their distinctive features.
As for—as you put it—the “advantages,” issues con-
nected with mutual payments were discussed at the
recent negotiations. As is known, Ukraine is running a
debt with Turkmenistan for natural gas deliveries. Turk-
menistan is a debtor to Iran, while the latter will have to
pay hefty sums to Ukraine for its deliveries of metal and
the work connected with construction of a whole range of
facilities. Mutual clearance of payments will consider-
ably reduce the time of financial operations, allow us to
slash our debt to Turkmenistan, and ensure regular
shipments of this precious energy source to Ukraine.

By the way, in Tehran the Iranians quite explicitly
expressed their interest in holding more trilateral nego-
tiations. It is envisaged that such talks should be
attended by Ukraine and Kazakhstan. Hopefully, this
summit will actually take place and allow our country to
better utilize its metallurgical potential.

[Holubchenko] In conclusion, I would like to ask a
question that is not directly related with the topic of our
conversation, but is interesting to our readers, nonethe-
less. Do you not feel uncomfortable because of the fact
that legally, you are not a minister but acting minister?
Actually, the entire Cabinet of Ministers, too, seems to
be just an acting cabinet.

{[Chornomorskyy] Of course, I feel some discomfort, but
I have no time for personal emotions. You know, the
minister does not belong to himself. When you work, you
think only about work, and all emotions disappear as a
mirage. To tell you honestly, however, I would like more
certainty. But you can do nothing about it—you can only
wait until the law on power is adopted.

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] BONN, April 27 (Interfax -

Kuchma Meets Havel. Signs Cooperation

Agreement
LD2704 183295 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1718
GMT 27 Apr 95

[From the “Presidential Bulletin” feature: Compiled by
Nikolay Zherebtsov, Andrey Petrovskiy; edited by
Vladimir Shishlin}

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Presidents of Ukraine and
Czechia Leonid Kuchma and Vaclav Havel met in Prague
late on Wednesday [26 April] to sign an outline friendship
and cooperation agreement between their countries.

Speaking afterwards, Havel said the incorporation of
different countries into different blocs should not create
the threat of a new Iron Curtain in Europe.

He reaffirmed that Czechia due to take presidency of the
Council Europe would help towards Kiev’s EC member-
ship.

For his part, Kuchma said European security must be
based equally on bilateral contacts and international
organizations such as the OSCE or the Council of
Europe.

On a possible NATO enlargement, he called for gradu-
alism saying both Russian and Ukrainian interests must
be taken into account.

In a speech to the meeting of Czech businessmen later in
the day, the Ukrainian leader called on them to invest
into the Ukrainian economy because “international
credits cannot save us”.

He said the present volume of bilateral trade ($240
million a year) fails to meet the two countries’ real needs.

On the future of the Chernoby! nuclear power station,
Kuchma said Kiev had decided to shut it down by the
year 2000 because of growing international pressure.

But he said the most serious problem is the encasement
of the 4th unit because “no serious expert could tell what
is going on inside it or how long it will last”.

Oil Firm Starts Test Drilling on Black Sea Shelf

LD2704 164395 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1421
GMT 27 Apr 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, April 27 (PIA-
Interfax)—The Canadian OTM company has started test
drilling on the oil fields of Aktash, situated on the Black
Sea shelf of the Crimean peninsular, where recoverable
oil reserves amount to 2 million tonnes, OTM President
Ron Cormick reported to the Petroleum Information
Agency [PIA] Thursday [27 April].

The PIA has learned, this two-stage program developed
by the firm’s experts provides for developing four fields
on Ukraine’s Black Sea shelf. In the company’s esti-
mates, the total volume of investments needed to imple-
ment this program totals 65 million Canadian dollars
(about $47 million).

At the first stage OTM plans to start extracting oil on the
Aktash field, it is to be put into operation in the second
half of 1995. In addition, under the schedule, the firm
will continue developing two earlier-drilled bore-holes
on the Fontanovskoe oil and gas condensate field, their
daily flow totals 340,000 cubic meters of gas and several
dozen tonnes of condensate.

The second stage includes exploration on fields neigh-
boring the Aktash ones. According to Ukrainian geolo-
gists, the recoverable reserves at these fields exceed |
million tonnes of oil.

OTM enjoys the exclusive right to develop oil and gas
fields on Crimea’s shelf under the product-sharing agree-
ment concluded between the Ukrainian State Surveying
enterprise Krymgeologiya and the HHO Ltd firm, all of
whose shares are held by OTM.

National Affairs

Kuchma, Udovenko, Marchuk Respond to Kozyrev
Statement

AU2704115095 Kiev HOLOS UKRAYINY in Ukrainian
26 Apr 95 p 6

[Report by Natalya Filipchuk: “Do Not Try To Scare
Those Who Had Been Scared Before”’}

{FBIS Translated Text] During the days of [Orthodox]
Easter, Ukrainian President Leonid Kuchma spoke about
his intention to attend the Victory Day festivities in Kiev,
rather than in Moscow, having accounted for his decision
with the following words: “If I fail to be with my people on
the festive days, my people will resent this.” On 9 May, in
the afternoon, L. Kuchma will visit Moscow and lay a
wreath at the grave of the unknown soldier.

It is possible that the Ukrainian president had made this
decision long ago, but in view of the recent statement by
Russian Foreign Minister Andrey Kozyrev, who finds it
possible to resort to “direct armed force for protecting
compatriots abroad,” primarily meaning Crimean [Rus-
sian-speaking] residents, some people assessed L.
Kuchma’s actions as concealed protest.

On Monday [24 April], at a briefing at the Ministry of
Foreign Affairs, Ukraine’s Foreign Minister Hennadiy
Udovenko also expressed his attitude toward the state-
ment by the head of the Russian Federation Foreign
Ministry. He stressed that Russia, just like Ukraine, is a
member of the United Nations. Consequently, all states
must adhere to the UN Charter. That Charter rules out
the use of force in tackling differences.

Acting Prime Minister Yevhen Marchuk was more cat-
egorical in his assessments. He believes that such intim-
idations on the part of a nuclear power, which at a
summit in Budapest signed a document guaranteeing
Ukraine’s security, must become a subject of scrutiny on
the part of international structures.

Consequently, even though it is customary to forgive
offenses during Easter holidays, it appears that Ukraine
does not want to swallow this one. It also appears that
the Ukrainian side does not consider this issue settled.

Deputies View U.S. Visit, Corruption

WS2704121595 Kiev VECHIRNIY KYYIV in
Ukrainian 13 Apr 95 p 1

[Interview with Anatoliy Yermak, deputy chairman of
the Ukrainian Supreme Council Commission for Com-
bating Organized Crime and Corruption, and Mykhaylo

Mafia in the United States’’—first paragraph is
VECHIRNIY KYYIV introduction}

UKRAINE 57

[FBIS Translated Text] A group of deputies, members of
the Supreme Council Commission for Combating Orga-
nized Crime and Corruption, headed by Commission
Chairman Hryhoriy Omelchenko paid a two-week visit to
the United States. The lawmakers participated in the
drafting of an agreement on cooperation between law
enforcement agencies from Ukraine and the United
States, including extradition of criminals. The definitive
fate of the agreement will be decided by the two countries’
presidents during their meeting scheduled for May. Com-
mission Deputy Chairman Anatoliy Yermak and Commis-
sion member Mykhaylo Ratushnyy spoke to our parlia-
mentary correspondent about their working visit in an
exclusive interview.

[Yermak] At the operational level, Ukrainian and U.S. law
enforcement agencies are already maintaining contacts
and exchanging information. However, a special interna-
tional agreement is needed to use such materials in court,
so that criminals do not flee from Ukraine to the United
States, and the opposite way to avoid responsibility, as well
as to make it possible to impound illegally transferred
money, and return it, so to speak, to the homeland. Thus,
we drafted a preliminary agreement.

We were interested in the FBI’s operations. The FBI’s
achievements may serve as an example for us. Inciden-
tally, the FBI does not deal with all cases, unlike our
Police, which is overworked. This institution has its
specific area—organized crime and, to a certain degree,
corruption. Not all Americans openly admit that corrup-
tion exists in the United States. We met people there
who believe that corruption is uncharacteristic of the
United States. We would reply that we had once believed
that there had been no prostitution or drug addiction in
Ukraine, but we can now see the results of this. Still, U.S.
experts admit that corruption exists among U.S. state
officials as well.

We brought with us many documents, which will be
helpful in providing a legal basis for the operations of
our National Bureau of Investigation—or, perhaps, this
subdepartment will have another name. However, the
name is not what really matters. The primary issue is to
set up this subdepartment to investigate cases involving
corruption and organized crime among the top echelons
of state administration.

It must be said that the Americans are as interested as we
are in cooperating with our law enforcement agencies,
and in signing the international agreement. They even
unexpectedly postponed our departure for a week. Had a
critical situation developed in the Supreme Council,
however, there is no doubt that we would have returned
immediately. We knew about developments in Ukraine:
We followed UKRINFORM reports, and learned about
developments from our Embassy and Consulate, and
from the Ukrainian community. On a few occasions, we
telephoned Kiev to find out about acute issues.

We certainly regret that we did not attend the Presidium
to hear its report. I personally, however, did not discover

Naval Forces Barred From VE Celebrations

WS2804111095 Kiev INTELNEWS in English 0415
GMT 28 Apr 95

(“Special to INTELNEWS” by Lilia Budzhurova]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Simferopol, April 27—The
Black Sea Fleet [BSF] command and the Sevastopol City
Council have decided to bar Ukrainian Naval Forces
from taking part in celebrations of the 50th anniversary
of the victory in World War II.

The refusal was justified by the fact that the Ukrainian
sailors swore allegiance to the people of Ukraine.

In his address to an April 25 historical conference, BSF
Commander Adm. Eduard Baltin said that “the BSF
command and many World War II veterans believe that
it would be immoral if Ukrainian Naval Forces take part
in celebrations of the great victory.”

The Sevastopol newspaper FLAG RODINY responded to
this statement in the following way: “The servicemen,
having broken their oath, do not have a moral right to do
military honor to those who perished in the battles for their
Motherland. Such is the opinion of the majority of mem-
bers of the hero city World War II veterans council.”

“The plan of the (Victory Day) military parade was
discussed privately by the BSF command and the city
administration and does not envisage participation of
Ukrainian Naval Forces, border guard troops, air
defense troops and a National Guard battalion that are
stationed in Sevastopol,” said Ukrainian Naval Forces
Deputy Commander, Sevastopol garrison chief Adm.
Mykola Kostrov.

Moroz Discusses Crimea, Power Bill, Future

WS2804 104595 Kiev INTELNEWS in English 0415
GMT 28 Apr 95

[Interview with Supreme Council Speaker Oleksandr
Moroz by Volodymyr Katzman; place and date not
given: “Moroz—The Speaker Speaks Out”—first para-
graph is INTELNEWS introduction]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Parliament Speaker Oleksandr
Moroz is rapidly materializing as the most vocal and
visible alternative to the policies of President Leonid
Kuchma. From his vantage point as the leader of
Ukraine’s legislature and its leftist majority, he has been

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

60 UKRAINE

in the forefront of controversies involving the future
distribution of power and wealth in the country. INTEL-
NEWS correspondent Volodymyr Katzman met recently
with Moroz and discussed his attitude toward Kuchma’s
Power Bill, the current situation in Crimea and where
Ukraine is heading in the future.

{[INTELNEWS] In the Crimean problem, three possible
solutions stand out. First, to leave everything as it is.
Second, to discharge the Crimean Parliament and form a
new one on the basis of those deputies who signed the
appeal to the Ukrainian Parliament. And third, to con-
duct new elections on the peninsula. Which way do you
think is the most rational?

[Moroz] The most rational, in other words, the legal way
is that the Crimean Parliament should continue to work
on the Crimean Constitution. That is why the Ukrainian
Parliament proposed in its statement that every Crimean
deputy personally has to make a choice whether he will
work on the legal ground of Ukraine or not. And it
should not be like writing a personal application, as some
insist. It should be resolved by a vote, not even neces-
sarily by names.

{[INTELNEWS] In your opinion, is it inexpedient to
discharge the Crimean Parliament?

[Moroz] Right. My strategic purpose is to save the
Crimean Parliament. We all will win if (the Crimean
Parliament) works on the legal ground of Ukraine.
Despite Crimean Parliament Speaker Sergey Tsekov’s
speech in the lower house of Russia’s Parliament, which
was, to put it mildly, tactless toward Ukraine, the Rus-
sian deputies showed much wisdom in not making any
decisions. I think it will sober up some separatists.
Moreover, we have met all the demands of Crimeans
regarding the elections. I hope it will also become a
stabilizing factor.

Besides, we are talking with Russia about this problem.
Within recent days, we have had many meetings with
Russian Parliament representatives. Deputy Speaker
Oleh Diomin left for Moscow to discuss the problems of
interstate cooperation on the parliamentary level.

[INTELNEWS] You supported the idea of creating a
strict legislative chain of command. Has your position
changed?

[Moroz] I never talked about a legislative chain of
command. I talked about the chain of command of the
entire government, about the structure of power, its
functions. The power cannot be divided, because it has
one source—the people, citizens of the state. People take
power as something unified, where every branch bears
responsibility for each function.

Today, we hear so often that the executive power does
not have enough authority and that the legislative branch
interferes in its functions. One should admit it happens
sometimes. But does it prevent the executive from
fulfilling its obligations? I do not understand how it can

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

disturb it. What instruments do we have to interfere
with, say, the situation in regions, the economy or some
spheres of public life? We regulate only the legal frame-
work, the activity of some entities and legal provisions
for public life. Well, when we assess the Cabinet of
Ministers’ activity or discuss publicly our opinion
toward one or another powerful official—this is quite
another thing. The Constitution gives us this right. We
use this right and we will use it, as in the whole world,
though I should note that parliaments throughout the
world have more rights and opportunities in this respect.

{INTELNEWS] What changes to the Power Bill would
you propose?

[Moroz] The Power Bill has some essential gaps which
did not go unnoticed by deputies. When it deals with the
executive chain of command, it does not raise any
difficulties. But when the executive branch wants to
strengthen its structure at the expense of the legislature,
that is no good. At any rate, it is not the most terrible
thing. Bearing in mind ihe current difficult situation, we
could agree to it, taking into consideration that the
Power Bill is a temporary measure. But I will never agree
with the idea of restricting the powers of the third
branch, the judicial. The main task of the executive and
legislative branches is to share their authority with the
judiciary. It will then guarantee the democratic process
in society and will form the branch that will be able to
settle quarrels in society.

The proposed draft law provides that the Constitutional
Court consists of representatives from the two branches,
while some deputies and I support the idea of the
Supreme Court being formed by representatives of all
three branches of power. Moreover, it would be better if
the functions of Parliament and presidential structures
were minimal while selecting nominees to the Constitu-
tional Court. I think lawyers should play the prior role,
because they know the constitutional process better and
are concrete professionals.

[INTELNEWS] Does Parliament as well as you feel any
Opposition to such an approach?

[Moroz] There are opponents, but they do not have good
arguments. I think the president himself would agree
with that.

There exists one more essential drawback in the bill. It
creates a certain gap in power structures at the regional
level. We keep repeating the notion of local self-
government and, at the same time, we restrict the power
of local authorities. We should not forget that life in our
society is organized according to a certain scheme. We
have a regional district structure and an oblast structure.
The state delegated certain functions, and they have to
realize these functions through their budgets by control-
ling the activities of certain structures. I do not mean
interference by saying controlling.

It does not mean that we want to mothball the power
system. We should develop it, give it new life. We cannot

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

ignore reality. So, the bill should be further refined
rather than used as an argument in a political struggle. I
am sure we have much potential to find an under-
standing with the president.

[INTELNEWS] How would you explain the controversy
surrounding the Power Bill?

[Moroz] It involves pure political aspects, rather than legal
ones. But, as a member of the legislative body, I prefer not
to comment on the problem. Different political organiza-
tions have expressed their concern about the bill. The
authors of the draft law should consider it. Adopting a
temporary law and temporary measures, we should take
into account the fact that we do not live in our country
temporarily. And this state of ours is not temporary either.
We should see our state in its proper perspective.

Prosecutor’s Office Declares Crimean Referendum

[FBIS Translated Text] The Prosecutor General's Office
of Ukraine protested the 25 April 1995 resolution of the
Supreme Council of the Autonomous Republic of
Crimea “On Holding a Crimea-Wide Referendum”
because it contradicts the laws of Ukraine and therefore
must be annulled. UNIAN was informed of this at the
press service of the Prosecutor-General’s Office of
Ukraine.

The supervision body believes that submitting the ques-
tion on confirmation of the Constitution of the Autono-
mous Republic of Crimea to a general referendum con-
tradicts the law of Ukraine “On Ukraine-Wide and Local
Referendums”’.

Besides, submitting the Constitution of Crimea, which was
annulled by the Supreme Council of Ukraine, for confir-
mation at a Crimea-wide referendum contradicts the Con-
stitution of Ukraine, in accordance with which Ukraine’s
laws have supremacy on the territory of Ukraine.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination

62 UKRAINE

surprised by Kiev’s failure to respond instantly to the

discussions, the Ukrainian public prosecutor’s office
issued a protest against the Crimean referendum. It said
on Thursday that Crimea’s decision to appeal to the
people whether they agree with the restoration of the
peninsular constitution, abolished by Kiev, contradicted
the Ukrainian constitution and should be annulled.

Under the Ukrainian laws, a Crimean constitution shall
be adopted by local legislature and approved by the
parliament of Ukraine.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

Karmazin told deputies anxious over the political stale-
mate that has evolved since Crimean Parliament
Speaker Sergey Tsekov’s recent trip to Russia that one or
more of the options would be presented for consider-
ation following consultation with President Leonid
Kuchma and other political leaders.

“We cannot make a decision on the Crimean issue
without the agreement of the president and the govern-
ment,” Karmazin told Parliament Thursday morning.

Tsekov engendered the anger of many in Ukraine when
he spoke to the Russian Duma earlier this month,
criticizing the country’s human rights record in Crimea
and appealing for the imposition of Russian economic
sanctions.

Crimea’s legislature split in two following the speech,
with 42 of its 98 members forming what they said was
their own Parliament and appealing for the right to deal
separately with the central government in Kiev.

Tatar Kurultay faction leader Refat Chubarov, who
heads the breakaway Crimean Parliament, argued in
favor of a partial dissolution of the regional legislature
that would leave his group able to work things out with
the Ukrainian government.

“The best decision would be to transfer authority from
the current Crimean Parliament to the Parliament of the
Autonomous Republic of Crimea,” he said, referring to
the name that the schismatic deputies have given their
assembly.

“The legal mechanism for such action should be worked
out in the Ukrainian Parliament,” he added.

Many, however, are doubting the legality of such a move.

Nina Karpachova, who r-resents a Crimean district in
the Kiev Parliament, was one of them. “This is a matter
for the voters, whom to recognize,” she argued.

Economic Issues

Public Opinion Survey on Economic Reform

MK2704134095 Moscow NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA in
Russian 27 Apr 95 p 2

(Report by Vladimir Skachko under the “Ukraine”
rubric: “Kiev Residents Have Faith in Reforms, But
Know Little About Them. Results of Public Opinion
Surveys Reflect Also Uncertainty Prevailing in Power
Structures”}

[FBIS Translated Text] A public opinion survey con-
ducted among Kiev residents by the “Democratic Initi-
atives” center shows that people have not yet fully
perceived the necessity of the reforms. According to the
results, only 37 percent of respondents think that mar-
ket-oriented reforms need to be accelerated; 30 percent
are for reforms to be gradual; and 33 percent are either
totally against reforms or have not yet determined their
stance on this question. Only 14 percent of respondents
believe that reforms will help Ukraine out of the crisis
within the next two or three years; 51 percent are
convinced that this will take 10 or more years; and 36
percent of respondents have no faith in reforms or have
not determined their stance toward them. Answering the
question whether they would be ready to accept eco-
nomic hardships during the reform, 13 percent of
respondents said that “they are ready for this, for any
duration of time; 29 percent could accept hardships,
but only for one er two years; and 58 percent said that
they are not prepared for any privations or had no
definite stance in this regard. Such results were indirectly
predetermined by the fact that 51 percent of respon-
dents, according to ““Democratic Initiatives,” had only a
vague idea about reform; 22 percent were sufficiently
well tuformed; and 27 percent of respondents were
completely ignorant of the ways of reforming the state
and its economy in general.

The following survey data are indicative of the reform
pace in Ukraine and the readiness of the population to
participate: Some 75 percent of respondents have not
used their privatization voucher yet, and 48 percent
would like to sell it.

When asked how they intended to use their voucher, 16
percent of Kiev residents answered that they would like
to invest it in any privatized enterprise; 11 percent
wished to invest it in the enterprise where they or their
relatives worked; 9 percent preferred to invest in an
investment company; 7 percent wanted to buy land; and
10 percent intended to privatize their apartment. The
remaining respondents regarded the investment of
vouchers as a senseless matter or did not know what to
do with them.

Kiev residents made contradictory judgments also
regarding the consequences of the reform. Some 36
percent of respondenis believe that unemployment
rather than a growth in inflation is preferable for them;

UKRAINE 63

18 percent gave preference to inflation on condition that
full employment is provided; and 48 percent did not
determine their stance in that regard. At the same time,
41 percent of respondents consider the threat of unem-
ployment during reform quite realistic; 23 percent do not
think so; and the rest are either already unemployed or
do not wish to work themselves, due to various reasons,
or else have not determined their stance.

Meanwhile, the results of this poll signify that the
“market potential” of Kiev residents is encouraging
enough. The majority of respondents are ready—in case
of unemployment—to look for any job and to engage in
any kind of activity that may bring profit. Some 78
percent of respondents were ready to work more inten-
sively in order to earn more, and only 11 percent of
respondents disagreed with this. Some 61 percent of
respondents, to varying degree, were ready to work for a
private employer, and only 32 percent were not prepared
to do so. Private enterprise was viewed positively by 62
percent of respondents, while 28 percent showed a
negative attitude, to varying degree. The replies of Kiev
residents on this question, however, were also contradic-
tory: Some 46 percent of respondents were ready to live
a poorer life, but with acceptable living standards guar-
anteed; and only 38 percent were prepared to take a risk
by investing money in new ventures in order to have a
chance of becoming more wealthy.

investment companies and funds, 500 trust funds, over
600 insurance companies, 250 auditing firms, and 228
commercial banks currently exist in Ukraine. During
this period, 91 commodity exchanges were established,
including 23 universal exchanges, 24 dealing in basic
commodities and raw materials, 8 in agricultural inputs
and products, 14 in commodities and stock, as well as 18
specialized exchanges. Trading is not very active so far,
and the number of deals does not exceed 2.1 percent of
the previously planned level. Real estate deals account
for 71.2 percent of the total amount. According to the
Ministry of the Economy, 113 tenders and auctions were
held at the Ukrainian Stock Exchange (USE), and 376
deals were concluded for a total sum of KBV [karbo-
vantsi] 174.8 billion. Security transactions at the USE
amounted to 25 percent of the total number of deals. The
Cabinet of Ministers is considering a draft law allowing
enterprises to establish or cofound new commodity and
stock exchanges, said Ms. Varenyk. Formerly, this right
was granted solely to banks. According to Ms. Varenyk,
80,000 small businesses currently operate in Ukraine,
including 3,000 joint ventures and 6,000 cooperatives.
The authorities plan to stimulate the development of
small business in 1995 and increase the market share of
the private sector, especially for those enterprises that
manufacture consumer goods and agricultural products.

Jan-Feb Trade Surplus With Non-CIS States

WS2804102595 Kiev INTELNEWS in English 0415
GMT 28 Apr 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Kiev, April 27—Ukraine had a
trade surplus with non-Commonwealth of Independent
States countries for the first two months of the year, but
an overall foreign trade deficit, the Statistics Ministry
reported recently.

The total volume of foreign trade (including bartered
raw materials and goods) during the period equaled
$2.592 billion. Exports were $1.120 billion, while
imports totaled $1.471 billion. The trade deficit was
$350 million.

The volume of trade with CIS countries equaled $1.482
billion, exports were $550.9 million and imports were
$931.3 million. The trade deficit was $380.4 million.

The volume of trade with the non-CIS countries equaled
$1.109 billion. Exports were $569.8 million and imports
were $540 million. The trade surplus was $29.8 million.

Barter deals remain in great demand in export opera-
tions, which encourages the outflow of currency from
Ukraine, the ministry reported.

People ‘Forced’ To Eat Contaminated Food

WS$2704 143195 Kiev DEMOKRATYCHNA
UKRAYINA in Ukrainian 15 Apr 95 p 2

[Report by Mykola Petrushenko: ““When Cheap Milk
Should Not Be a Matter of Joy. Cows Are Being Sent To
Graze. Due to the Authorities’ Indifference, They Will
Collect Delayed-Action Poison and Bring It to the
Farmers’ Table. Hostages of Chernoby!"’]

[FBIS Translated Text] Poliskyy and Ivankivskyy
Rayons in Kiev Oblast border with the 30-kilometer
zone of the Chernobyl nuclear power plant. A program
for evacuating the population from contaminated vil-
lages in Poliskyy Rayon has never been carried out.
People living in this area should eat only “clean” food.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

Such food does not exist there. Or more precisely, this
region’s population lacks money to buy it. This means,
that there is only one option for them: To eat food grown
in their own gardens and produced by themselves. This
future also waits those who live in respectively clean
villages.

To somehow reduce the concentration of radionuclides
in vegetables, scientists recommend enriching the soil
with mineral fertilizers and lime. This is not being done.
Local farms have no possibility to buy fertilizers. The
state, as everybody knows, has left everybody to cope
with their problems on their own.

In this case, the talk is not even about production at
collective farms. The talk is about garden plots providing
food for people who suffered from the Chernobyl
disaster. The owners of the gardens are not able to
purchase fertilizers, because they have been robbed, to
make it possible for the authorities and the new elite to
thrive and gorge themselves on tropical fruit.

The victims, however, have been forced to eat only soup
with radionuclides. Wise reformers, who think about
redistributing land even as they sleep, want to reap
profits from everything. They do not care about the rest,
even about the protection of our nation’s health.

As it is, it was rayons in Kiev, Zhytomyr, Rivne, and
Volyn Oblasts which suffered most from the Chernobyl
disaster. In these regions, cattle was traditionally grazed
on meadows and pastures. These pastures survived the
accident. In many places, the concentration of radionu-
clides exceeds admissible norms. Nevertheless, animals
are still being sent to graze there.

In the spring, thousands of cows will graze there. In
addition to milk, they will bring radionuclides to their
farmers’ table. Of course, it is impossible to close the
pastures. Cows are essential to the population of contam-
inated villages. So, should they put up with the evil?

People who live in the contaminated areas are already
trying to disregard the evil. They eat food grown in their
gardens. They drink milk coming from cows grazing on
contaminated pastures. They even sell this food at street

UKRAINE 65

markets. Fortunately, nobody inspects it. It is selling
more quickly, because it is cheaper than food offered by
stores. The authorities have turned a blind eye to every-
thing. They feel more comfortable this way. The author-
ities, however, are responsible for the protection of the
people’s health. Therefore, if they exerted more efforts,
there would be more order in our state.

This is only one aspect of the opportunities wasted by the
authorities. According to scientists, polluted pastures
can be decontaminated by enriching them with mineral
fertilizers and plowing repeatedly.

To do that, naturally, certain plots of land should be
singled out to make it possible for local residents to graze
cows. Who has tried to do this? The authorities have
been indifferent.

The area of private meadows and pastures is continuing
to decrease. No decontamination is being conducted
there. To keep a cow, which often feeds an entire family,
a farmer lets it graze next to a road. Thus, lead and other
hazardous elements add to the radionuclides.

Is there a way out? As long as our national wealth is
plundered to make certain individuals and groups of
people rich, citizens will not obtain any fertilizers or land
for grazing. There are thus different ways out. The
peasants can utilize swamps on which they have, and will
continue to graze their cattle. The way of those who
“graze” state policy [as published] leads to clean foreign
products purchased for money stolen from the working
people.

By the way, food from Poliskyy Rayon in Kiev Oblast or
villages adjacent to the Chernobyl plant is not sent to the
dining rooms and buffets of the presidential administra-
tion, the Supreme Court, or the Cabinet of Ministers.
Their food is delivered from clean regions at the tax-
payers’ expense. Would it not be more expedient to reduce
expenditures on these deliveries, and use the saved money
to purchase mineral fertilizers for Poliskyy Rayon?
Among others, excellent cream butter was once produced
in this rayon, which certainly did not fall behind the
famous batter from Vologda....

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

elected and a referendum initiated by the president held,
the Belarusian people would display their best quali-

Prime Minister Chyhir hopes the economic situation
would help achieve this, because the government had
managed to stabilize the financial system and the
exchange rate of the national currency and put in order
the system of energy consumption.

National Banks Losses Due to ‘Risky Credit
Policy’

WS$2704 143395 Minsk BELAPAN in English 1400
GMT 27 Apr 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Profits of Belarusian banks
over the first quarter of this year totalled 326 billion
rubles [R] at the beginning of April. According to official
statistics, seven banks in the country, including such
banks as Dukat, Belarusbank, and Vitkambank sus-
tained losses to the total amount of R26.8 billion.
Among the reasons for the losses experts name a too
risky credit policy and the sharp increase of expenditure
items in view of additional allocations to debt reserves.

Experts also talk of an unfavorable tendency when banks
pay more for the use of interbank resources than they
receive as interest for their extension. Experts’ forecast for
the state of the banking sector in the country does not give
grounds for optimism mainly because of the artificial
maintenance of the exchange rate of the Belarusian ruble.

Registration of Parliamentary Candidates Ends

LD2704181295 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1629
GMT 27 Apr 95

[From the “Presidential Bulletin” feature: compiled by
Nikolay Zherebtsov, Andrey Petrovskiy; edited by
Vladimir Shishlin}

[FBIS Transcribed Text] In the parliamentary elections in
Belarus on May 14 2,348 candidates will contest the 260
seats in the national legislature, the chairman of the central
electoral commission Aleksandr Abramovich has said. He
said that 176 citizens had been denied registration.

Abramovich said that 1,126 candidates had been nomi-
nated by 29 political parties and election alliances; 603
candidates were registered after collecting the required
500 signatures, 462 candidates were nominated by labor
staffs and the rest, by organizations of veterans and
military units.

Abramovich said that though the registration deadline
had expired, the number of candidates might change:
“Most probably it will reduce, because the leaders of a
number of parties have filed about 20 requests saying
they wish to recall their candidates for abusing party
discipline”.

Abramovich said that the problem of financing the
parliamentary elections and the national referendum
was about to be resolved. He said the Ministry of
Finance had transferred to the central electoral commis-
sion’s account 32.1 billion Belarussian rubles (about $2.8
million) to finance the elections and 4.7 billion rubles
($400,000) to hold the referendum.

Abramovich said that the central electoral commission
and the Cabinet of Ministers had come to terms on the
financial issue after parliament slashed the sum
requested for the elections from 68 billion Belarussian
rubles to 40.7 billion. The executive authorities have also
undertaken to pay all the costs of equipping the polling
stations and providing electoral commission staff with
transport and means communications.

Moldova

Snegur Rules Out Union With Romania
LD2804054295 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 0214 GMT 28 Apr 95

[P ~AR-TASS correspondent Andrey Varlamov]

WESTERN REGION 67

{FBIS Translated Text] Tokyo, 28 April—Moldova
intends to join the Council of Europe - the consultative
political organization of 23 countries of Europe - by
June, the first CIS country to do so. Mircea Snegur,
president of the Republic of Moldova, who met a
NIHON KEIZAI correspondent in Chisinau, said this in
an interview to the Japanese newspaper which was
published today.

He said that Moldova had already sent delegations to
attend Council of Europe sessions as observers on sev-
eral occasions and had successfully undergone a number
of checks by experts of this organization as to whether
Moldovan democracy conformed to common demo-
cratic principles. ““Council of Europe specialists came to
the conclusion that all the necessary conditions exist in
our country for it to join the Council of Europe’, the
newspaper quotes the president as saying.

Mircea Snegur also said that Moldova “will never unite
with Romania”. He said that although both neigh-
bouring countries share a common language and culture,
on the basis of which part of the national opposition
favors their merging, Chisinau intends to preserve its
independence, developing bilateral humanitarian and
economic relations. The president thinks that relations
between the United States and Canada should serve as
the model for relations between Moldova and Romania.

Snegur Suggests Changing Language Name to
Romanian

LD2704164495 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1527
GMT 27 Apr 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] CHISINAU, April 27 (Inter-
fax)—Moldovan President Mircea Snegur suggested
changing the name of his country’s state language, Mold-
ovan, to Romanian.

He told parliament that the country’s constitution must
be consistent with the scientific truth. Snegur said that
since the adoption of the Constitution last July
numerous scientists called for changing the name of the
language.

This change, he said, was one of the main demands made
by the Chisinau students and faculty in their month-long
strike. Snegur justified the strike as a tool to protect the
professional rights but the way it was staged it broke the
legislation and turned into a political action because
opposition leaders used the strikers’ rallies to advance
their views.

Even so, Snegur said, it is time discussions and political
speculations around the name of the language, which has
become a contentious issue, were ended. He emphasized
that the problem has become purely political and
acquired an international dimension.

Snegur said that change of the name would not imply
that the Moldovan state will cease to exist.

Parliament took note of his address.

Parliament Protests Moscow Duma Resolution on
14th Army

AU2804112195 Chisinau INFOTAG in English 2000
GMT 27 Apr 95

{FBIS Transcribed Text] Chisinau INFOTAG, 27/
4/1995—The Moldovan Parliament has regarded the
Russian State Duma’s resolution (“On Situation Around
the 14th Army”) as an unfriendly act towards Moldova.

The Moldovan parliamentarians passed a statement
today in which they pointed out that the Duma’s reso-
lution contradicts the Moldovan-Russian accords on the
[14th] Army withdrawal from Moldovan territory, and,
in fact, signifies a direct support of the self-proclaimed
Dniester Republic.

The Moldovan document indicates that the resolution
contradicts the principles of international law, UN, OSCE,
and CIS documents, as well as the guarantees of the
Russian leadership on the necessity to solve the problem of
the Moldovan eastern areas by using solely peaceful,
political means. “Such actions cannot be qualified other-
wise, but as an interference into Moldova’s internal affairs,
as an overt support of separatist regimes outside the
Russian Federation's territory,” reads the statement.

Twelve of the 28 deputies of the Socialist Unity faction
voted against passing the aforementioned statement.
They motivated their decision by saying that “the doc-
ument may damage bilateral Moldovan-Russian rela-
tions” and that “we should not hurry to pass the state-
ment on the eve of the forthcoming holidays.” [to mark
World War II victory] They reminded that the resolution
had not yet been signed by State Duma chairman Ivan
Rybkin, who will be away from Moscow until 4 May, and
insisted that by that time the resolution may suffer
principal changes.

However, the majority of Moldovan parliamentarians con-
sidered the adoption of the statement as absolutely neces-
sary. In particular, the oldest of them, 84-year-old Artem
Lazarev of the Socialist Unity, called his colleagues to vote
in favor of the document because “Russia persists in
pursuing a senior-junior policy with respect to Moldova.”

Luchinskiy Puzzled Over Russian Duma
Statement on Dniester

LD2704185795 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1629
GMT 27 Apr 95

[From the ‘Presidential Bulletin” feature: compiled by
Nikolay Zherebtsov, Andrey Petrovskiy; edited by
Vladimir Shishlin}

[FBIS Transcribed Text} Moldova's Parliament
Chairman Petr Luchinskiy has voiced dismay over the

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

decision on the 14th Army adopted by the Russian State
Duma on Wednesday [26 April]. He told an Interfax
interviewer that he was amazed by the fact that the
document speaks of the Dniester Moldova republic, in
view of the fact that the Russian leadership has repeat-
edly referred to Dniester as part and parcel of the
Republic of Moldova.

“This decision can only serve to aggravate rather than
normalize the situation in the region,” Luchcinskiy
stressed.

He announced that the Moldova parliament would soon
review the situation and would come up with an official
document to this effect.

INTERFAX IF note: The State Duma has adopted a
resolution saying that the withdrawal of the 14th Army
from Dniester would escalate tensions there. The law-
makers recommended the government to take all the
necessary measures to ensure the effective functioning of
the 14th Army. The president and government were
asked to take additional political and economic mea-
sures to normalize the situation in the Dniestern Mold-
ovan Republic. The resolution also says that the 14th
Army “performs the most important function of stabi-
lizing the situation in the region, preventing inter-ethnic
conflicts and protecting the peaceful life of Dniester’s
multi-ethnic population”.

Luchinskiy said the possibility of reforming the com-
mand of the 14th Army was an internal affair of Russia.
However, he said that Moldova was worried about the
risk of destabilization in that army and its involvement
in political conflicts. This would be very undesirable, he
said, because it may lead to the looting of military
hardware and ammunition.

Luchinskiy said the Moldovan leadership regarded Gen-
eral Aleksandr Lebed as the most suitable candidate for the
post of the 14th Army commander in the current situation.
“Whatever is said about General Lebed, he is an excellent
commander, there is order and discipline in his units and
there is a guarantee that the army's equipment and other
assets will not be plundered”, Luchinskty said.

The state secretary of the self-declared Dniestern
republic Valeriy Litskay has said in an interview that
irrespective of who commands the army, the position of
the Dniestern authorities remained unchanged. It was
formulated in the course of the referendum on the issue:
The army must stay in Dniester as the chief safeguard of
peace and security.

Defence Minister Pavel Grachev told journalists on
Wednesday that General Lebed had been offered the
post of the deputy commander of the Transbaykal Mil-
itary District for combat training. Defence Minister
Grachev said General Lebed had asked for time to
consider the proposal.

Grachev said that a decision had been made to abolish
the command of the 14th Army to replace it with a
day-to-day management group with a staff of 80-90
members.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

Defence Minister Grachev did not rule out General
Lebed might tender resignation in order to start a
political career. At the same time he said that “as the
Defence Minister I would like General Lebed to stay in
the armed forces at least because he is my trainee. I
supervised him all the way from cadet to 14th Army
commander.”

Meanwhile, General Lebed said that nobody had ever
approached him with official proposals for a new post.
Interviewed by Interfax on Thursday, he described as
“lies” all rumors to that effect, including the claim he
had been offered the post of deputy commander of the
Transbaykal Military District.

Asked who was being untrue, General Lebed was curt:
“The defence minister”.

He also said he learned about the Defence Ministry's
directive to reform the army from the press. He said that
he had received no official notifications and no steps
were being taken to effect the directive.

General Lebed refrained from comments on the State
Duma’s resolution on the 14th Army.

Lebed on Implications of Downgrading 14th Army

MK2704111095 Moscow OBSHCHAYA GAZETA in
Russian No. 17 (93), 27 Apr - 3 May 95 (Signed to press
26 Apr) p 2

{Interview with Lieutenant General Aleksandr Lebed,
14th Army commander, by Yevgeniy Aleksandrov in the
“Hello! column: “Will Lebed Make Life Easier for
Grachev?”—place, date not given; first paragraph is
introduction]

{FBIS Translated Excerpt] A report has come in from the
Russian Federation Defense Ministry about a reorganiza-
tion of the 14th Army Headquarters and the removal of its
commander. The latter for his part told representatives of
news agencies that he has been left without work and will
resign. OBSHCHAYA GAZETA’s correspondent Yev-
geniy Aleksandrov asked Lt. Gen. Aleksandr Lebed to
comment on this statement. [passage omitted]

{[Alieksandrov] Aleksandr Ivanovich, what problems will
arise as a result of reorganizing the army command?

[Lebed] The army is a living, operating organism, whose
head is being cut off in the hope that the remaining part
will continue to move through reflex. After all, nothing
has been done to implement any of the current agree-
ments on withdrawing the army from the Dniester
Region. Not a single issue has been resolved. Meanwhile
officers and services, which are to execute this decision,
are being downsized. The engineers department and the
road service has been abolished. Equipment, people, and
property need to be evacuated. Who will be doing this?
The army headquarters should be the last to leave. But
here it is the other way around. [passage omitted]

WESTERN REGION 69

[Aleksandrov] Will you obey the order unconditionally,
or do you have some other options?

[Lebed] I will resign, and let somebody else decide the
question about the army withdrawal—without me.

[Aleksandrov} How will this decision affect the situation
in the Dniester Region?

[Lebed] Very negatively. At present the situation here is
balanced. This balance will be disturbed. Equipment and
weapons will again start to be seized, unrest will resume,
and a high alert status will have to be introduced. You
must know that under a 15 February 1994 edict by the
Dniester Region president, the equipment, weapons,
ammunition, and movable and immovable property of
the army were declared the property of the people of the
Dniester Region.

[Aleksandrov] But the army is under Russia's jurisdic-
tion?

[Lebed] They could not care less about this jurisdiction.

[Aleksandrov] What if the status of troops stationed on
this territory changes?

[Lebed] Then a large, well-trained and well-managed
armed fist could emerge in the center of Europe. This is
the result of a policy pursued by the Russian Defense
Ministry and the Russian Government. [interview ends]

P.S. Yelena Yefimova, press secretary of the Dniester
Moldova Republic president, said that Igor Smirnov does
not intend to revise his position on the withdrawal of the
14th Army. The Dniester people, the president said,
declared categorically against the withdrawal of the army
in the 26 April 1995 referendum. The Dniester Region
leadership has taken a number of moves which resulted in
the discussion of this question in the Russian State Duma.
Igor Smirnov hopes that Russian politicians will take the
will of the people of the Dniester Region into account. “I
cannot venture to interfere in the actions by the Russian
Defense Ministry. I am interested in only thing: that the
army should be here,” the president said.

participation in market reforms.”

privatization ministry by the U.S. companies of the PBN
and R&R Advertising. The beginning of the new nation-
wide campaign is one of the six sections of a complex
informative and educative program in the privatization
sphere for 1995.

Kazakhstan

Preparations Complete for Vote on President’s
Powers

LD2704193495 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1817
GMT 27 Apr 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] ALMATY, April 27 (Inter-
fax}—The preparations for holding a referendum on
extending the powers of President Nursultan Naz-
arbayev have been essentially completed. The refer-
endum will take place on April 29, Chairman of the
Kazakh Central Election Commission Yuriy Kim told a
press conference in Almaty today.

He said that 10,197 constituencies had been created in
the republic where 8.897.281 million voters would cast
their ballots. The voting will be held from 7:00 am till
20:00 pm.

The referendum will be valid if more than 50 percent of
registered voters take part in it. The Kazakh president's
powers will be extended if 51 percent of those voting cast
their ballots in favor of this, Kim said.

He said he believed that the majority of Kazakh voters,
including himself, would vote in favor of extending the
president's powers. Kim said voters would have to
decide not on the future of President Nazarbayev but on
the future of Kazakhstan and the reforms.

Kim said international organizations had not yet sent
their observers for the referendum. He added that “some
Kazakh parties and public movements” willing to
observe the referendum had been allowed to do so.

LD2704145595 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1445

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, April 27 (Itar-
Tass}—Russian Foreign Ministry official Nikita Matk-
ovskiy today described as “a major phase of dialogue
between the Tajik government and the opposition” the
decision on the extension of the ceasefire agreement
until May 26 and on the next round of talks to be held in
Almaty from May 22.

The talks will center around on the basic aspects of the
constitutional system and consolidation of the state in
the Republic of Tajikistan.

The consultations which led to the adoption of these
decisions have ended in Moscow. They were organised
with assistance from Russia and held under the U.N.
aegis.

The level and duration of the consultations, the presence
of representatives of observer-countries, the scope and
significance of the subjects discussed, and the nature of
the final document place them on a par with a full-scale
round of talks.

Of special importance is that the parties to the conflict
pledged not to relocate the armed forces, including the
opposition armed groups on Afghan territory, Nikita
Matkovskiy said.

Along with this he said it was to be regretted that the
well-grounded proposal by the government delegation to
settle disputes about relocation of units on the basis of
the return to the situation which existed on both sides of

28 April 1995

the Tajik-Afghan border prior to the signing of the
ceasefire agreement was rejected by the opposition del-
egation.

beginning of the next round of talks.

The forthcoming meeting between President Emomali
Rakhmanov of Tajikistan and the leader of the Islamic
Revival Movement of Tajikistan Sayyed Abdullohi
Nouri is expected to become a landmark in the course of
dialogue between the government and the opposition.
The negotiators in Moscow confirmed their agreement to

Foreign Ministry Welcomes UN Statement on

NC2704211295 Yerevan Armenia's Radio First
Program Network in Armenian 1900 GMT 27 Apr 95

[Statement by Foreign Ministry press center; place and
date not given—read by announcer]

[FBIS Translated Text] The UN Security Council issued
a presidential statement on the Nagorno-Karabakh con-
flict on 26 April 1995. In keeping with the procedure
stipulated in Article 8 of UN Security Council Resolu-
tion 884, the Security Council heard a report from
Co-chairmen of the OSCE Minsk Conference Jan
Eliasson and Valentin Lozinskiy before adopting the
statement.

That report described the reactivated political p#cess in
the aftermath of the OSCE summit in Budapest and also
marked the end of the Swedish term as co-chairman of
the Minsk Conference. The position of co-chairmanship
has been assumed by Finland as of 21 April 1995.

The presidential statement has essentially maintained
the spirit of the documents adopted previously by the
UN Security Council on the Nagorno-Karabakh
problem. It basically aims at spurring the process of the
resolution of the Nagorno-Karabakh conflict within the
OSCE, a tenet which, as the manifestation of the contin-
uation of the [words indistinct] of the heads of state of
OSCE countries, has been enshrined in the [word indis-
tinct] documents of the Budapest summit. The Budapest
resolution had also defined the basic issues of enlisting
the UN in this problem with the aim of putting it at the
service of consolidating [word indistinct] OSCE’s medi-
atory efforts.

It was with the intention of contributing to that issue that
the UN Security Council gave its consent to the presi-
dential statement.

The document of 26 April contains an exhaustive (?effu-
sion) of those fundamental elements essential for the
progress of the Minsk process which had been enshrined
at the Budapest summit; namely, the positioning of
peacekeeping forces, [words indistinct], and the speedy
resumption of talks for a political agreement on the
termination of the military operations.

The Armenian Foreign Ministry assesses the UN Secu-
rity Council presidential statement as a manifestation of
the continued awareness and interest by that prestigious
international tribunal as regards the process for the
resolution of the Nagorno-Karabakh conflict. At the
same time, the Armenian Foreign Ministry hopes that
this document will spur the process of the resolution of
the Nagorno- Karabakh conflict within the OSCE Minsk
Conference, thus assisting the future productivity of
OSCE’s efforts.

Karabakh Official Details Azeri-Caused Damage

NC2704152895 Yerevan ARAGIL ELECTRONIC
NEWS BULLETIN in English 0840 GMT 27 Apr 95

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Stepanakert (“Hayastani Han-
rapetutyun” daily), 04/27/95—“Based on the most
modest calculations, at least $2.5bIn damage was caused
to the economy of Karabakh as a result of the Azeri
aggression”, said First Deputy Prime Minister of the
RMK [Republic of Mountainous Karabakh] and Min-
ister of Economy Spartak Tevosyan.

He said that the policy of “burnt land”, carried out by
the Azeri troops, has ruined the economy of newly
independent republic. About 8,000 industrial, agricul-
tural, construction, and other objects, 7,000 living build-
ings, 172 educational and 47 medical institutions have
suffered as a result of enemy shellings.

Mr. Tevosyan said that, according to preliminary data,
several hundreds of Armenian hostages are kept in Azeri
prisons, among them women and children.

The same sources reported that the Azeri army currently
controls about 22 percent of the Karabakh territory, and
are damaging historio-architectural monuments, created
by Armenian, Russian, and Greek people. Particularly,
the enemy destroyed Urek (Sth century) church in the
village of Talish, Surb Nshan (18th century) in the village
of Haterk, Vardany (13th century) in the village of
Vaghuhas in the Martakert district, St. Virgin church in
the village of Arakial and St. Hovhannes in the village of
Khandzadzor in the Hadrut district. Three of six
churches in the town of Shusha were destroyed. 18
ancient monuments in the Martuni district were dam-
aged as a result of shellings, among them a Kazakh
church in the Gevorgavan region. According to Spartak
Tevosyan, the damage in the field of education and
culture is evaluated at $300mIn.

rounding it. The Armenian armed units committed
massacres and evicted almost | million Azerbaijanis

Security Council resolutions have remained only on
paper. Regrettably, the OSCE—which was first chaired
by an Italian, then a Swede, and now a Hungarian
diplomat—has spent its time so far not differentiating
between the just and unjust but learning the positions of
the sides. Despite the fact that it is clear as daylight who
is the occupier and who is the innocent victim, it

Azerbaijan Excludes Iran from Oil Consortium

MM2704104095 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 26 Apr 95 First Edition p 7

{Article by Aleksandr Krasulin: “Azerbaijan: Political
Auctions and Economic Blind Alleys. Hopes of Fabulous
Profits from ‘Contract of the Century’ Not Being Ful-
filled”’}

{FBIS Translated Text] An incident unprecedented in
international practice occurred recently in relations
between two neighboring states—Azerbaijan and iran.
Baku had agreed to cede to Tehran a quarter of its
20-percent stake in the [Caspian Sea] oil consortium but
under U.S. pressure suddenly excluded Iran from the
parties to the project.

Tehran had still not completely recovered from this
shattering news when Baku played host to Turkish Prime

CAUCASUS 7

Minister Tansu Ciller and offered her the same 5 percent
that Iran was already regarding as its own according to
the statement of intention signed between the two coun-
tries.

The idea of this contract dates back to the time of
President Mutalibov, who was hoping for a single deal
which would resolve at a stroke all the economic and
political problems which had befallen Azerbaijan after
the disintegration of the USSR. But President Elchibey,
the leader of the People’s Front, gave a marked anti-
Russian slant to this idea. He adopted a course of
rapprochement with Turkey not so much because it is a
country of similar religious persuasion but because he
wanted to use it as an intermediary in seeking a rap-
prochement with the West. He was succeeded in office
by Geydar Aliyev, who pressed ahead with this scheme
entirely, revising only a few terms of the contract which,
in his opinion, harmed the country’s national interests.

The new version was signed in September last year.
According to it, Azerbaijan can expect to receive $34
billion from the sale of oil and 55 billion cubic meters of
casinghead gas, all of which will remain in the republic.
The local press carried optimistic articles which said that
in the near future Azerbaijan would be transformed into
a new Kuwait and that the Western partners would help
to settle the conflict in Karabakh. However, difficulties
soon emerged and the situation is now virtually out of
control,and not only as far as Azerbaijan is concerned.

Initially the Russian Foreign Ministry had stated that the
contract has no legal force. The main argument here is
that since the disintegration of the USSR the legal status
of the Caspian has not been defined. The Casp‘an cannot
be divided by borders because it is not a sea but a closed
inland waterway and therefore cannot be subject to the
norms of international maritime law which make it
possible to separate exclusive economic zones and the
continental shelf—such is the essential point of them.
That is also enshrined in the treaty between the USSR
and Iran of 25 March 1940.

Tehran, thus slighted, then suddenly revealed a hostility
to the “contract of the century” which it had not
expressed before. The Iranian foreign minister stated
recently that “until the status of the Caspian has been
defined, no state has the right to exploit its resources
unilaterally.”” As Radio Liberty has reported, the Iranian
Foreign Ministry has coordinated this position with the
Russian Foreign Ministry

Thus, of the five countries whose shores are washed by
the Caspian the two most powerful have opposed Baku’s
actions while a third, Kazakhstan, tacitly supported
them. In this situation it is hard to imagine how the
consortium will operate and even though Turkey is
increasingly actively entering the fray for Caucasian oil
in the hope of making up to 120 billion [currency
unspecified] in pure profit, this changes little.

The fact is that the consortium’s main problem is less to
do with organizing oil extraction than with its transport.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

78 CAUCASUS

During the recent visi. to Baku Tansu Ciller stated that
“cooperation with Azerbaijan, involving Kazakhstan
and other neighboring countries, will continue within the
framework of the construction of the export pipeline,”
giving the impression that she was unaware of the
Caspian pipeline consortium in which Russia, Kaza-
khstan, and Oman are participating. Meanwhile, this
consortium is already carrying out the field work prior to
siting the pipeline (see ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA 12
April 1995). The defeat of the Dudayev regime in
Chechnya removes the last objections to this route,
which will link the Tengiz oil field with [the oil terminal
of] Novorossiysk. But that will make it necessary to
reconsider the preliminary plans in both Baku and
Ankara.

In the West they are beginning to understand that
without Russia’s participation the Caspian oil fields
could go the way of the gold fields in the Kyrgyz
mountains and the Tengiz project in Kazakhstan. As
David Nikolov, a senior official of British Petroleum,
stated recently, his company is not reducing its staff in
Baku and work is continuing according to the original
plan but tension is already in evidence. As ITAR-TASS
reported, citing reliable sources in the City of London,
the project is still at the preliminary stage and work is
continuing “exceptionally slowly.” America’s Chevron
Corporation has decided to drastically scale down its
current expenditure on developing the Tengiz oil field
and has postponed the construction of a third oil refinery
there until 1996 at the earliest because it is not getting
the return from the project that it expected.

‘Azadlyg’ Leader Reviews Week’s Events
954F2159D Baku ZERKALO in Russian 22 Apr 95 p 2

{Commentary by Azadlyg Party Chairman Akhmed
Orudzhev: “Dead Calm in the Opposition Camp”]

[FBIS Translated Text] Notable among the events of the
past week were the conference within the presidential
apparatus dedicated to agriculture, the president's visit
to Brussels, Azerbaijan’s transfer to the American firm
Exxon of 5 percent of its share in the consortium, and
steps taken by Iran related to the status of the Caspian
and other attempts by that country to exert pressure on
Azerbaijan after being denied participation in the con-
sortium.

Although the conference within the presidential appa-
ratus was in fact devoted to agriculture, it failed to clear
up the situation facing us today, or tomorrow for that
matter, in this sector. The conference seemed to boil
down to the president's utterances on the political situ-
ation and personnel changes.

The fact that this is far from the first time the president
has mentioned bribery, corruption, and other phe-
nomena in the state apparatus in his speeches points out
the true state of affairs which has taken shape within
state structures. The president seems, in the eyes of the

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

public, to be simultaneously depriving the opposition of
its means of exerting pressure on the authority. Every
time the president delivers a speech on corruption, etc.,
a significant portion of the leading cadres in the audito-
rium (and outside it) feel uncomfortable and seem to be
biding their time. This methodology is entirely suitable
today for controlling high-ranking officials.

The inability of leading opposition forces to respond in
worthy fashion to attacks in their direction says some-
thing about their true state of affairs. Ideological efforts
of the authorities aimed at the average populace and the
fact of how often the opposition falls into a trap are an
indication that even if they desired to do so, leading
opposition forces would not be able to abandon their
““maternal” opposition functions and that they must play
this “role” over the allotted period of time.

The calm continued throughout the week in the camp of
the political opposition. There was no meeting of the
“roundtable,” which by its designation and composition
cannot espouse a fixed position and is used by leaders of
the parties to present themselves.

Generally speaking, since June of 1993, if we disregard
the demonstration of 21 May 1994, the opposition has
been unable to take any step worthy of note. In the same
way that over this entire period, parties considered the
main political forces in society have continued their
activity, issuing the regular “‘operational’’ statements.

To date, we do not sense the “social upheaval of spring”
promised by certain representatives of the political
opposition. In actuality, there exists only one factor that
might draw the “danger of social upheaval” closer. It is
the means the opposition has been employing since 1988
to exert pressure on the authorities—the unsatisfactory
overall state of the populace. While other factors—an
Organization the masses believe in, ideas that would
attract these masses and whose reality would not elicit
doubt—are absent.

As far as the main task facing society is concerned—the
task of creating effective institutions of an independent
state, this is not fully perceived by the ruling circles, and
activity in this direction is not yet discernible.

Lack of perception of the main problem and the oppo-
sition’s inability to propose a realistic alternative are
bringing about a state of mass psychosis in society and an
atmosphere of hopelessness.

The visit of republic President G. Aliyev to Brussels is a
step aimed at resolving Azerbaijan's food problem, now
that borders with Russia are closed, and there is no
guarantee of continued economic partnership with Iran,
following the events involving the consortium. Today
the influence uf the European Economic Union in
resolving the republic's food problem is great, and we do
not yet see any alternative approach.

Whereas the contract concluded with the American firm
Exxon is, on the one hand, the result of Azerbaijan's

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-082
28 April 1995

need to settle the problem of initial dues for entry into
the consortium, it indicates, on the other, that the
current authority is showing a preference for cooperation
with the West and that it will in time accept their rules of
the game.

After Iran was denied participation in the consortium,
official circles in Azerbaijan imy ‘ied to the Iranians that
they would invite them to participate in the exploitation
of other oil fields in the Caspian region. Despite this
development, Iranian authorities are insisting that the
Caspian is a “closed sea” and that exploitation of its
natural resources is the business of all the Caspian states.
They are demanding discussion of the issue of the
Caspian's status. In addition, the meeting of heads of
state of Iran, Armenia, and Turkmenistan dedicated to
economic questions and Azerbaijan’s nonparticipation
in it constitute evidence of Iran’s intention to afford
Armenia an outlet through its territory to Central Asian
markets.

Of course, the fact that Iran raised the question of the
status of the Caspian after attempts on the part of
political circles in Russia—the country with the greatest
power and prestige in the region—to discuss this ques-
tion petered out, is regrettable. But when we compare
Iran with the countries participating in the consortium.
it becomes apparent that this country will not prove an
insurmountable obstacle in the path of implementation
of this agreement.

The situation as it has taken shape today reinforces
Russia’s position on the oil pipeline question. Agree-
ments on military bases that Russia has concluded with
Armenia and Georgia enhance the possibility that the oil
pipeline will be laid across territory it controls directly or
indirectly. As far as Iran is concerned, although passage
of the oil pipeline through its territory meets the coun-
try’s long-range interests, today this is not linked with
Iranian state policy.

Nothing significant has taken place with regard to the
conflict in Nagornyy Karabakh. Armenia 1s insisting on
resolution of the issue of the status of Nagornyy Kara-
bakh prior to the dislocation of peacemaking forces
there.

There have been no significant changes in the exchange
rate of the dollar, the degree of provision of bread, or the
hopes of contributors to charitable organizations. Most
plants are still not operating.

Radar Facility To Be Leased to Russia
954F2159A Baku ZERKALO in Russian 22 Apr 95 p 1

{Unattributed report: “Azerbaijan To Afford Russia
Gabala Radar Facility on Long-Term Lease”’}

{[FBIS Translated Text] Work on the draft Azerbaijan-
Russia agreement on use of the Gabala Radar Facility is
nearing completion. The agreement wiil be a topic of
discussion during the coming visit to Baku of Russian
Deputy Minister of Foreign Affairs Albert Chernyshev.
Providirg information in this regard to a KHABAR-
SERVIS Agency correspondent, the State Commission
on preparation of the document noted that the radar
facility will be leased to Russia over an extended period
and will obtain status as the only Russian military base
in this country.

“Azerbaijan will not be able to independently use the
Gabala Radar Facility, and has no need to do so,” stated
Alik Abbasov, first deputy general director of the Azer-
bayan Aerospace Agency, to our KHABAR-SERVIS
correspondent. Mr. Abbasov said that after this facility
was placed in operation in 1984, the radiation back-
ground in Gabala and adjoining regions deteriorated by
a factor of three. “The most acceptable way for the
republic to handle this matter, therefore, is to lease the
radar facility, while establishing maximum permissible
norms with respect to influence on the surrounding area
and strictly controlling the ecological environment in the
vicinity of the facility,” A. Abbasow stated.

Georgia Refuses To Sign CIS Agreements

LD2704125695 Tbilisi Radio Thilisi Network in
Georgian 1200 GMT 27 Apr 95

[FBIS Translated Text] As is known, a meeting of the
CIS states’ foreign ministers has been held in Moscow.
One of the questions on the agenda was the discussion of
the draft agreement on the joint protection of the outer
borders of the CIS states.

According to Georgian Deputy Foreign Minister Giga
Burduli, Georgia did not sign the draft as Georgia's
territorial integrity is currently being infringed and he
stated that Georgia will sign the draft provided its
territorial integrity is restored.

The meeting also discussed human rights. Georgian
Foreign Minister Aleksandre Chikvaidze suggested
changing the word “concept” to “declaration” in the
title. The suggestion was not accepted. Therefore, the
Georgian delegation did not sign the document.

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Georgian leader Eduard She-
vardnadze issued an order about social support to vet-

groups of the population.

The signing of this document is timed to coincide with
the 50th anniversary of the Victory Day, the presidential
press service reported to Interfax Thursday.

invalids, refugees, etc., 3 million coupons each.
Employees of higher institutions are in the list of the
most needy people.

Interior Ministry Mounts Anticrime Drive in
Tbilisi

LD2804 105995 Tbilisi IPRINDA in Russian 1015 GMT
28 Apr 95

[FBIS Translated Text] Tbilisi (IPRINDA)—In the early
hours of the morning on 28 April the Georgian Interior
Ministry within the framework of its anticrime cam-
paign started to carry out a wide-scale operation in the
Georgian capital. The action is being carried out jointly
with the Guard Department, a subunit of which is

CAUCASUS 81

headed by General Jemal Chumburidze. The operation
is being supervised by Interior Minister Lt-Gen Shota
Kviraia. Colonel Valerian Gogolashvili, head of the
Interior Ministry press center, told an agency correspon-
dent that the details of the operation have been agreed
with the rerublic’s prosecutor-general, Jamlet Babilash-
vili. He has also noted that despite a certain stabilization
of the criminal situation, it still remains complex, which
necessitated carrying out of the above operation
(instances of terrorist acts, clashes and altercations
between bandit formations and the so-called “brother-
hoods” have increased. As a result of these clashes
innocent people have suffered).

Since the morning of 28 April the operation participants
(about 1,800 men) sealed off 19 approaches to Tbilisi;
they also cordoned off 10 bridges in Tbilisi, using
armored vehicles. Picket lines have been deployed at 41
points in highways. All vehicles are being checked for
weapons and narcotics. According to data at 1200, 27
people have been detained, out of which seven were
wanted criminals. One gang has been disarmed and four
crimes cleared up; four couriers, trying to smuggle drugs,
have been arrested; five fire arms and a great number of
ammunition and grenades have been withdrawn.

The operation continues.
